Unit 1 — Propositional Logic 50 solved questions
Propositions & connectives 9
DM-PL1-Q01MCQeasy

Which one of the following is a proposition — that is, a declarative statement that has exactly one definite truth value?

  1. x + 1 = 5
  2. Is it raining outside?
  3. Every even integer greater than 2 is a sum of two primes.
  4. Close the door, please.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A proposition is a declarative sentence that is either true or false, but not both. Interrogatives, imperatives and sentences with free variables are not propositions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) contains the free variable x, so its truth value changes with x; it is an open sentence (a predicate), not a proposition.
  2. (B) is a question and (D) is a command; neither has a truth value at all.
  3. (C) is a declarative sentence. Whether or not anyone can prove it, it is objectively either true or false, so it is a proposition. (It is Goldbach's conjecture, still open — but openness is about our knowledge, not about the statement's form.)

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is a predicate; it becomes a proposition only after x is given a value.
  • (B) is interrogative and (D) is imperative; truth values do not apply.

Common misconception. Students often reject (C) with “nobody knows if it is true, so it is not a proposition”. Being unknown is not the same as being truth-valueless: a proposition only needs a definite truth value, not a known one.

Faster method. Ask two questions: is it declarative, and does it contain a free variable? If yes-then-no, it is a proposition.

Takeaway. “Open sentence ≠ proposition” and “unknown ≠ not a proposition” are the two traps in this standard GATE opener.

DM-PL1-Q11MCQmedium

Which one of the following sets of connectives is functionally complete — that is, able to express every Boolean function?

  1. {∧, ∨}
  2. {¬, →}
  3. {∨, →}
  4. {¬, ↔}
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A set of connectives is functionally complete if every truth function can be written using only those connectives. {¬, ∧}, {¬, ∨}, {¬, →}, {↑} and {↓} are complete.

Step by step.

  1. (B) {¬, →}: ¬ is available directly, and p ∨ q ≡ ¬p → q, so ∨ is definable; with {¬, ∨} we already have a complete set. Hence {¬, →} is complete.
  2. (A) {∧, ∨}: both are monotone — replacing a false input by true never makes the output false — so no formula built from them alone can express ¬p.
  3. (C) {∨, →}: exhaustively, only 6 of the 16 binary functions are expressible; for instance no formula in one variable built from ∨ and → can produce ¬p or the constant F.
  4. (D) {¬, ↔}: ↔ and ¬ preserve the property “f(x,x) = f(y,y) for all x, y”, so only 8 of the 16 functions are reachable; ∧ is not one of them.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) cannot express negation (monotonicity).
  • (C) reaches only 6 of the 16 binary truth functions.
  • (D) reaches only 8 of the 16.

Common misconception. Assuming any “reasonably rich” pair works. Completeness is a strong condition; {∧, ∨} fails because negation is unavailable, which is easy to forget.

Faster method. If the set contains ¬ plus anything that can build ∨ (or ∧), it is complete. Without ¬ you need a single connective such as ↑ or ↓.

Takeaway. NAND and NOR are each complete on their own; {∧, ∨} and {¬, ↔} are the classic incomplete traps.

DM-PL1-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following are propositions? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 2 + 2 = 5
  2. x > 3
  3. New Delhi is the capital of India.
  4. Please submit your answer sheet before 5 p.m.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A proposition is a declarative sentence with exactly one truth value. It may be false — falsity does not disqualify it.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is declarative and false; false propositions are still propositions.
  2. (B) has a free variable, so its truth value depends on x; it is a predicate, not a proposition.
  3. (C) is declarative and true.
  4. (D) is a request; it has no truth value.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is an open sentence.
  • (D) is an imperative sentence.

Common misconception. Excluding (A) because it is false. A proposition needs a definite truth value, not the value “true”.

Faster method. Declarative + no free variable = proposition, regardless of truth value.

Takeaway. “2 + 2 = 5” is a proposition (a false one); “x > 3” is not.

DM-PL1-Q21NATeasy

For how many of the four truth assignments to (p, q) is the implication p → q TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. p → q is false only when p is true and q is false; the other three rows make it true.

Step by step.

  1. (T,T): T → T = T.
  2. (T,F): T → F = F — the only false row.
  3. (F,T): F → T = T.
  4. (F,F): F → F = T.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting (F,F) as false. A false hypothesis makes the implication vacuously true, which is exactly why 3 and not 2 is the answer.

Faster method. False rows of an implication = 1 for each pair of variables; so true rows = 2ⁿ − 2ⁿ⁻¹ = 2 for n = 2 ... here simply 4 − 1 = 3.

Takeaway. p → q is true on 3 of the 4 rows and false on exactly one.

DM-PL2-Q01MCQeasy

Which one of the following sentences is not a proposition?

  1. 7 is an odd integer.
  2. Every integer greater than 1 has a prime divisor.
  3. Let n be an even integer.
  4. No real number has square −1.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A proposition is a declarative sentence that has exactly one definite truth value. A sentence that merely introduces or stipulates a variable — “let n be …” — makes no claim, so it has no truth value.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is declarative and true; its truth value is definite.
  2. (B) is declarative and true (every integer > 1 has a prime factor); it is a proposition.
  3. (D) is declarative and true, since x² ≥ 0 for every real x.
  4. (C) is an instruction that fixes the value of n; nothing is asserted, so nothing is true or false. It is not a proposition.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (D) are all declarative sentences with definite truth values, so each is a proposition regardless of whether it is true.
  • Confusing “introduces a variable” with “is false”: a proposition may be false and still be a proposition.

Common misconception. Students accept (C) because it “looks mathematical”. The test is whether the sentence asserts something that is either true or false — “let …” asserts nothing.

Faster method. Ask one question: does the sentence make a claim? An instruction or a definition makes none.

Takeaway. Declarative claim ⇒ proposition. Questions, commands, requests and “let …” stipulations are not propositions, however mathematical they look.

DM-PL2-Q11MCQmedium

How many distinct binary (two-argument) truth functions are there?

  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 16
  4. 256
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A binary truth function is fixed by its output on each of the 2² = 4 input pairs, and each output can be chosen independently, giving 2^4 functions.

Step by step.

  1. The inputs are the four pairs (F,F), (F,T), (T,F), (T,T).
  2. An output value (0 or 1) is chosen for each pair: 2⁴ = 16 possible output columns.
  3. Each column is a distinct truth function — for example ∧, ∨, →, ⊕, ↔ and the two projections are among the 16.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • 4 counts the input pairs, not the functions.
  • 8 is 2³, the number of functions of one variable, or the number of commutative binary functions.
  • 256 is 2⁸, the number of truth functions of three variables.

Common misconception. Using the number of inputs as the base instead of the number of outputs. The count is 2^(2ⁿ) for n arguments.

Faster method. Functions of n variables: 2^(2ⁿ). Here 2⁴ = 16.

Takeaway. 1 variable → 4 functions, 2 variables → 16, 3 variables → 256. The double exponent is the point.

DM-PL2-Q12MCQhard

Of the 16 binary truth functions, how many are associative — that is, satisfy f(f(a, b), c) = f(a, f(b, c)) for all truth values a, b, c?

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 8
  4. 10
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Associativity lets you drop the brackets. For binary connectives it is a property of the truth function, checked on the eight triples (a, b, c).

Step by step.

  1. The connectives ∧, ∨, ⊕ and ↔ are all associative — each is a Boolean operation (min, max, addition mod 2, equality) that is associative on {0,1}.
  2. The two projections f(a, b) = a and f(a, b) = b are associative: both sides reduce to the same one of a, b, c.
  3. The two constant functions (always T, always F) are associative, since both sides are the same constant.
  4. The remaining eight fail. Implication is the standard counterexample: at a = F, b = F, c = F we get (F → F) → F = T → F = F while F → (F → F) = F → T = T, so → is not associative; NAND, NOR and the four mixed functions fail similarly.
  5. Count: 4 + 2 + 2 = 8.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • 4 counts only the familiar AND, OR, XOR, IFF and forgets the projections and constants, which are also associative.
  • 6 or 10 come from testing associativity on a subset of the eight triples, typically only those with a = b or b = c.

Common misconception. Assuming every “reasonable-looking” connective is associative. Implication and NAND are the standard counterexamples, and the constant functions are the ones students never check.

Faster method. Group by structure: the 4 lattice/Boolean operations, the 2 projections, the 2 constants = 8. Every other binary function fails on some triple.

Takeaway. Associativity is a property of the function's table, not of the symbol. Constants and projections are associative too — do not forget them.

DM-PL2-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about functionally complete sets of connectives are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. NAND alone is functionally complete.
  2. {¬, ∨} is functionally complete.
  3. {∧, ↔} is functionally complete.
  4. {→} alone is functionally complete.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A set of connectives is functionally complete if every truth function can be written using only those connectives. NAND and NOR are each complete on their own; so is any set from which negation plus one of ∧, ∨ can be built.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: ¬p ≡ p ↑ p and p ∧ q ≡ ¬(p ↑ q), so NAND builds everything.
  2. (B) True: with ¬ and ∨ we get p ∧ q ≡ ¬(¬p ∨ ¬q), and {¬, ∧, ∨} is complete.
  3. (C) False: using only ∧ and ↔ from variables p, q one can build just 8 of the 16 binary functions, and in particular no negation of a single variable; the set cannot be complete.
  4. (D) False: → is 1-preserving (a true consequent makes it true), so no formula built from → alone can express ¬p or the constant F.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the standard “two connectives must be enough” trap; the pair generates only half the functions.
  • (D) confuses “can express ∧ and ∨” with “complete”. Without negation, completeness is impossible for → alone.

Common misconception. Assuming any pair of connectives is complete. Completeness needs a way to produce negation or a known complete single connective.

Faster method. If the set contains ¬ plus ∧ or ∨, it is complete. If it contains NAND or NOR alone, it is complete. Otherwise suspect incompleteness.

Takeaway. The complete single connectives are NAND and NOR. Among pairs, {¬, ∧}, {¬, ∨}, {¬, →} work; {∧, ∨}, {∧, ↔}, {∨, →} do not.

DM-PL2-Q23NATeasy

How many distinct truth functions of three propositional variables are there?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A truth function of n variables is determined by its output column, which has 2ⁿ entries; each entry can be 0 or 1 independently, giving 2^(2ⁿ) functions.

Step by step.

  1. Three variables give 2³ = 8 rows.
  2. Each of the 8 outputs is chosen freely as T or F: 2⁸ = 256 output columns.
  3. So there are 256 distinct truth functions of three variables.

Answer: 256.

Common misconception. Answering 2³ = 8 (the number of rows) or 2 × 3. The double exponent is what matters.

Faster method. Memorise the ladder: 1 variable → 4, 2 → 16, 3 → 256, 4 → 65536.

Takeaway. Truth functions of n variables: 2^(2ⁿ). The count grows doubly exponentially, which is why functional completeness matters.

Truth tables 9
DM-PL1-Q02MCQeasy

How many rows does a complete truth table for the compound proposition (p ∧ q) → (r ∨ ¬s) contain?

  1. 8
  2. 16
  3. 24
  4. 32
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A truth table for a formula with n distinct propositional variables has 2ⁿ rows, one per assignment of truth values to those variables.

Step by step.

  1. List the distinct variables: p, q, r, s — four of them.
  2. So the number of rows is 2⁴ = 16.
  3. The number of connectives (three here) is irrelevant; only the variable count matters.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 8 would be right for three variables; here there are four.
  • (C) and (D) do not correspond to any power of two.

Common misconception. Counting occurrences instead of distinct variables — ¬s and r∨¬s both use s, but s still contributes one column, not two.

Faster method. Count distinct variable names, then take 2 to that power.

Takeaway. Rows = 2^(distinct variables); columns = distinct variables + one per subformula.

DM-PL1-Q07MCQmedium

For how many of the eight truth assignments to (p, q, r) is the proposition (p ∨ q) → r false?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An implication is false in exactly one situation: antecedent true and consequent false.

Step by step.

  1. (p ∨ q) → r is false iff (p ∨ q) is true and r is false.
  2. (p ∨ q) is true for three of the four assignments to (p, q): (T,T), (T,F), (F,T).
  3. Fix r = F for each of those three: (T,T,F), (T,F,T), (F,T,F) — three falsifying assignments.
  4. Equivalently, the formula is true on 8 − 3 = 5 of the eight rows.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 1 would be right only if the antecedent were a single variable.
  • (B) and (D) come from counting rows of (p, q) only, or from forgetting that r is free.

Common misconception. Counting the rows where r is false (four) instead of intersecting with the rows where the antecedent holds (three).

Faster method. False rows = (rows making the antecedent true) × (rows making the consequent false) = 3 × 1 = 3.

Takeaway. For a count of falsifying rows, count models of the antecedent and multiply by models of ¬consequent, then subtract any overlap.

DM-PL1-Q13MCQhard

Which one of the following formulas is TRUE exactly when an odd number of p, q, r are TRUE?

  1. (p ⊕ q) ⊕ r
  2. (p ⊕ q) ∧ r
  3. (p ∨ q ∨ r) ∧ ¬(p ∧ q ∧ r)
  4. p ⊕ q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. XOR is addition modulo 2. Iterating it over three variables gives the parity function: true exactly when the number of TRUE inputs is odd.

Step by step.

  1. Check the four odd rows. One TRUE: (T,F,F) → T⊕F = T, T⊕F = T. Symmetric for the other two single-TRUE rows.
  2. Three TRUE: (T,T,T) → T⊕T = F, F⊕T = T. True, as parity requires.
  3. Check the even rows. Two TRUE: (T,T,F) → F⊕F = F. Zero TRUE: (F,F,F) → F⊕F = F. Correct.
  4. (B) is true only on rows with exactly two TRUE values and r = T, i.e. only (T,T,T) — wrong. (C) is true for one or two TRUE values, so it wrongly accepts the two-TRUE rows. (D) ignores r entirely.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) collapses to (p ⊕ q) ∧ r, true on a single row.
  • (C) is the “at least one but not all” formula, which is not parity.
  • (D) is independent of r, so it cannot distinguish rows that differ only in r.

Common misconception. Assuming “odd number of TRUE” means “exactly one TRUE”. Parity also includes three TRUE, which is where (C) and (B) fail.

Faster method. Parity over n variables is the n-fold XOR. Write p ⊕ q ⊕ r and verify the two extreme rows (all false, all true).

Takeaway. This is a variation on the GATE CSE 2014 Set 1 Q53 pattern (formula true exactly when a given number of p, q, r are TRUE); the original asked for “exactly two”, this asks for odd parity. It is practice material, not the official question.

DM-PL1-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following are tautologies? (Select all that apply.)

  1. p → (q → p)
  2. (p → q) → (q → p)
  3. (p ∧ (p → q)) → q
  4. (p ∨ q) → p
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A tautology is true under every assignment. (A) and (C) are the two standard “absorption” tautologies; (B) and (D) are not.

Step by step.

  1. (A) p → (q → p) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q ∨ p ≡ T. Tautology.
  2. (B) p = F, q = T: (F → T) → (T → F) = T → F = F. Not a tautology.
  3. (C) p ∧ (p → q) → q is modus ponens as a formula: ≡ ¬(p ∧ (¬p ∨ q)) ∨ q ≡ (¬p ∨ (p ∧ ¬q)) ∨ q ≡ T. Tautology.
  4. (D) p = F, q = T: (F ∨ T) → F = F. Not a tautology.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) fails at p = F, q = T — it is the converse-flavoured formula.
  • (D) fails at p = F, q = T.

Common misconception. Assuming every formula with the shape “implication between implications” is a tautology. Test p = F, q = T, the standard killer row.

Faster method. Convert to ¬/∨ form; a tautology becomes an expression that reduces to T for every assignment.

Takeaway. Modus ponens and “p implies (q implies p)” are tautologies; anything that reverses an arrow usually is not.

DM-PL1-Q22NATeasy

For how many of the eight truth assignments to (p, q, r) is the proposition p → (q ∧ r) TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count the falsifying rows and subtract from 8: the implication fails only when p is true and q ∧ r is false.

Step by step.

  1. p = F: the implication is vacuously true for all four assignments of (q, r) — 4 rows.
  2. p = T: the implication is true only when q ∧ r is true, i.e. only (q, r) = (T, T) — 1 row.
  3. Total true rows = 4 + 1 = 5; the three false rows are (T,T,F), (T,F,T), (T,F,F).

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Forgetting the four vacuous rows with p = F and answering 1 or 3.

Faster method. True rows = 2ⁿ⁻¹ (hypothesis false) + (rows with hypothesis true and conclusion true) = 4 + 1.

Takeaway. Split on the hypothesis first; the false-hypothesis half is always free.

DM-PL2-Q02MCQeasy

How many rows does a complete truth table for the proposition (p ∧ ¬q) ∨ (r ⊕ s) ∨ ¬t contain?

  1. 16
  2. 24
  3. 32
  4. 64
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A complete truth table has one row per assignment of truth values to the distinct propositional variables, so 2ⁿ rows for n distinct variables.

Step by step.

  1. List the distinct variables: p, q, r, s, t — five of them.
  2. The number of assignments is 2⁵ = 32.
  3. The connectives (¬, ∧, ∨, ⊕) and how often a variable repeats do not change the row count.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • 16 = 2⁴, the count for four variables — the trap if t is overlooked.
  • 24 and 64 are not powers of two at all.

Common misconception. Counting occurrences of variables instead of distinct variables. p appears once here, but ¬t and t would still be one variable.

Faster method. Count distinct letters, then take 2 to that power.

Takeaway. Rows = 2^(distinct variables). Read the formula once, listing letters, before computing anything.

DM-PL2-Q07MCQmedium

If the implication p → q is FALSE, which one of the following must be TRUE?

  1. p is false.
  2. q is true.
  3. p is true and q is false.
  4. p and q have the same truth value.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An implication is false in exactly one of its four rows: the antecedent true and the consequent false. Every other combination makes it true.

Step by step.

  1. Write p → q as ¬p ∨ q.
  2. For this to be false, both disjuncts must be false: p is true and q is false.
  3. So a false implication forces p = T and q = F — and nothing else is forced.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) would make the implication vacuously true, not false.
  • (B) also makes it true, whichever value p has.
  • (D) covers (T,T) and (F,F), both of which make the implication true.

Common misconception. Believing that a false implication means both parts are false. Only the antecedent-true/consequent-false row fails.

Faster method. Memorise the single falsifying pattern of →: (T, F).

Takeaway. One row falsifies an implication. Everything about “if … then …” in GATE turns on that row.

DM-PL2-Q17MSQmedium

Among the eight truth assignments to (p, q, r), which of the following formulas are TRUE on exactly two of them? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (p ↔ q) ∧ r
  2. (p ⊕ q) ∧ ¬r
  3. p ∧ ¬q ∧ r
  4. p ∨ q ∨ r
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count the models by splitting on one variable: fix the rows that satisfy the “outer” condition, then multiply by the number of choices for the remaining variable.

Step by step.

  1. (A) needs p ↔ q (true on (T,T) and (F,F)) and r = T: 2 × 1 = 2 rows.
  2. (B) needs p ⊕ q (true on (T,F) and (F,T)) and r = F: 2 × 1 = 2 rows.
  3. (C) needs p = T, q = F, r = T — a single row.
  4. (D) fails only when all three are false, so it is true on 8 − 1 = 7 rows.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the near-miss: three conjuncts pinned to fixed values give one row, not two.
  • (D) counts the complement of a single row.

Common misconception. Counting the rows of p ↔ q and forgetting that r must also be fixed, which halves nothing here but doubles the answer if r is left free.

Faster method. Two-variable condition with 2 models × 1 fixed value of the third variable = 2. Read the count off the structure.

Takeaway. Models of a conjunction = models of the two-variable part × models of the fixed literal.

DM-PL2-Q21NATeasy

For how many of the four truth assignments to (p, q) is the exclusive-or p ⊕ q TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Exclusive-or is true exactly when its two arguments differ, so it has one true row for each ordered pair of distinct values.

Step by step.

  1. Row (T, T): equal, so p ⊕ q is false.
  2. Row (T, F): different, so true.
  3. Row (F, T): different, so true.
  4. Row (F, F): equal, so false. Count = 2.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Confusing ⊕ with inclusive ∨: ∨ is true on three of the four rows, ⊕ on two.

Faster method. Over n variables, XOR is true on exactly half the rows: 2^(n−1). Here 2¹ = 2.

Takeaway. p ⊕ q ≡ (p ∨ q) ∧ ¬(p ∧ q); it is the “exactly one” connective.

Logical equivalences 7
DM-PL1-Q03MCQeasy

Which proposition is logically equivalent to ¬(p ∨ q)?

  1. ¬p ∨ ¬q
  2. ¬p ∧ ¬q
  3. p ∧ q
  4. ¬p → q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. De Morgan's laws: ¬(p ∨ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q and ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q. Negation flips the connective.

Step by step.

  1. Write the truth table of ¬(p ∨ q): true only when p ∨ q is false, i.e. only when p = F and q = F.
  2. ¬p ∧ ¬q is true exactly when both p and q are false — the same single row.
  3. So the two formulas agree on all four assignments and are equivalent.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the negation of p ∧ q, not of p ∨ q.
  • (C) is true on exactly one row (p = q = T), the opposite corner.
  • (D) ¬p → q ≡ p ∨ q, which is the formula being negated.

Common misconception. Pushing the ¬ inside while keeping the same connective — the commonest single error in this unit.

Faster method. Negation of a disjunction is a conjunction of negations; of a conjunction, a disjunction of negations.

Takeaway. De Morgan is applied to the connective that the ¬ meets first; nothing else changes.

DM-PL1-Q08MCQmedium

Which one of the following is NOT logically equivalent to p → q?

  1. ¬q → ¬p
  2. ¬p ∨ q
  3. ¬(p ∧ ¬q)
  4. q → p
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q ≡ ¬q → ¬p ≡ ¬(p ∧ ¬q). The converse q → p is not among the equivalents.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the contrapositive — equivalent.
  2. (B) is material implication — equivalent.
  3. (C) is the negation of “p true and q false”, the only falsifying pattern — equivalent.
  4. (D) reverses the arrow. With p = F, q = T: p → q is true but q → p is false, so the two differ.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) are the three standard rewrites of an implication and are all equivalent to it.

Common misconception. Believing an implication and its converse say the same thing; “if it rains the ground is wet” does not license “if the ground is wet it rained”.

Faster method. Test the single row p = F, q = T: any candidate that is false there is not equivalent to p → q.

Takeaway. Memorise the three equivalent rewrites; the converse and the inverse are never among them.

DM-PL1-Q12MCQhard

Which one of the following pairs of formulas is NOT logically equivalent?

  1. p → (q → r) and (p ∧ q) → r
  2. (p → q) → r and p → (q → r)
  3. ¬(p ↔ q) and p ⊕ q
  4. p → q and ¬p ∨ q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Implication is right-associative and curries: p → (q → r) ≡ (p ∧ q) → r. Grouping the other way, (p → q) → r, is a different function.

Step by step.

  1. (A) p → (q → r) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q ∨ r ≡ ¬(p ∧ q) ∨ r ≡ (p ∧ q) → r. Equivalent.
  2. (C) ¬(p ↔ q) is true exactly when p and q differ, which is the definition of p ⊕ q. Equivalent.
  3. (D) is material implication. Equivalent.
  4. (B) Test p = F, q = F, r = F: (p → q) = T, so (p → q) → r = T → F = F; but p → (q → r) = F → T = T. Different truth values, so not equivalent.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (C) and (D) are genuine equivalences, each provable by the rewrites ¬x ∨ y and ¬(p ↔ q) ≡ p ⊕ q.

Common misconception. Treating → as associative. It is not: only the right-nested form curries into a conjunction of hypotheses.

Faster method. Push all implications into ¬/∨ form and compare the resulting formulas as Boolean expressions.

Takeaway. (p ∧ q) → r ≡ p → (q → r) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q ∨ r. The left-nested (p → q) → r is not in this family.

DM-PL1-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following pairs are logically equivalent? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q
  2. p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p
  3. p ↔ q ≡ (p → q) ∧ (q → p)
  4. p ∨ (q ∧ r) ≡ (p ∨ q) ∧ (p ∨ r)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The equivalences to check are De Morgan (correctly and incorrectly stated), contraposition, the definition of the biconditional, and distributivity of ∨ over ∧.

Step by step.

  1. (A) De Morgan gives ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q. The option states ∧, so it is wrong: with p = F, q = T the left side is T and the right side is F.
  2. (B) Contraposition — equivalent.
  3. (C) Definition of ↔ — equivalent.
  4. (D) Distributivity of ∨ over ∧ — equivalent (check p = T: both sides are T; check p = F: both reduce to q ∧ r).

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) flips the connective the wrong way; ¬p ∨ ¬q is the correct De Morgan partner.

Common misconception. (A) is deliberately the “common wrong De Morgan”. The negation of a conjunction is a disjunction of negations.

Faster method. Two formulas are equivalent iff they have the same truth table — four rows settle any two-variable candidate instantly.

Takeaway. De Morgan, contraposition, the ↔ definition and distributivity are the four workhorses of this unit.

DM-PL2-Q03MCQeasy

Which proposition is logically equivalent to ¬(p → q)?

  1. p ∧ ¬q
  2. ¬p ∧ q
  3. p ∨ ¬q
  4. ¬p ∨ q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An implication p → q is false in exactly one case — p true and q false — so its negation must be true in exactly that case.

Step by step.

  1. Rewrite the implication: p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q.
  2. Negate and apply De Morgan: ¬(¬p ∨ q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q.
  3. Check the single row p = T, q = F: p → q is false there, and p ∧ ¬q is true there. All other rows make both false. The two formulas agree everywhere.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is true only when p is false and q is true — that row already makes p → q true.
  • (C) is true on three rows, so it cannot be the negation of a formula that is false on three rows.
  • (D) is p → q itself, written in material-implication form.

Common misconception. Applying De Morgan without first removing the arrow. Negating “if p then q” is not “if p then not q”.

Faster method. The negation of an implication is a conjunction: p ∧ ¬q. Remember the single falsifying row.

Takeaway. ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q. This one-line identity resolves most “negate the implication” items.

DM-PL2-Q08MCQmedium

The formula ¬(¬p ∧ q) ∨ p simplifies to

  1. p ∨ ¬q
  2. p ∧ ¬q
  3. ¬p ∨ q
  4. p ∨ q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. De Morgan turns a negated conjunction into a disjunction of negations; then absorption removes any term that is already present.

Step by step.

  1. Apply De Morgan: ¬(¬p ∧ q) ≡ p ∨ ¬q.
  2. Substitute: (p ∨ ¬q) ∨ p.
  3. ∨ is idempotent and commutative, so the second p adds nothing: the result is p ∨ ¬q.
  4. Cross-check with the falsifying row: p ∨ ¬q is false only at p = F, q = T, and the original is false only there too.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is false on three rows, whereas the original is false on one.
  • (C) is the negation of the answer, and is false on a different row.
  • (D) is true on three rows; the original is true on three rows as well but on a different set, so they differ at (F,T).

Common misconception. Applying De Morgan to the wrong connective, or dropping the outer ∨ p. Keep the whole formula until the brackets are removed.

Faster method. De Morgan first, then delete duplicate literals. Two rewrites finish this item.

Takeaway. De Morgan + idempotence + absorption handle almost every “simplify” question in this unit.

DM-PL2-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following are logically equivalent to p → (q ∧ r)? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (p → q) ∧ (p → r)
  2. ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r)
  3. (p ∧ q) → r
  4. ¬(p ∧ ¬(q ∧ r))
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three tools settle this: material implication p → X ≡ ¬p ∨ X, distributivity of → over a conjunction in the consequent, and the negation identity p → X ≡ ¬(p ∧ ¬X).

Step by step.

  1. (A) (p → q) ∧ (p → r) ≡ (¬p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ r) ≡ ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r) — equivalent.
  2. (B) is material implication applied to the whole implication — equivalent by definition.
  3. (D) ¬(p ∧ ¬(q ∧ r)) ≡ ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r) by De Morgan — equivalent.
  4. (C) (p ∧ q) → r ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q ∨ r, which is weaker: it is satisfied by p = F, q = T, r = F while p → (q ∧ r) is false there.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the classic mix-up between p → (q ∧ r) and (p ∧ q) → r; the latter only forbids p, q true with r false, and says nothing about q when p is false.

Common misconception. Treating “and” in the consequent as if it were “and” in the hypothesis. Moving a conjunct across the arrow changes the formula.

Faster method. Push every arrow out with p → X ≡ ¬p ∨ X and compare the resulting OR-of-AND forms.

Takeaway. p → (q ∧ r) ≡ (p → q) ∧ (p → r), but p → (q ∧ r) is NOT (p ∧ q) → r.

Implications & inference 9
DM-PL1-Q04MCQeasy

The contrapositive of “If it rains, then the ground is wet” is

  1. If the ground is wet, then it rains.
  2. If it does not rain, then the ground is not wet.
  3. If the ground is not wet, then it did not rain.
  4. It rains if and only if the ground is wet.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The contrapositive of p → q is ¬q → ¬p, and it is logically equivalent to the original implication.

Step by step.

  1. p: it rains. q: the ground is wet.
  2. Negate the conclusion: ¬q = “the ground is not wet”.
  3. Negate the hypothesis: ¬p = “it did not rain”.
  4. Contrapositive: if the ground is not wet, then it did not rain.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the converse (q → p).
  • (B) is the inverse (¬p → ¬q).
  • (D) is a biconditional, strictly stronger than the original.

Common misconception. Swapping the two clauses without negating them (the converse), or negating without swapping (the inverse). Only swapping and negating preserves the statement.

Faster method. Swap the two halves and put a “not” on both — done.

Takeaway. Contrapositive ≡ original; converse and inverse are equivalent to each other but not to the original.

DM-PL1-Q09MCQmedium

Which one of the following argument forms is invalid?

  1. From p → q and p, infer q.
  2. From p → q and ¬q, infer ¬p.
  3. From p ∨ q and ¬p, infer q.
  4. From p → q and q, infer p.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Valid propositional argument forms include modus ponens, modus tollens, disjunctive syllogism and hypothetical syllogism. Affirming the consequent is a fallacy.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is modus ponens — valid.
  2. (B) is modus tollens — valid.
  3. (C) is disjunctive syllogism — valid.
  4. (D) is affirming the consequent: take p = F, q = T. Both premises (p → q) and q are true, but the conclusion p is false, so the form is invalid.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) are all standard valid rules of inference.

Common misconception. Treating “q is true” as evidence for p. The implication only runs from p to q; a true conclusion can come from many causes.

Faster method. For any candidate rule, look for the one assignment that makes all premises true and the conclusion false.

Takeaway. Denying the antecedent and affirming the consequent are the two named fallacies; both are distractors in GATE inference questions.

DM-PL1-Q15MCQhard

Consider the three premises: (i) p → (q ∨ r), (ii) ¬q, (iii) ¬r. Which one of the following conclusions follows logically from them?

  1. p
  2. ¬p
  3. q ∧ r
  4. p ∨ q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A conclusion follows logically iff every assignment satisfying all the premises also satisfies the conclusion.

Step by step.

  1. From (ii) and (iii), q = F and r = F, so q ∨ r = F.
  2. Premise (i) becomes p → F, which is true only when p = F.
  3. So the only model of the three premises is p = F, q = F, r = F.
  4. In that model ¬p is true and p, q ∧ r, p ∨ q are all false. Hence only ¬p follows.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) p is false in the only model.
  • (C) q ∧ r is false because both q and r are false.
  • (D) p ∨ q is false in the model as well.

Common misconception. Applying modus tollens to the wrong part: from p → (q ∨ r) and ¬q alone you cannot conclude ¬p, because r might still be true. You need ¬r as well, so that the consequent fails as a whole.

Faster method. Substitute the units first: two negated literals turn the disjunction into F, and p → F gives ¬p immediately.

Takeaway. To use modus tollens on a disjunctive consequent, you must refute every disjunct — refuting one is not enough.

DM-PL1-Q25NATmedium

Consider the premises p → q, q → r and r → s. For how many of the sixteen truth assignments to (p, q, r, s) are all three premises simultaneously TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. All three implications hold exactly when the truth values never decrease along p → q → r → s, i.e. p ≤ q ≤ r ≤ s.

Step by step.

  1. The satisfying assignments are the non-decreasing 4-tuples over {F, T}:
  2. (F,F,F,F), (F,F,F,T), (F,F,T,T), (F,T,T,T), (T,T,T,T).
  3. Any tuple with a T before an F (such as (T,F,F,T)) falsifies one of the implications, e.g. p → q here.
  4. Count: 5.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Multiplying 3 premises by 2 assignments, or assuming 2³ = 8 rows satisfy a chain of three implications.

Faster method. Count non-decreasing sequences of length 4 over 2 values: C(4+2−1, 4) = C(5,4) = 5.

Takeaway. A chain of implications is a monotonicity constraint; counting its models is a stars-and-bars count.

DM-PL2-Q04MCQeasy

Let P be “you pass the exam” and S be “you submit the assignment”. The sentence “You will pass only if you submit the assignment” is written formally as

  1. S → P
  2. P → S
  3. P ↔ S
  4. ¬S → P
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. “A only if B” means A cannot happen without B, i.e. A → B. The phrase “only if” marks the necessary condition, which is the consequent.

Step by step.

  1. “You pass only if you submit” says: passing guarantees submitting.
  2. That is the implication from passing to submitting: P → S.
  3. Equivalently ¬S → ¬P: no submission, no pass — the contrapositive of the same statement.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) says submitting is enough to pass, which the sentence does not claim.
  • (C) claims both directions, which is stronger than the sentence.
  • (D) says that not submitting leads to passing — the opposite of what is meant.

Common misconception. “Only if” is read as “if”. “A if B” is B → A, but “A only if B” is A → B; the two differ by the direction of the arrow.

Faster method. Replace “only if” by “implies”: A only if B ⇔ A → B.

Takeaway. if = sufficient condition on the right of the arrow; only if = necessary condition on the right of the arrow as well, but with the arrow starting at A.

DM-PL2-Q09MCQmedium

Consider the premises p → q, q → r and ¬r. Which one of the following conclusions follows logically from them?

  1. p
  2. q
  3. ¬p
  4. r
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Modus tollens: from X → Y and ¬Y infer ¬X. Applied twice to a chain it walks backwards.

Step by step.

  1. From q → r and ¬r, modus tollens gives ¬q.
  2. From p → q and ¬q, modus tollens gives ¬p.
  3. So the premises force p = F, q = F, r = F; the only conclusion in the list that holds in that model is ¬p.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) are both false in the unique model of the premises.
  • (D) contradicts the premise ¬r outright.

Common misconception. Stopping after one step and reporting ¬q — it does follow, but it is not offered; the item rewards finishing the chain. Also note that denying the antecedent would be invalid here.

Faster method. Push the negation backwards through the chain: ¬r ⇒ ¬q ⇒ ¬p.

Takeaway. A chain of implications plus the negation of the last consequent yields the negation of the first antecedent.

DM-PL2-Q15MCQhard

Consider the three premises (i) p ∨ q, (ii) ¬p ∨ r, (iii) ¬q ∨ r. Which one of the following follows logically from them?

  1. r
  2. p
  3. q
  4. ¬r
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A conclusion follows when every assignment satisfying all the premises also satisfies it. Resolution — combining two clauses that contain complementary literals — is the fastest way to see it.

Step by step.

  1. Resolve (i) with (ii) on p: the resolvent is q ∨ r.
  2. Resolve that with (iii) on q: the resolvent is r ∨ r ≡ r.
  3. So r is derivable, hence follows.
  4. Model check: the premises hold exactly on (F,T,T), (T,F,T), (T,T,T), and r is true in all three.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (C) each fail in one of the models: p is false in (F,T,T) and q is false in (T,F,T).
  • (D) contradicts every model.

Common misconception. Trying to derive p or q because they appear in the premises. Only the variable that appears on the right of both implications is forced.

Faster method. Rewrite (ii) and (iii) as implications: p → r and q → r. With p ∨ q, a proof by cases gives r immediately.

Takeaway. “p ∨ q, p → r, q → r ⊢ r” is the rule of proof by cases — one of the most useful valid forms in this unit.

DM-PL2-Q19MSQmedium

Let the premises be p → q, q → r and r → s. Which of the following conclusions follow logically? (Select all that apply.)

  1. p → s
  2. ¬s → ¬p
  3. ¬p → ¬s
  4. p → r
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Implication is transitive, so a chain can be shortened to any link; and any derived implication may be replaced by its contrapositive.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Hypothetical syllogism applied twice: p → q, q → r, r → s give p → s.
  2. (B) is the contrapositive of (A), hence equivalent to it.
  3. (D) One application of hypothetical syllogism gives p → r.
  4. (C) ¬p → ¬s is the inverse of (A) and is not implied: at p = F, s = T (with q = r = F) all premises hold but ¬p → ¬s is T → F = F.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) reverses the direction of the arrow while negating — that is the inverse, which is never implied by the original implication.

Common misconception. Assuming that a valid implication licenses its inverse. Only the contrapositive is free.

Faster method. Shorten chains freely; then, for each candidate, test one assignment that makes the hypothesis true and the conclusion false.

Takeaway. From a chain you may conclude any “tail → later element”, and the contrapositive of each such conclusion.

DM-PL2-Q25NATmedium

Consider the premises p → q, q → r and ¬p. For how many of the eight truth assignments to (p, q, r) are all three premises simultaneously TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count the models of the conjunction by taking the constraints one at a time; each implication forbids one pattern of truth values.

Step by step.

  1. Premise (iii) forces p = F, leaving four rows to examine.
  2. With p = F, p → q is vacuously true, so only q → r remains.
  3. If q = F, q → r is true for both values of r: rows (F,F,F) and (F,F,T).
  4. If q = T, q → r needs r = T: row (F,T,T). Count = 3.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Forgetting that p = F makes p → q vacuously true, and instead assuming q must also be false.

Faster method. With p fixed false, count the models of q → r over (q, r): 3 of the 4 rows. Answer 3.

Takeaway. Fix the variables that are pinned by unit premises first; the remaining count is usually a small truth table.

CNF / DNF 8
DM-PL1-Q05MCQeasy

Which one of the following formulas is in conjunctive normal form (CNF)?

  1. (p ∧ q) ∨ r
  2. p ∧ (q ∨ ¬r)
  3. ¬(p ∨ q)
  4. p → q
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CNF is a conjunction of clauses, where each clause is a disjunction of literals (a variable or its negation).

Step by step.

  1. (B) reads as p ∧ (q ∨ ¬r): the outer connective is ∧, and each of its two conjuncts is a clause (p is a one-literal clause). So it is CNF.
  2. (A) has ∨ at the top level — it is a disjunction, not a conjunction of clauses.
  3. (C) has ¬ applied to a disjunction, so a clause is negated as a whole; that is not CNF until De Morgan is applied.
  4. (D) contains →, which is not a CNF connective at all.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is in DNF, not CNF.
  • (C) becomes ¬p ∧ ¬q only after applying De Morgan; as written it is not CNF.
  • (D) is an implication, not a normal form.

Common misconception. Confusing “is equivalent to some CNF” with “is written in CNF”. Every formula has a CNF, but the question asks about the written shape.

Faster method. Look at the outermost connective: ∧ with clause-shaped operands means CNF; ∨ with term-shaped operands means DNF.

Takeaway. CNF = AND of ORs; DNF = OR of ANDs. Read the outermost connective first.

DM-PL1-Q10MCQmedium

The disjunctive normal form (DNF) of p ⊕ q (exclusive or) is

  1. (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ ¬q)
  2. (p ∧ ¬q) ∨ (¬p ∧ q)
  3. (p ∧ q) ∨ (¬p ∧ ¬q)
  4. (p ∨ ¬q) ∧ (¬p ∨ q)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The full DNF is the OR of one minterm per row where the formula is true. p ⊕ q is true exactly on the rows where p and q differ.

Step by step.

  1. Rows: (T,F) gives the minterm p ∧ ¬q; (F,T) gives ¬p ∧ q.
  2. (T,T) and (F,F) make p ⊕ q false, so those minterms are excluded.
  3. Therefore p ⊕ q ≡ (p ∧ ¬q) ∨ (¬p ∧ q).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the CNF of p ⊕ q (a conjunction of clauses), not the DNF.
  • (C) is the DNF of p ↔ q — the rows where the two agree.
  • (D) is the CNF of p ↔ q.

Common misconception. Mixing up ⊕ with ↔: XOR is true when the inputs differ, equivalence when they agree. The two have complementary minterm sets.

Faster method. Read the name: DNF = OR of the true rows; CNF = AND of the false rows. XOR has two true rows and two false rows, so both forms have two terms.

Takeaway. Every Boolean function has both a DNF and a CNF; the DNF lists the 1-rows, the CNF the 0-rows.

DM-PL1-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following formulas are in conjunctive normal form (CNF)? (Select all that apply.)

  1. p ∧ (¬q ∨ r)
  2. (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ r)
  3. ¬(p ∧ q)
  4. p ∨ (q ∧ r)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CNF is a conjunction of clauses; each clause is a disjunction of literals only.

Step by step.

  1. (A) outer ∧, conjuncts p and (¬q ∨ r) — both clauses. CNF.
  2. (B) outer ∧, both operands are disjunctions of literals. CNF.
  3. (C) ¬ is applied to a conjunction, not to a single literal, so the operand of the top-level ¬ is not a clause. Not CNF (it is equivalent to ¬p ∨ ¬q, which is a single clause).
  4. (D) outer ∨ with a conjunctive right operand — that is DNF shape, not CNF.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) becomes CNF only after De Morgan is applied.
  • (D) is in DNF.

Common misconception. Accepting (C) because “it has only two variables”. CNF constrains the shape — negations may only sit on single literals.

Faster method. Scan for a ¬ in front of a bracket: if one is at the top of a clause, the formula is not yet CNF.

Takeaway. CNF: literals → clauses (∨) → one big ∧. DNF: literals → terms (∧) → one big ∨.

DM-PL1-Q23NATeasy

How many minterms appear in the full disjunctive normal form of p ↔ q?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The full DNF has one minterm per row on which the formula is true.

Step by step.

  1. p ↔ q is true exactly when p and q have the same value.
  2. Rows: (T,T) contributes the minterm p ∧ q; (F,F) contributes ¬p ∧ ¬q.
  3. The rows (T,F) and (F,T) make it false and contribute nothing.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Confusing the number of minterms with the number of variables, or with the number of clauses in the CNF (also 2 here, which hides the error).

Faster method. Count the rows where the formula is true — that is exactly the minterm count.

Takeaway. Minterms of the DNF = 1-rows; maxterms of the CNF = 0-rows.

DM-PL2-Q05MCQeasy

Which one of the following formulas is in disjunctive normal form (DNF)?

  1. (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ r)
  2. (p ∧ q) ∨ (¬p ∧ r)
  3. ¬(p ∨ q)
  4. p → (q ∧ r)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. DNF is a disjunction of terms, where each term is a conjunction of literals (a variable or its negation). The outermost connective must be ∨ and the next level must be ∧ over literals.

Step by step.

  1. (B) reads as (p ∧ q) OR (¬p ∧ r): outer ∨, each operand a conjunction of literals. This is DNF.
  2. (A) has outer ∧ with clause-shaped operands — that is CNF, not DNF.
  3. (C) has a negation applied to a disjunction, so a negation does not sit on a literal; it is not a normal form as written.
  4. (D) contains an implication, which no normal form allows.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is CNF (an AND of ORs) — the mirror image of the definition.
  • (C) becomes ¬p ∧ ¬q only after De Morgan is applied; as written it is not DNF.
  • (D) is an implication, not a normal form.

Common misconception. Reading the shape from the inside out. Read the outermost connective first: ∨ at the top with AND-terms inside means DNF.

Faster method. DNF = OR of ANDs; CNF = AND of ORs. Check the top two levels only.

Takeaway. “In DNF” is a syntactic test on the written formula, not a claim about equivalence. Every formula has some DNF; only some formulas are written in it.

DM-PL2-Q10MCQmedium

The conjunctive normal form (CNF) of p → (q ∧ r) is

  1. (¬p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ r)
  2. ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r)
  3. (p ∨ q) ∧ (p ∨ r)
  4. (¬p ∧ q) ∨ (¬p ∧ r)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CNF is a conjunction of clauses, and each clause is a disjunction of literals. Turning an implication into CNF needs material implication followed by distribution of ∨ over ∧.

Step by step.

  1. Remove the arrow: p → (q ∧ r) ≡ ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r).
  2. Distribute ∨ over ∧: ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r) ≡ (¬p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ r).
  3. Both factors are clauses of literals and the outer connective is ∧, so this is CNF.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is correct as an equivalence but is not in CNF: a conjunction sits inside a disjunction.
  • (C) drops the negations, giving a different function (it is the CNF of ¬p → (q ∧ r)).
  • (D) is a DNF-shaped formula, not a CNF.

Common misconception. Stopping at ¬p ∨ (q ∧ r). “Equivalent to a CNF” and “written in CNF” are different requirements.

Faster method. Implication first, then distribute. The clause ¬p is copied into every clause of the answer.

Takeaway. A formula with n conjuncts in the consequent produces n clauses in CNF, all sharing the negated antecedent.

DM-PL2-Q14MCQhard

How many clauses (maxterms) does the full CNF — the product of maxterms — of p ⊕ q ⊕ r contain?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 8
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The full CNF has one maxterm for every row on which the formula is FALSE; the full DNF has one minterm for every row on which it is TRUE.

Step by step.

  1. XOR is true exactly when an odd number of its inputs are true. Over three variables that is the rows with one true input (three rows) and with three true inputs (one row): 4 rows.
  2. The remaining 8 − 4 = 4 rows — zero true inputs and two true inputs — are the falsifying rows.
  3. One maxterm per falsifying row gives 4 clauses.
  4. Sanity check: the full DNF has 4 minterms, and 4 + 4 = 8 accounts for every row.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • 2 counts the true/false groups of the two-variable case.
  • 3 counts only the rows with exactly one true input.
  • 8 is the number of rows, not the number of falsifying rows.

Common misconception. Counting the true rows and calling them clauses. Minterms come from true rows (DNF); maxterms come from false rows (CNF).

Faster method. For n-ary XOR the count is symmetric: 2^(n−1) true rows and 2^(n−1) false rows. Here 4 and 4.

Takeaway. Full CNF size = number of falsifying rows. Full DNF size = number of satisfying rows. Their sum is always 2ⁿ.

DM-PL2-Q22NATeasy

How many minterms appear in the full disjunctive normal form of p ∧ q?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The full DNF has exactly one minterm per row on which the formula is true.

Step by step.

  1. The formula is true only on the row (p, q) = (T, T).
  2. That row contributes the single minterm p ∧ q.
  3. All other rows are false and contribute nothing. Count = 1.

Answer: 1.

Common misconception. Counting the variables (2) or the literals in the term (2) instead of the number of true rows (1).

Faster method. Number of minterms = number of satisfying rows. A conjunction of k literals over k variables has exactly one.

Takeaway. Full DNF size = number of models. For a single conjunction of literals that is 1.

Validity & satisfiability 8
DM-PL1-Q06MCQeasy

The proposition p ∧ ¬p is

  1. a tautology.
  2. a contradiction (unsatisfiable).
  3. a contingency.
  4. a biconditional that is always true.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A formula is a tautology if true on every assignment, a contradiction if true on none, and a contingency if true on at least one but not all.

Step by step.

  1. Try p = T: p ∧ ¬p = T ∧ F = F.
  2. Try p = F: p ∧ ¬p = F ∧ T = F.
  3. False on both assignments, so it is unsatisfiable — a contradiction.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) p ∨ ¬p is the tautology; the conjunction is its opposite.
  • (C) a contingency must be true somewhere, which this is not.
  • (D) no biconditional is involved.

Common misconception. Reading p ∧ ¬p as “p and not p can both be considered”, or assuming any formula built from one variable must be satisfiable.

Faster method. A formula containing a literal and its negation as direct conjuncts is immediately unsatisfiable.

Takeaway. Validity = true on all rows; satisfiability = true on some row. p ∨ ¬p and p ∧ ¬p are the two extreme cases.

DM-PL1-Q14MCQhard

Which one of the following formulas is satisfiable but not valid?

  1. p ∨ ¬p
  2. p ∧ ¬p
  3. (p → q) ∧ (q → r) ∧ ¬(p → r)
  4. (p ∨ q) ∧ ¬(p ∧ q)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Valid = true on every assignment. Satisfiable = true on at least one. “Satisfiable but not valid” means true somewhere and false somewhere.

Step by step.

  1. (A) p ∨ ¬p is true on both assignments — valid, so it fails the “not valid” half.
  2. (B) p ∧ ¬p is false everywhere — unsatisfiable, so it fails the “satisfiable” half.
  3. (C) Suppose p → q and q → r hold but p → r fails. Failing p → r forces p = T, r = F. Then p → q forces q = T, and q → r forces r = T — contradiction. So (C) is unsatisfiable.
  4. (D) p = T, q = F gives (T ∨ F) ∧ ¬(T ∧ F) = T ∧ T = T, so it is satisfiable; p = q = T gives T ∧ ¬T = F, so it is not valid.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is valid.
  • (B) is unsatisfiable.
  • (C) is unsatisfiable, because → is transitive.

Common misconception. Trying to break transitivity with (C): (p → q) ∧ (q → r) ∧ ¬(p → r) looks plausible but has no model. Implication is transitive, so that conjunction is a contradiction.

Faster method. For “satisfiable but not valid”, hunt one model and one counter-model. Only (D) admits both; (A) admits no counter-model and (B), (C) admit no model.

Takeaway. ¬(p → r) = p ∧ ¬r. Combining it with p → q and q → r is the standard way to build an unsatisfiable formula.

DM-PL1-Q20MSQhard

Which of the following formulas are satisfiable? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ q) ∧ (p ∨ ¬q) ∧ (¬p ∨ ¬q)
  2. (p → q) ∧ (q → r) ∧ (r → p)
  3. (p ∨ q ∨ r) ∧ (¬p ∨ ¬q ∨ ¬r)
  4. (p ↔ q) ∧ (q ⊕ r)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A formula is satisfiable if at least one assignment makes it true. For a CNF, look for an assignment satisfying every clause simultaneously.

Step by step.

  1. (A) The first three clauses force q = T (from ¬p ∨ q and p ∨ q with p = T) and then the fourth clause ¬p ∨ ¬q fails. Exhaustively: p = T forces q = T from (¬p ∨ q) and then (¬p ∨ ¬q) is false; p = F forces q = T from (p ∨ q) and then (¬p ∨ ¬q) is false again. Unsatisfiable.
  2. (B) p = q = r = T satisfies all three implications; p = q = r = F does too.
  3. (C) p = T, q = F, r = F satisfies both clauses.
  4. (D) p = q = T, r = F: p ↔ q is true and q ⊕ r is true.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the CNF of p ⊕ q — an unsatisfiable conjunction of all four clauses over two variables.

Common misconception. Believing any CNF with many clauses is satisfiable, or checking clauses one at a time instead of simultaneously.

Faster method. For a two-variable formula, try the four rows in order; for (A) note it demands q with p and q with ¬p at once.

Takeaway. (B) shows that a cycle of implications is satisfiable — it forces p, q, r to agree, which is a useful pattern.

DM-PL1-Q24NATmedium

For how many of the eight truth assignments to (p, q, r) is (p → q) ∧ (q → r) TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The conjunction is true exactly on the assignments where p, q, r never decrease in truth value, i.e. p ≤ q ≤ r.

Step by step.

  1. List the non-decreasing triples over {F, T}: (F,F,F), (F,F,T), (F,T,T), (T,T,T).
  2. Check (T,T,F): p → q is true but q → r is false. Excluded.
  3. Check (F,T,F): p → q true, q → r false. Excluded.
  4. Check (T,F,T) and (T,F,F): p → q is false. Excluded.
  5. Remaining valid assignments: the four listed above.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Assuming the conjunction is true whenever at least one implication is true, which would give 6 or more rows.

Faster method. Both implications true ⇔ p ≤ q ≤ r as a chain of truth values; the number of non-decreasing sequences of length 3 over two values is C(3+2−1, 3) = 4.

Takeaway. A chain of implications behaves like an ordering constraint on truth values.

DM-PL2-Q06MCQeasy

The proposition p ∨ ¬p is

  1. a tautology.
  2. a contradiction.
  3. a contingency.
  4. satisfiable but not valid.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A tautology is true under every assignment, a contradiction false under every assignment, and a contingency true under some and false under others.

Step by step.

  1. If p is true, p ∨ ¬p is true because the first disjunct is true.
  2. If p is false, ¬p is true, so the disjunction is true again.
  3. Both possible assignments make it true, so it is true under every assignment: a tautology.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) confuses p ∨ ¬p with p ∧ ¬p, which is the contradiction (the law of non-contradiction).
  • (C) and (D) both require at least one falsifying assignment, and there is none.

Common misconception. Mixing up the law of excluded middle (p ∨ ¬p, always true) with the law of non-contradiction (¬(p ∧ ¬p), also always true) and with p ∧ ¬p, which is always false.

Faster method. Any formula of the shape X ∨ ¬X is a tautology; any of the shape X ∧ ¬X is a contradiction.

Takeaway. Recognise the two classical laws by shape — no truth table needed.

DM-PL2-Q13MCQhard

Which one of the following formulas is a tautology?

  1. (p → q) → (q → p)
  2. (p → q) ∨ (q → p)
  3. (p ⊕ q) → (p ∧ q)
  4. (p ↔ q) → (p ∧ q)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A formula is a tautology when every assignment makes it true. To refute a candidate, exhibit one falsifying assignment.

Step by step.

  1. Test (B) by cases on p → q. If p → q is true, the disjunction is true.
  2. If p → q is false, then p = T and q = F; then q → p is F → T, which is true. So the disjunction is true here as well.
  3. Both cases cover all four assignments, so (B) is a tautology.
  4. Refute the others: (A) fails at p = F, q = T since T → F = F; (C) fails at p = T, q = F since T → F = F; (D) fails at p = q = F since T → F = F.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reverses the arrow and fails on the asymmetric row (F, T).
  • (C) is false whenever exactly one of p, q is true.
  • (D) is false when both are false, because p ↔ q is then true while p ∧ q is not.

Common misconception. Reading (B) as “one implication or its converse” and assuming a disjunction of two non-tautologies cannot be a tautology. Two formulas that are never false together can have a tautological disjunction.

Faster method. For “X ∨ Y is a tautology”, check that X and Y cannot both be false. Here p → q false forces q → p true.

Takeaway. (p → q) ∨ (q → p) is the standard example of a tautology that is not built from tautological parts.

DM-PL2-Q20MSQhard

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every tautology is satisfiable.
  2. Every satisfiable formula is a tautology.
  3. A formula and its negation can both be satisfiable.
  4. If a formula is not valid, then its negation is satisfiable.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Satisfiable = true on at least one assignment. Valid (tautology) = true on every assignment. Unsatisfiable = true on no assignment.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: a tautology is true on all 2ⁿ assignments, so certainly on one.
  2. (B) False: p is satisfiable but false at p = F, so it is not valid.
  3. (C) True: take p and ¬p; each is satisfiable (on different rows). Only a formula and its negation cannot both be valid.
  4. (D) True: if F is not valid, some assignment makes it false, and on that assignment ¬F is true — so ¬F is satisfiable.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) confuses “true somewhere” with “true everywhere”. Satisfaction is an existential condition, validity a universal one.

Common misconception. The common error is to swap the quantifiers: valid ⇔ satisfiable is a one-way implication, not an equivalence.

Faster method. Keep the ladder: valid ⇒ satisfiable; the converse fails. F unsat ⇔ ¬F valid.

Takeaway. Validity and satisfiability are duals under negation: F is valid iff ¬F is unsatisfiable.

DM-PL2-Q24NATmedium

For how many of the eight truth assignments to (p, q, r) is the formula (p ↔ q) → r TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An implication is false only when the antecedent is true and the consequent false. So count the falsifying rows and subtract from 8.

Step by step.

  1. The antecedent p ↔ q is false on (T, F) and (F, T); on those two rows the implication is vacuously true whatever r is: 2 × 2 = 4 rows.
  2. On the two rows where p ↔ q is true — (T, T) and (F, F) — the implication is true only when r = T: 2 rows.
  3. Total true rows = 4 + 2 = 6; the two falsifying rows are (T, T, F) and (F, F, F).

Answer: 6.

Common misconception. Counting the rows where r = T (four) and forgetting the vacuous rows where p ↔ q is false.

Faster method. Falsifying rows = (models of the antecedent) × (rows where the consequent fails) = 2 × 1 = 2. True rows = 8 − 2 = 6.

Takeaway. Count falsifying rows for implications: models of the antecedent × counter-models of the consequent.

Unit 2 — Predicate & First-Order Logic 50 solved questions
Predicates & domain 10
DM-FOL1-Q01MCQeasy

Let P(x) be the predicate “x + 2 > 5”, where the domain of x is ℤ. Which one of the following is correct?

  1. P(x) is a proposition, and it is true.
  2. P(x) is not a proposition; it becomes a proposition only when x is given a value or is quantified.
  3. P(x) is a proposition, and it is false.
  4. P(x) is a proposition only when x is a positive integer.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predicate with a free variable is an open sentence, not a proposition. It acquires a truth value only when the variable is bound — by substitution of a value or by a quantifier.

Step by step.

  1. The expression P(x) contains the free variable x, so its truth value depends on which integer is substituted.
  2. For x = 4 it is true (4 + 2 > 5), and for x = 1 it is false — two different truth values from the same expression.
  3. Something that is sometimes true and sometimes false is not a proposition; it is a predicate.
  4. Once we write P(4) or ∀x P(x), the variable is bound and the result is a proposition.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) both assign a single truth value to an expression whose truth depends on x.
  • (D) confuses the domain restriction with the property of being a proposition: restricting the domain still leaves x free, so P(x) is still not a proposition.

Common misconception. Many students answer (A) because “x + 2 > 5 is true for most integers”. Being true for most values is not the same as having a definite truth value.

Faster method. Look for a free variable. Free variable present ⇒ predicate; no free variable ⇒ proposition.

Takeaway. A predicate is a truth-valued function; a proposition is its value at a point or under a quantifier.

DM-FOL1-Q06MCQeasy

Over which one of the following domains is the statement ∀x (x² ≥ x) FALSE?

  1. ℕ = {0, 1, 2, 3, …}
  2. ℤ
  3. the finite set {1, 2, 3}
  4. ℝ
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A quantified statement's truth value depends on the domain. x² − x = x(x − 1) is negative exactly for 0 < x < 1.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: x² − x = x(x − 1). It is ≥ 0 when x ≤ 0 or x ≥ 1, and negative for 0 < x < 1.
  2. Over ℕ: values 0, 1, 2, … all satisfy x² ≥ x. TRUE.
  3. Over ℤ and over {1, 2, 3}: every element is ≤ 0 or ≥ 1, so the inequality holds. TRUE.
  4. Over ℝ: take x = 0.5; then x² = 0.25 < 0.5. FALSE — this domain supplies a counterexample.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) contain no element strictly between 0 and 1, so the negative region of x(x − 1) is never entered.

Common misconception. Believing the truth of a quantified statement is a property of the formula alone. Change the domain and the truth value can flip — here from true to false.

Faster method. Solve the inequality once; the counterexamples are exactly the complement of its solution set. Then ask which domain meets that complement.

Takeaway. Before judging a quantified statement, read its domain. Fractions are the usual source of failures for x² ≥ x.

DM-FOL1-Q11MCQmedium

For which one of the following domains is the statement ∀x ∃y (y > x) FALSE?

  1. ℕ = {0, 1, 2, …}
  2. ℤ
  3. ℝ
  4. the finite set {1, 2, …, 10}
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An unbounded domain always supplies a larger element; a finite domain has a maximum, and that maximum has no y above it.

Step by step.

  1. Over ℕ, ℤ and ℝ the witness y = x + 1 always lies in the domain. TRUE in each case.
  2. Over {1, …, 10}, take x = 10. No element of the domain exceeds 10, so the inner ∃y fails.
  3. One failing x is enough to falsify the universal statement, so the statement is FALSE on the finite domain.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) are unbounded above, so x + 1 is always available.

Common misconception. Checking only “typical” small values of x. Universally quantified statements over a finite domain fail exactly at the boundary element — here the maximum.

Faster method. For ∀x ∃y (y > x), ask whether the domain has a maximum element. If it does, the statement fails there.

Takeaway. Finiteness is a domain property that changes quantifier truth values; always test the extreme element.

DM-FOL1-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following have a definite truth value as written — that is, are propositions? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∃x ∈ ℤ (x² = 4)
  2. x + 1 = 7
  3. ∀n ∈ ℕ (n + 0 = n)
  4. “P(x)”, where P is a predicate symbol and x is a free variable
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A statement is a proposition when every variable in it is bound. A quantifier binds its variable; a free variable leaves the statement open.

Step by step.

  1. (A) has no free variable — x is bound by ∃ — and it is true, since x = 2 works. A proposition.
  2. (B) contains a free x; it is true for x = 6 and false otherwise. Not a proposition.
  3. (C) has no free variable and is true for every natural number. A proposition.
  4. (D) is a predicate applied to a free variable; it has no truth value until x is bound. Not a proposition.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is an open sentence: its truth value changes with x.
  • (D) is an unevaluated predicate application, which is exactly what a free variable produces.

Common misconception. Judging by whether the sentence “looks mathematical”. The test is syntactic: is any variable left free?

Faster method. Scan for free variables only. No free variable ⇒ proposition, whatever the content.

Takeaway. Quantifying a predicate turns it into a proposition; leaving a variable free keeps it a predicate.

DM-FOL1-Q21NATeasy

Let P be a predicate on the three-element domain D = {1, 2, 3}, described by its truth set T = {x ∈ D : P(x)}. For how many of the 8 possible truth sets T is ∃x P(x) TRUE?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predicate on a finite domain is determined by its truth set. ∃x P(x) is true exactly when the truth set is non-empty.

Step by step.

  1. There are 2³ = 8 subsets of D, hence 8 predicates.
  2. ∃x P(x) holds iff T ≠ ∅.
  3. Only the empty set fails, so the count is 8 − 1 = 7.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Counting 8 by including the empty truth set. With T = ∅ there is no witness, so the existential is false.

Faster method. Number of predicates satisfying ∃x P(x) on an n-element domain is 2ⁿ − 1.

Takeaway. Truth sets turn quantifier questions into counting questions: ∃ means non-empty, ∀ means the whole domain.

DM-FOL2-Q01MCQeasy

Let the domain be ℤ and let P(x) be the predicate “x² = x”. Which one of the following is the truth set {x ∈ ℤ : P(x)}?

  1. {0, 1}
  2. {1}
  3. {0}
  4. {−1, 0, 1}
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The truth set of a predicate on a domain D is the subset of D on which the predicate is true. Finding it means solving the equation, not guessing.

Step by step.

  1. Solve x² = x: bring everything to one side, x² − x = 0.
  2. Factor: x(x − 1) = 0, so x = 0 or x = 1.
  3. Both 0 and 1 are integers, so both lie in the domain ℤ.
  4. Hence the truth set is {0, 1}.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) drops the solution x = 0; dividing both sides by x is invalid precisely because x = 0 is a solution.
  • (C) drops x = 1.
  • (D) adds x = −1, but (−1)² = 1 ≠ −1.

Common misconception. The most common error is cancelling x from both sides of x² = x, which silently assumes x ≠ 0 and loses a root.

Faster method. A polynomial of degree 2 has at most 2 roots over ℤ, so once you have 0 and 1 you can stop.

Takeaway. Truth sets turn a predicate into a subset; questions about a predicate become questions about its truth set.

DM-FOL2-Q06MCQmedium

Let D be a non-empty domain and P a predicate on D. Which one of the following must be TRUE for every such P?

  1. (∃x P(x)) → (∀x P(x))
  2. (∀x P(x)) → (∃x P(x))
  3. (∃x P(x)) → P(a) for a fixed element a ∈ D
  4. ∀x P(x) is true
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Because D is non-empty, a universal truth has a witness: ∀x P(x) entails ∃x P(x). The converse implication fails.

Step by step.

  1. If every element of D satisfies P, pick any element (possible because D is non-empty); it satisfies P.
  2. Hence ∃x P(x) holds, so (∀x P(x)) → (∃x P(x)) is true for every P.
  3. Counterexample for the other direction: D = {1, 2} with P true only at 1. Then ∃x P(x) is true but ∀x P(x) is false.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails on the two-element counterexample above.
  • (C) fails whenever P holds somewhere other than at a.
  • (D) is plainly false — take P false everywhere.

Common misconception. The empty domain is the trap. Over ∅, ∀x P(x) is true for every P but ∃x P(x) is false, so the implication in (B) would fail. The question's non-emptiness assumption is what makes (B) valid.

Faster method. Whenever a quantifier question hinges on existence, check the domain: non-empty or possibly empty.

Takeaway. On a non-empty domain ∀ ⇒ ∃, never the reverse. On the empty domain even that fails.

DM-FOL2-Q11MCQmedium

By convention, ∀x P(x) is TRUE over the empty domain ∅ for every predicate P. Which one of the following follows from that convention?

  1. ∀x P(x) is FALSE over ∅ for every P.
  2. ∃x P(x) is TRUE over ∅.
  3. Both ∀x P(x) and ∀x ¬P(x) are TRUE over ∅.
  4. No formula is TRUE over the empty domain.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A universal statement is a conjunction over the domain. Over the empty domain it is the empty conjunction, which is true — and that holds for any predicate, including ¬P.

Step by step.

  1. Over D = ∅, ∀x P(x) has no instance to fail, so it is vacuously true.
  2. The same argument applies to the predicate ¬P: ∀x ¬P(x) is also vacuously true.
  3. So both universals hold simultaneously, even though they contradict each other on any non-empty domain.
  4. The existential ∃x P(x), by contrast, is FALSE over ∅ for every P, since there is no witness.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the opposite of the convention.
  • (B) inverts the rule for existentials: an existential needs a witness, and the empty domain has none.
  • (D) is false: universals over ∅ are true.

Common misconception. The result looks paradoxical, and students reject it by intuition. It is the price of the convention, and it is what makes ∀x P(x) and ¬∃x ¬P(x) remain equivalent even when the domain is empty.

Faster method. Remember the chain: universals over ∅ are true, existentials over ∅ are false.

Takeaway. Vacuous truth is a consequence of the definition, not a special case to be patched.

DM-FOL2-Q16MSQeasy

Let the domain be D = {2, 4, 6}. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∀x (x is even)
  2. ∃x (x > 5)
  3. ∀x (x > 1)
  4. ∃x (x = 3)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. On a finite domain every quantified statement can be decided by checking each element: a universal is a conjunction over the domain, an existential a disjunction.

Step by step.

  1. (A) 2, 4 and 6 are all even. TRUE.
  2. (B) 6 > 5, so a witness exists. TRUE.
  3. (C) 2, 4 and 6 are all greater than 1. TRUE.
  4. (D) 3 is not in D, so no element equals 3. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) fails because the witness must come from the domain; 3 is outside D.

Common misconception. Students answer (D) by reading “x = 3” as an arithmetic fact rather than a statement about the domain. The quantifier ranges over D only.

Faster method. On a small finite domain, evaluate every quantifier by hand; there is no need for theory.

Takeaway. Always check the domain before evaluating a witness.

DM-FOL2-Q21NATeasy

Let D = {1, 2, 3, 4}. How many of the 2⁴ = 16 predicates on D have a truth set of size exactly 2?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A predicate on a finite domain is determined by its truth set, and every subset of the domain is the truth set of exactly one predicate.

Step by step.

  1. A predicate with truth set of size exactly 2 is determined by choosing 2 of the 4 elements to be true.
  2. The number of such choices is C(4, 2) = 6.
  3. The other two elements are forced to be false, so each choice gives exactly one predicate.

Answer: 6.

Common misconception. Counting predicates of size 3 instead of size 2, or using 2⁴ directly. The truth set size is fixed at exactly 2, so the answer is a single binomial coefficient.

Faster method. Number of predicates with truth set of size k on an n-element domain is C(n, k).

Takeaway. Predicates on a finite domain ↔ subsets of the domain; counting predicates is counting subsets.

Universal & existential quantifiers 10
DM-FOL1-Q02MCQeasy

Let the domain be D = {1, 2, 3, 4}. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. ∀x ∃y (x + y = 5)
  2. ∃x ∀y (x + y = 5)
  3. ∀x ∀y (x + y < 8)
  4. ∃x ∃y (x + y = 9)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The order of quantifiers changes meaning. ∀x ∃y lets the witness y depend on x; ∃x ∀y demands one witness that works for every y.

Step by step.

  1. ∀x ∃y (x + y = 5): take y = 5 − x. For x = 1, 2, 3, 4 the witnesses are 4, 3, 2, 1, all inside D. TRUE.
  2. ∃x ∀y (x + y = 5): a single x would need x + 1 = 5 and x + 4 = 5 at once. FALSE.
  3. ∀x ∀y (x + y < 8): fails at x = y = 4, where the sum is exactly 8, not less. FALSE.
  4. ∃x ∃y (x + y = 9): the largest possible sum is 4 + 4 = 8. FALSE.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) needs one x working for all four y values; no such x exists.
  • (C) is off by one at the boundary 4 + 4 = 8.
  • (D) asks for a sum that the domain cannot produce.

Common misconception. Treating ∀x ∃y and ∃y ∀x as the same statement. The witness in the first may depend on x; in the second it may not.

Faster method. Evaluate a finite domain by drawing the addition table and scanning each row (for ∀x ∃y) or each column (for ∃x ∀y).

Takeaway. ∀x ∃y is a per-row claim; ∃x ∀y is a single-witness claim. Always check the boundary values of a finite domain.

DM-FOL1-Q07MCQmedium

Let the domain be ℝ. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. ∀x ∃y (x + y = 0)
  2. ∃y ∀x (x + y = 0)
  3. ∀x ∀y (x · y = 0)
  4. ∃x ∀y (x · y = 1)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In ∀x ∃y φ(x, y) the witness may depend on x; in ∃y ∀x φ(x, y) one fixed witness must serve every x.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Given x, choose y = −x, which is in ℝ. TRUE.
  2. (B) A single y would need x = −y for every x, which is impossible for two different x. FALSE.
  3. (C) Fails at x = y = 1: the product is 1, not 0. FALSE.
  4. (D) For a fixed x, x · y = 1 fails at y = 0 whatever x is. FALSE.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (D) both assert the existence of one witness that must work for all values of the other variable — the classic ∃∀ overreach.
  • (C) is refuted by any pair of non-zero reals.

Common misconception. Reading ∀x ∃y as ∃y ∀x. The first is almost always easier to satisfy because the witness is allowed to depend on x.

Faster method. Try to name the witness as a function of x. If you can, the ∀∃ statement is true; if you must fix a constant first, check the ∃∀ form.

Takeaway. Only one direction is valid in general: ∃y ∀x P(x, y) ⇒ ∀x ∃y P(x, y), never the reverse.

DM-FOL1-Q15MCQhard

Which one of the following formulas is VALID — true in every interpretation over every non-empty domain?

  1. ∀x (P(x) ∨ Q(x)) → (∀x P(x) ∨ ∀x Q(x))
  2. (∀x P(x) ∨ ∀x Q(x)) → ∀x (P(x) ∨ Q(x))
  3. (∃x P(x) ∧ ∃x Q(x)) → ∃x (P(x) ∧ Q(x))
  4. ∀x ∃y P(x, y) → ∃y ∀x P(x, y)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A formula is valid if every interpretation makes it true. Universal quantifiers distribute over ∧ but not over ∨; existential quantifiers distribute over ∨ but not over ∧.

Step by step.

  1. (B): if every element is a P, or every element is a Q, then certainly every element is P or Q. Valid.
  2. (A) fails on a two-element domain with P = {1}, Q = {2}: the hypothesis holds, both disjuncts of the conclusion fail.
  3. (C) fails on the same interpretation: ∃x P and ∃x Q hold, but no element is both.
  4. (D) fails on {1, 2} with P holding only on (1,1) and (2,2): ∀x ∃y P holds, ∃y ∀x P does not.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reverses the valid distribution: a pointwise disjunction does not give a uniform choice of disjunct.
  • (C) merges two existentials that may be witnessed by different elements.
  • (D) is the invalid direction of the quantifier swap.

Common misconception. Assuming ∀ distributes over both ∧ and ∨. It distributes over ∧ only. Likewise ∃ distributes over ∨ only.

Faster method. For any claimed validity, try the two-element domain with P and Q on different elements. It refutes most false distribution laws in one line.

Takeaway. Valid: ∀(P ∧ Q) ⇔ ∀P ∧ ∀Q and ∃(P ∨ Q) ⇔ ∃P ∨ ∃Q. The ∨/∧ mirror images are invalid.

DM-FOL1-Q17MSQmedium

Let the domain be ℝ. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ∀x ∃y (x + y = 0)
  2. ∃x ∀y (x · y = y)
