  6. Finite binary strings: the strings of length n number 2ⁿ, and the union over countably many n of finite sets is countable — countably infinite.
  7. Count: ℤ, ℚ, ℕ×ℕ and the finite binary strings — 4 sets.

Answer: Answer: 4.

Common misconception. The set of all binary strings of infinite length is a different object: it is uncountable. The word “finite” is what keeps this set countable, and it is the most frequently missed word in the list.

Faster method. Two questions decide each entry: can I enumerate it? If it is a finite product, a finite string or a finite subset, yes.

Takeaway. Every program is a finite binary string, so programs are countable — while the set of all functions ℕ → ℕ is not, which is why most functions are uncomputable.

Set algebra & De Morgan 10
DM-ST1-Q07MCQeasy

For subsets A and B of a universal set U, (A ∪ B)′ equals

  1. A′ ∩ B′
  2. A′ ∪ B′
  3. A ∩ B
  4. A′ − B′
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. De Morgan’s law for sets: the complement of a union is the intersection of the complements, and the complement of an intersection is the union of the complements.

Step by step.

  1. Work by membership: x ∈ (A ∪ B)′ ⇔ x ∉ A ∪ B.
  2. x ∉ A ∪ B ⇔ x ∉ A and x ∉ B ⇔ x ∈ A′ and x ∈ B′.
  3. That is x ∈ A′ ∩ B′, so the two sets have exactly the same elements.

Answer: Option (A) — (A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∩ B′.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the complement of the intersection, (A ∩ B)′ — the other half of De Morgan.
  • (C) is A ∩ B, which is not a complement at all.
  • (D) is a proper subset of the correct answer and omits elements outside both A and B that still lie in the other set’s complement.

Common misconception. The sign flips: a union becomes an intersection. Writing A′ ∪ B′ is the single most common error in set algebra.

Faster method. Complement swaps ∪ and ∩, exactly as negation swaps ∨ and ∧ in logic.

Takeaway. (A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∩ B′ and (A ∩ B)′ = A′ ∪ B′.

DM-ST2-Q07MCQeasy

For any sets A and B, the set A − (A − B) equals

  1. A ∩ B
  2. A − B
  3. B − A
  4. A ∪ B
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rewrite difference as intersection with a complement and simplify: A − X = A ∩ X′.

Step by step.

  1. A − B = A ∩ B′, so A − (A − B) = A ∩ (A ∩ B′)′.
  2. By De Morgan, (A ∩ B′)′ = A′ ∪ B.
  3. Distribute: A ∩ (A′ ∪ B) = (A ∩ A′) ∪ (A ∩ B) = ∅ ∪ (A ∩ B) = A ∩ B.

Answer: Option (A) — A ∩ B.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) A − B is the inner set, which was removed; it cannot survive.
  • (C) B − A shares no element with A, so it cannot be the answer.
  • (D) A ∪ B includes elements of B outside A, which the double difference never contains.

Common misconception. “Removing the removal puts everything back” is the natural but wrong intuition. Removing A − B from A leaves exactly the part of A that was in B.

Faster method. Test with A = {1, 2}, B = {2, 3}: A − B = {1} and A − {1} = {2} = A ∩ B.

Takeaway. Double difference is not the identity map; A − (A − B) = A ∩ B.

DM-ST2-Q08MCQmedium

For any sets A, B, C, the set (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ B′ ∩ C) equals

  1. A ∩ B ∩ C
  2. A ∪ (B ∩ C)
  3. A ∩ (B ∪ C)
  4. (A ∪ B) ∩ C
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Factor out the common A, then simplify the bracket with the absorption identity B ∪ (B′ ∩ C) = B ∪ C.

Step by step.

  1. Factor: (A ∩ B) ∪ (A ∩ B′ ∩ C) = A ∩ (B ∪ (B′ ∩ C)).
  2. Distribute the bracket: B ∪ (B′ ∩ C) = (B ∪ B′) ∩ (B ∪ C) = U ∩ (B ∪ C) = B ∪ C.
  3. Therefore the set is A ∩ (B ∪ C).

Answer: Option (C) — A ∩ (B ∪ C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A ∩ B ∩ C is far too small: it drops the whole of A ∩ B.
  • (B) A ∪ (B ∩ C) adds elements outside A, which neither term contains.
  • (D) (A ∪ B) ∩ C requires membership in C, which the first term does not.

Common misconception. Students reach for A ∩ B ∩ C because the second term looks like a narrowing of the first. The union of the two terms is what widens the result to all of A ∩ (B ∪ C).

Faster method. Take A = U = {1, 2, 3}, B = {1}, C = {2}. The expression gives {1, 2}, which matches only A ∩ (B ∪ C).

Takeaway. Difference-flavoured terms usually simplify by factoring out the common set and applying B ∪ (B′ ∩ C) = B ∪ C.

DM-ST2-Q09MCQhard

Starting from two sets A and B and applying ∪, ∩ and complement (relative to U = A ∪ B) any number of times, at most how many distinct sets can be produced?

  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 16
  4. 32
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two sets cut the universe into four disjoint regions; every set built from A, B by the Boolean operations is a union of some of those regions, so there are at most 2⁴ = 16 of them.

Step by step.

  1. The four Venn regions are A ∩ B, A ∩ B′, A′ ∩ B, A′ ∩ B′; they partition U.
  2. Any expression built with ∪, ∩, ′ is a union of whole regions — it never splits a region, because the generators are constant on each region.
  3. So each obtainable set is described by choosing a subset of the four regions: at most 2⁴ = 16 sets.
  4. All 16 really occur (the empty set, the four regions, the four unions of two regions, …), so the bound is attained.

Answer: Option (C) — 16.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 4 is the number of Venn regions, not the number of sets built from them.
  • (B) 8 would be the answer for a single generator with a universe of three regions.
  • (D) 32 = 2⁵ counts a fifth region that does not exist for two sets.

Common misconception. Counting expressions instead of counting the distinct sets they denote. Infinitely many expressions collapse onto 16 sets, which is why a finite bound exists at all.

Faster method. Memorise the formula: n generators give 2^{2ⁿ} sets in the free Boolean algebra — 4 for one, 16 for two, 256 for three.

Takeaway. The Boolean algebra on n generators has 2^{2ⁿ} elements; for n = 2 that is 16.

DM-ST2-Q18MSQmedium

For arbitrary sets A, B, C, which of the following identities hold? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A − (B ∪ C) = (A − B) ∩ (A − C)
  2. (A − B) − C = A − (B − C)
  3. (A ∪ B)′ = A′ ∩ B′
  4. (A − B) ∪ (B − A) = (A ∪ B) − (A ∩ B)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. De Morgan governs complements of unions and intersections; difference is a masked intersection; symmetric difference is the union minus the overlap.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A ∩ (B ∪ C)′ = A ∩ B′ ∩ C′ = (A ∩ B′) ∩ (A ∩ C′). TRUE.
  2. (B) Take A = {1, 2}, B = {2}, C = {2}. Left side is {1}, right side is {1, 2}. FALSE.
  3. (C) De Morgan's law for two sets. TRUE.
  4. (D) Both sides are exactly the elements in precisely one of A and B. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) are correct.

Common misconception. (B) is accepted because difference “looks” associative, as union and intersection are. It is not: the order of subtraction matters, and the right-hand side removes less than the left.

Faster method. For any claimed set identity, test one small case such as A = {1, 2}, B = {2}, C = {2} before doing algebra.

Takeaway. Difference is not associative; De Morgan and the symmetric-difference identity are the reliable rewrites.

DM-ST2-Q23NATeasy

Let |A| = 18, |B| = 14 and |A ∩ B| = 5. How many elements lie in (A − B) ∪ (B − A)?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The set (A − B) ∪ (B − A) is the symmetric difference: the elements in exactly one of the two sets, so count each side separately and add.

Step by step.

  1. |A − B| = |A| − |A ∩ B| = 18 − 5 = 13.
  2. |B − A| = |B| − |A ∩ B| = 14 − 5 = 9.
  3. The two parts are disjoint, so the union has 13 + 9 = 22 elements.

Answer: 22

Common misconception. Computing |A| + |B| = 32 and forgetting to remove the overlap twice. Symmetric difference excludes the shared part entirely.

Faster method. Symmetric difference count = |A| + |B| − 2|A ∩ B| = 18 + 14 − 10 = 22.

Takeaway. Exactly-one-of-two counts as |A| + |B| − 2|A ∩ B|.

DM-ST1-Q08MCQmedium

For any sets A, B, C, the set A − (B ∩ C) equals

  1. (A − B) ∪ (A − C)
  2. (A − B) ∩ (A − C)
  3. (A ∪ B) − C
  4. A ∩ (B − C)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Set difference is intersection with a complement, A − X = A ∩ X′. Applying De Morgan then flips the connective, which is where most errors come from.

Step by step.

  1. A − (B ∩ C) = A ∩ (B ∩ C)′.
  2. De Morgan: (B ∩ C)′ = B′ ∪ C′.
  3. Distribute: A ∩ (B′ ∪ C′) = (A ∩ B′) ∪ (A ∩ C′).
  4. Each bracket is a set difference: (A − B) ∪ (A − C).

Answer: Option (A) — A − (B ∩ C) = (A − B) ∪ (A − C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is A − (B ∪ C), the identity with the connective swapped — the intended trap.
  • (C) is generally different: with A = {1}, B = {2}, C = {1} it gives {2} while the correct set is {1}.
  • (D) is contained in B, whereas A − (B ∩ C) is contained in A; the two coincide only in special cases.

Common misconception. Students remember “difference distributes over intersection” and keep the intersection. The connective must flip: over ∩ you get ∪, and over ∪ you get ∩.

Faster method. Test with a two-element example before committing; the wrong identity usually dies at once.

Takeaway. A − (B ∩ C) = (A−B) ∪ (A−C) and A − (B ∪ C) = (A−B) ∩ (A−C).

DM-ST1-Q09MCQhard

Here A ⊕ B = (A ∪ B) − (A ∩ B) denotes symmetric difference. Which one of the following identities is NOT true in general?

  1. A ⊕ B = (A ∪ B) − (A ∩ B)
  2. A ⊕ (B ∩ C) = (A ⊕ B) ∩ (A ⊕ C)
  3. (A ⊕ B) ⊕ B = A
  4. A ⊕ ∅ = A
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Symmetric difference makes P(U) into an elementary abelian 2-group: it is associative, commutative, has identity ∅, and every set is its own inverse. It does not distribute over ∩.

Step by step.

  1. Test (B) with A = {1, 2}, B = {1}, C = {2}.
  2. Left side: B ∩ C = ∅, so A ⊕ ∅ = {1, 2}.
  3. Right side: A ⊕ B = {2} and A ⊕ C = {1}, so the intersection is {2} ∩ {1} = ∅.
  4. {1, 2} ≠ ∅, so (B) fails.
  5. The other three follow from the group laws: (A) is the definition, (C) uses associativity with B ⊕ B = ∅, and (D) is the identity law.

Answer: Option (B) — symmetric difference does not distribute over intersection.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the defining formula, so it holds by construction.
  • (C) holds because (A ⊕ B) ⊕ B = A ⊕ (B ⊕ B) = A ⊕ ∅ = A.
  • (D) holds because ∅ adds no elements and removes none.

Common misconception. Because ⊕ behaves like addition mod 2, candidates expect it to distribute like multiplication over addition. Here the operation that distributes is ∩ over ∪ — not ⊕ over ∩.

Faster method. For an identity question, hunt a counterexample on a two-element universe first: {1}, {2}, {1,2}, ∅ is enough to kill most false set identities.

Takeaway. ⊕ is a group operation (associative, commutative, self-inverse), not a ring multiplication; do not assume distributivity.

DM-ST1-Q18MSQeasy

For subsets of a universal set U, which of the following identities hold for all A, B, C? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (A ∩ B)′ = A′ ∪ B′
  2. (A ∪ B ∪ C)′ = A′ ∩ B′ ∩ C′
  3. A − B = A ∩ B′
  4. (A − B)′ = A′ − B′
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. De Morgan generalises to any finite number of sets, and set difference is intersection with a complement. Complementing a difference is where the naive guess goes wrong.

Step by step.

  1. (A) De Morgan for two sets: true.
  2. (B) De Morgan for three sets, obtained by applying the two-set law twice: (A ∪ B ∪ C)′ = ((A ∪ B) ∪ C)′ = (A ∪ B)′ ∩ C′ = A′ ∩ B′ ∩ C′. True.
  3. (C) x ∈ A − B ⇔ x ∈ A ∧ x ∉ B ⇔ x ∈ A ∩ B′. True.
  4. (D) is false: take U = {1, 2}, A = B = {1}. Then A − B = ∅, so (A − B)′ = {1, 2}, while A′ − B′ = ∅ − ∅ = ∅.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) has the correct identity (A − B)′ = A′ ∪ B, which is neither A′ − B′ nor A′ ∪ B′.

Common misconception. Candidates treat ′ as distributing over − the way it distributes over ∪ and ∩. Convert the difference to A ∩ B′ first, then apply De Morgan properly.

Faster method. Translate every difference into an intersection with a complement; De Morgan then applies mechanically.

Takeaway. (A − B)′ = A′ ∪ B — worth memorising, since it appears often in GATE set-algebra options.

DM-ST1-Q23NATeasy

In a universal set U with |U| = 40, let |A| = 25, |B| = 18 and |A ∩ B| = 10. How many elements lie in A′ ∩ B′?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. By De Morgan, A′ ∩ B′ = (A ∪ B)′. So the question asks for the elements in neither A nor B: compute the union, then subtract from |U|.

Step by step.

  1. |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B| = 25 + 18 − 10 = 33.
  2. By De Morgan, A′ ∩ B′ = (A ∪ B)′, whose size is |U| − |A ∪ B|.
  3. 40 − 33 = 7.

Answer: Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Two frequent slips: computing A′ ∪ B′ by mistake (that is (A ∩ B)′, size 30), or forgetting to subtract the intersection when forming the union (giving 40 − 43 < 0).

Faster method. Elements in neither = |U| − |A| − |B| + |A ∩ B|. Substitute directly: 40 − 25 − 18 + 10 = 7.

Takeaway. “In neither” is a complement of a union: |U| − |A ∪ B|.

Inclusion-exclusion 10
DM-ST1-Q10MCQeasy

Let |A| = 20, |B| = 15 and |A ∩ B| = 6. Then |A ∪ B| equals

  1. 29
  2. 35
  3. 41
  4. 9
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two-set inclusion–exclusion principle: |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|. The shared elements are counted twice by the sum and must be removed once.

Step by step.

  1. Sum the sizes: 20 + 15 = 35.
  2. The 6 elements of the intersection have now been counted twice, so subtract one copy: 35 − 6 = 29.
  3. So |A ∪ B| = 29, which is consistent with |A ∪ B| ≤ |A| + |B|.

Answer: Option (A) — 29.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 35 adds the sizes without correcting the double count of the intersection.
  • (C) 41 adds the intersection instead of subtracting it.
  • (D) 9 subtracts the whole of B from A, which answers a different question.

Common misconception. Forgetting the correction term is the single most common inclusion–exclusion error; check that the answer lies between max(|A|,|B|) and |A|+|B|.

Faster method. Plug straight into the formula, then sanity-check the bounds.

Takeaway. |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|, equivalently |A ∩ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∪ B|.

DM-ST2-Q10MCQeasy

In a group of 40 students, 25 play cricket, 20 play football and 10 play both. How many play neither game?

  1. 0
  2. 5
  3. 10
  4. 15
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two-set inclusion-exclusion gives the size of the union; the answer is the complement of that union inside the group.

Step by step.

  1. |C ∪ F| = |C| + |F| − |C ∩ F| = 25 + 20 − 10 = 35.
  2. Students playing neither = 40 − 35 = 5.

Answer: Option (B) — 5.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 0 would mean the union fills the group, i.e. |C ∪ F| = 40, which it does not.
  • (C) 10 is the size of the overlap, not of the complement.
  • (D) 15 = 25 − 10, the number playing cricket only.

Common misconception. Adding 25 + 20 = 45 and concluding that 5 students were double counted is correct arithmetic but answers a different question. The overlap is given as 10, not 5.

Faster method. Union first, complement second — always in that order for “neither” questions.

Takeaway. “Neither” = total − union, and the union needs inclusion-exclusion.

DM-ST2-Q11MCQmedium

How many integers n with 1 ≤ n ≤ 200 are divisible by exactly one of 3 and 5?

  1. 53
  2. 66
  3. 80
  4. 93
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Multiples of 3 form a set, multiples of 5 another; “exactly one” means the symmetric difference, counted as |A| + |B| − 2|A ∩ B| with A ∩ B = multiples of 15.

Step by step.

  1. ⌊200/3⌋ = 66 multiples of 3.
  2. ⌊200/5⌋ = 40 multiples of 5.
  3. ⌊200/15⌋ = 13 multiples of both.
  4. Exactly one: (66 − 13) + (40 − 13) = 53 + 27 = 80.

Answer: Option (C) — 80.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 53 counts only the multiples of 3 that are not multiples of 5.
  • (B) 66 is the total number of multiples of 3, including the shared ones.
  • (D) 93 = 66 + 40 − 13 is the union — divisible by 3 or 5, which includes multiples of 15.

Common misconception. “Exactly one” is routinely read as “at least one”. The union counts multiples of 15 once; symmetric difference excludes them altogether.

Faster method. Exactly one of two = union − intersection = 93 − 13 = 80. Same number, one line less.

Takeaway. Exactly-one-of-two = |A ∪ B| − |A ∩ B|.

DM-ST2-Q12MCQhard

How many integers n with 1 ≤ n ≤ 500 are divisible by exactly two of the numbers 2, 3 and 5?

  1. 102
  2. 118
  3. 134
  4. 150
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Sum the pairwise intersections, then subtract three times the triple intersection, because an element in all three sets is counted in all three pairwise terms but must not be counted at all.

Step by step.

  1. |2 ∧ 3| = ⌊500/6⌋ = 83, |2 ∧ 5| = ⌊500/10⌋ = 50, |3 ∧ 5| = ⌊500/15⌋ = 33.
  2. Sum of pairwise intersections: 83 + 50 + 33 = 166.
  3. |2 ∧ 3 ∧ 5| = ⌊500/30⌋ = 16; these 16 elements appear in all three pairwise terms and must be removed.
  4. Exactly two = 166 − 3·16 = 166 − 48 = 118.

Answer: Option (B) — 118.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 102 subtracts the triple intersection twice instead of three times.
  • (C) 134 subtracts the triple intersection only once.
  • (D) 150 is close to the sum of pairwise intersections with the triple intersection ignored in a different way; it does not correspond to “exactly two”.

Common misconception. The coefficient of the triple intersection is 3, not 1 or 2. Each element divisible by 30 lies in all three pairwise intersections, so it must be cancelled three times.

Faster method. Exactly-two = (sum of pairwise intersections) − 3·(triple). For exactly-one it is (sum of singles) − 2·(sum of pairwise) + 3·(triple).

Takeaway. Learn the coefficients: exactly one uses 1, −2, 3; exactly two uses 0, 1, −3.

DM-ST2-Q19MSQmedium

For finite sets A, B, C, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. |A ∪ B| ≥ max(|A|, |B|)
  2. |A ∩ B| ≤ min(|A|, |B|)
  3. |A − B| = |A| − |B|
  4. |A ∪ B ∪ C| ≥ |A| + |B| + |C| − |A ∩ B| − |B ∩ C| − |A ∩ C|
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Monotonicity gives the first two bounds; the third-set inequality follows from the exact three-set formula by dropping the non-negative triple term.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A ⊆ A ∪ B and B ⊆ A ∪ B, so the union is at least as large as each part. TRUE.
  2. (B) A ∩ B ⊆ A and A ∩ B ⊆ B. TRUE.
  3. (C) Take |A| = 3, |B| = 5 disjoint; |A − B| = 3 but 3 − 5 = −2. FALSE.
  4. (D) The exact identity has + |A ∩ B ∩ C| at the end; dropping a non-negative term can only lower the right side, so the inequality holds. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are correct.

Common misconception. (C) is believed because “difference” suggests subtraction of sizes. The correct identity is |A − B| = |A| − |A ∩ B|, and |A| − |B| can even be negative.

Faster method. Check (C) on disjoint sets, where it is most obviously wrong; that settles it without any algebra.

Takeaway. Sizes are monotone under ⊆, and the three-set formula becomes an inequality when the last term is dropped.

DM-ST2-Q24NATmedium

In a survey of 100 people, 60 read newspaper A, 50 read B, 40 read C, 25 read A and B, 20 read B and C, 15 read A and C, and 10 read all three. How many read exactly one newspaper?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Exactly one = (sum of the three sizes) − 2·(sum of the three pairwise intersections) + 3·(triple intersection). Each person reading k papers must be counted exactly once.

Step by step.

  1. Sum of singles: 60 + 50 + 40 = 150.
  2. Sum of pairwise intersections: 25 + 20 + 15 = 60.
  3. Triple intersection: 10.
  4. Exactly one = 150 − 2·60 + 3·10 = 150 − 120 + 30 = 60.

Answer: 60

Common misconception. Using 150 − 60 + 10 = 100, which is the union (and here also the whole population). Exactly-one needs the coefficients 1, −2, +3, not 1, −1, +1.

Faster method. Compute the union first (150 − 60 + 10 = 100), then sanity-check your exactly-one figure against it — exactly one must be smaller than the union unless nobody reads two papers.

Takeaway. Exactly-one coefficients are 1, −2, +3; the union's are 1, −1, +1.

DM-ST1-Q11MCQmedium

Let |A| = |B| = |C| = 10, let every pairwise intersection have size 4, and let |A ∩ B ∩ C| = 2. Then |A ∪ B ∪ C| equals

  1. 20
  2. 18
  3. 22
  4. 26
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three-set inclusion–exclusion: |A ∪ B ∪ C| = Σ|A| − Σ|pairwise ∩| + |A ∩ B ∩ C|. The pairwise terms are subtracted three times and the triple term added once.

Step by step.

  1. Sum of single sizes: 10 + 10 + 10 = 30.
  2. Sum of pairwise intersections: 4 + 4 + 4 = 12. Subtract: 30 − 12 = 18.
  3. Add the triple intersection back once: 18 + 2 = 20.
  4. So |A ∪ B ∪ C| = 20.

Answer: Option (A) — 20.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 18 stops after the pairwise subtraction and never adds the triple back.
  • (C) 22 adds a pairwise value (4) instead of the triple value (2).
  • (D) 26 subtracts the pairwise term only once, as if there were a single intersection.

Common misconception. The alternating signs are not decoration: each element of the triple intersection is counted 3 times, removed 3 times, and must be restored exactly once.

Faster method. Write the three lines +Σ singles −Σ pairs + triple before substituting any numbers.

Takeaway. The inclusion–exclusion signs alternate with the number of sets intersected.

DM-ST1-Q12MCQhard

How many integers n with 1 ≤ n ≤ 1000 are divisible by at least one of 2, 3, 5?

  1. 734
  2. 667
  3. 266
  4. 733
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count multiples with inclusion–exclusion, using least common multiples for the intersections: ⌊1000/lcm⌋ multiples of each combination.

Step by step.

  1. Singles: ⌊1000/2⌋ = 500, ⌊1000/3⌋ = 333, ⌊1000/5⌋ = 200; sum = 1033.
  2. Pairs: lcm(2,3) = 6 → 166; lcm(2,5) = 10 → 100; lcm(3,5) = 15 → 66; sum = 332.
  3. Triple: lcm(2,3,5) = 30 → 33.
  4. Result: 1033 − 332 + 33 = 734.

Answer: Option (A) — 734 integers.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 667 counts multiples of 2 or 3 only (500 + 333 − 166) and ignores divisibility by 5.
  • (C) 266 is the complement — the integers divisible by none of 2, 3, 5 (1000 − 734). It answers the opposite question.
  • (D) 733 drops the triple term, an off-by-one-class error.

Common misconception. Two traps: subtracting a product instead of an lcm (2·3 = 6 happens to be right here, but 2·5 and 3·5 must also be lcms, not products), and answering the complement when the question says “at least one”.

Faster method. For divisibility by primes, the lcm of a subset is just their product — so compute ⌊1000/p⌋ for each product of distinct primes.

Takeaway. |A∪B∪C| = Σ|A| − Σ|pairs| + |triple|, with every intersection counted by an lcm.

DM-ST1-Q19MSQmedium

For finite sets, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|
  2. |A ∪ B ∪ C| = |A| + |B| + |C| − |A∩B| − |B∩C| − |A∩C| + |A∩B∩C|
  3. If |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B|, then A ∩ B = ∅
  4. |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| for all sets A, B
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Inclusion–exclusion is an identity for finite sets, and its correction term is exactly what measures overlap. Equality of the two sides is therefore a test for disjointness.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the two-set inclusion–exclusion identity: true.
  2. (B) is the three-set form; each element of the triple intersection is counted 3 times, removed 3 times, then restored once. True.
  3. (C) From (A), |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|. The hypothesis forces |A ∩ B| = 0, i.e. A ∩ B = ∅. True.
  4. (D) is false whenever the sets overlap: with A = B = {1} the left side is 1 and the right side is 2.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the additive rule that holds only for disjoint sets — the single most common misuse of the sum rule.

Common misconception. The sum rule |A| + |B| counts every element of A ∩ B twice. Without disjointness it is an over-count, not an identity.

Faster method. Remember one sentence: inclusion–exclusion is the sum rule plus the correction for overlap.

Takeaway. |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| holds exactly when A and B are disjoint.

DM-ST1-Q24NATeasy

In a class of 60 students, 30 like tea, 25 like coffee and 20 like milk. Also 12 like tea and coffee, 10 like coffee and milk, 8 like tea and milk, and 5 like all three. How many students like at least one of the three drinks?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three-set inclusion–exclusion: |T ∪ C ∪ M| = Σ|singles| − Σ|pairs| + |triple|.

Step by step.

  1. Sum of singles: 30 + 25 + 20 = 75.
  2. Sum of pairwise intersections: 12 + 10 + 8 = 30. Subtract: 75 − 30 = 45.
  3. Add the triple back: 45 + 5 = 50.
  4. So 50 students like at least one drink; the other 10 like none.

Answer: Answer: 50.

Common misconception. Students often stop at 75 − 30 = 45. Each student who likes all three has been counted 3 times, removed 3 times, and must be restored once — the +5 is not optional.

Faster method. Write the three rows before substituting: singles +, pairs −, triple +.

Takeaway. The same identity answers “at least one” directly; “exactly one” needs the region formulas, which are a different computation.

Partitions & Cartesian products 10
DM-ST1-Q13MCQeasy

Let |A| = 5 and |B| = 4. How many elements does the Cartesian product A × B have?

  1. 20
  2. 9
  3. 25
  4. 32
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A × B = {(a, b) : a ∈ A, b ∈ B} — the set of ordered pairs. Each of the |A| first coordinates pairs with each of the |B| second coordinates.

Step by step.

  1. Fix the first coordinate: there are 5 choices, and for each of them there are 4 choices of second coordinate.
  2. By the product rule the number of ordered pairs is 5 × 4 = 20.
  3. Order matters, so (a, b) and (b, a) are different objects; A × B and B × A both have 20 elements here.

Answer: Option (A) — 20 elements.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 9 adds the sizes, which would count A ∪ B if the sets were disjoint.
  • (C) 25 squares the first size and ignores B entirely.
  • (D) 32 = 2⁵ counts subsets of A, not pairs.

Common misconception. Mixing up union and Cartesian product is the usual error. A union of disjoint sets is additive; a product is multiplicative.

Faster method. |A × B| = |A|·|B|, and for a finite product of k sets multiply all k sizes.

Takeaway. |A × B| = |A||B|; consequently |P(A × B)| = 2^(|A|·|B|).

DM-ST2-Q13MCQeasy

Let A = {1, 2, 3}. How many elements does the set P(A) × {x, y} have?

  1. 6
  2. 8
  3. 12
  4. 16
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The product rule multiplies cardinalities, and |P(A)| = 2^{|A|}.

Step by step.

  1. |A| = 3, so |P(A)| = 2³ = 8.
  2. The second factor has 2 elements.
  3. Therefore |P(A) × {x, y}| = 8 · 2 = 16.

Answer: Option (D) — 16.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 6 = 3 · 2 uses |A| instead of |P(A)|.
  • (B) 8 is |P(A)| alone, forgetting the second factor.
  • (C) 12 = 8 + 4 or 3 · 4: neither the product rule nor a correct sum.

Common misconception. Reading P(A) as A. The power set has 2³ = 8 members, not 3.

Faster method. Cartesian product sizes always multiply; get the size of each factor right first.

Takeaway. A product of sets is counted by the product rule, with |P(A)| = 2^{|A|}.

DM-ST2-Q14MCQmedium

How many partitions of {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} have exactly three blocks?

  1. 15
  2. 31
  3. 45
  4. 90
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of partitions of an n-element set into exactly k blocks is the Stirling number of the second kind S(n, k), computed by S(n,k) = k·S(n−1,k) + S(n−1,k−1).

Step by step.

  1. Base values: S(5, 2) = 2⁴ − 1 = 15 and S(5, 3) = 25.
  2. Recurrence: S(6, 3) = 3·S(5, 3) + S(5, 2) = 3·25 + 15.
  3. = 75 + 15 = 90.

Answer: Option (D) — 90.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 15 = S(5, 2), the two-block count for a five-element set.
  • (B) 31 is S(6, 4) + S(6, 3) partially added — an arithmetic slip, not a valid count.
  • (C) 45 is half of 90 and comes from treating the three blocks as distinguishable.

Common misconception. Counting ordered triples of blocks instead of partitions overcounts by 3! = 6 — the blocks of a partition are an unordered collection.

Faster method. Learn the Stirling triangle: row 6 is 1, 31, 90, 65, 15, 1. The third entry is 90.

Takeaway. Exactly k blocks = S(n,k); total partitions = Bell number B(n).

DM-ST2-Q15MCQhard

How many ordered pairs (A, B) of subsets of {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} satisfy A ∩ B = ∅?

  1. 125
  2. 243
  3. 252
  4. 1024
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Independently for each element there are three legal placements — in A only, in B only, or in neither — and disjointness forbids the fourth.

Step by step.

  1. Fix an element x of the 5-element ground set.
  2. Disjointness allows exactly three placements: x ∈ A, x ∈ B, or x ∉ A ∪ B. The placement x ∈ A ∩ B is forbidden.
  3. Choices for different elements are independent, so the count is 3⁵ = 243.

Answer: Option (B) — 243.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 125 = 5³ mixes the roles of the base and the exponent.
  • (C) 252 = C(10, 5), which counts something entirely different.
  • (D) 1024 = 2^{10} = 4⁵ counts all pairs (A, B) with no disjointness condition.

Common misconception. Starting from 4⁵ = 1024 and trying to subtract the non-disjoint pairs is messy and error-prone; counting the three allowed placements per element is immediate.

Faster method. Two sets with a disjointness constraint = ternary choice per element = 3ⁿ. Drop the constraint and it becomes 4ⁿ.

Takeaway. Constraints that exclude one combination per element turn a 4ⁿ count into a 3ⁿ count.

DM-ST2-Q20MSQmedium

Which of the following claims about products, partitions and equivalence relations are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (A × B) ∪ (C × D) = (A ∪ C) × (B ∪ D) for all sets A, B, C, D.
  2. For non-empty sets A and B, A × B = B × A if and only if A = B.
  3. Every partition of a set A determines an equivalence relation on A.
  4. The number of partitions of a 5-element set is 52.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Products are not distributive over unions in the way it looks; a partition and an equivalence relation are two views of the same data; the Bell numbers begin 1, 1, 2, 5, 15, 52.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Take A = {1}, B = {2}, C = {2}, D = {1}. Left side is {(1,2), (2,1)}, right side is {1,2} × {1,2}, which is larger. FALSE.
  2. (B) If A × B = B × A then the sets of first coordinates agree, so A = B; the converse is immediate. TRUE.
  3. (C) Declare x ~ y exactly when x and y lie in the same block; reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity follow at once. TRUE.
  4. (D) B(5) = S(5,1) + S(5,2) + S(5,3) + S(5,4) + S(5,5) = 1 + 15 + 25 + 10 + 1 = 52. TRUE.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D) are correct.

Common misconception. (A) looks like distributivity and is often accepted. A union of two products is much smaller than the product of the two unions; the two sides coincide only in degenerate cases.

Faster method. For (A), test single-element sets with the coordinates crossed, as above; one counterexample settles it.

Takeaway. A partition and an equivalence relation are the same object seen twice; products of unions are not unions of products.

DM-ST2-Q25NATeasy

How many partitions does the set {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The total number of partitions of an n-element set is the Bell number B(n), the sum of the Stirling numbers of the second kind over all block counts.

Step by step.

  1. Split by the number of blocks k: S(5,1) = 1, S(5,2) = 15, S(5,3) = 25, S(5,4) = 10, S(5,5) = 1.
  2. Add: 1 + 15 + 25 + 10 + 1 = 52.

Answer: 52

Common misconception. Counting only the “interesting” partitions, or reporting S(5,2) = 15. The Bell number sums every possible number of blocks, including the one-block and five-block partitions.

Faster method. Recall the Bell ladder 1, 1, 2, 5, 15, 52 for n = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5; the fifth entry is the answer.

Takeaway. Bell numbers: 1, 1, 2, 5, 15, 52, 203 — worth memorising to B(6).

DM-ST1-Q14MCQmedium

How many partitions does the set {1, 2, 3, 4} have?

  1. 15
  2. 14
  3. 24
  4. 16
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A partition is a set of non-empty, pairwise disjoint blocks whose union is the whole set. Count them by the number of blocks using the Stirling numbers of the second kind S(n, k); the total is the Bell number B(n) = Σₖ S(n, k).

Step by step.

  1. One block: {1234} — 1 partition, S(4,1) = 1.
  2. Two blocks: S(4,2) = 2³ − 1 = 7 (split into a non-empty part and its non-empty complement, then halve: (2⁴ − 2)/2 = 7).
  3. Three blocks: choose the pair that shares a block, C(4,2) = 6, so S(4,3) = 6.
  4. Four blocks: all singletons — 1 partition, S(4,4) = 1.
  5. Total 1 + 7 + 6 + 1 = 15.

Answer: Option (A) — 15 partitions.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 14 usually comes from forgetting one of the 7 two-block partitions.
  • (C) 24 = 4! counts orderings of the four elements; a partition has no order inside a block and no order among blocks.
  • (D) 16 = 2⁴ counts subsets of the four elements, not partitions.

Common misconception. Order does not exist in a partition: {{1,2},{3,4}} and {{3,4},{1,2}} are the same partition. Counting ordered splits inflates the answer by a factor of k! for k blocks.

Faster method. Know the Bell ladder: B(0..5) = 1, 1, 2, 5, 15, 52. For four elements the answer is 15 without any computation.

Takeaway. Partitions of an n-element set are counted by Bell numbers; equivalence relations on the set are in bijection with them, so there are also 15 equivalence relations on 4 elements.

DM-ST1-Q15MCQhard

How many ordered pairs (A, B) of non-empty sets satisfy A ∪ B = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} and A ∩ B = ∅?

  1. 62
  2. 64
  3. 31
  4. 63
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A two-block partition is a function assigning each element to one of two blocks. Ordered pairs count each assignment separately; unordered partitions identify the two orders.

Step by step.

  1. Each of the 6 elements must go to A or to B, giving 2⁶ = 64 assignments.
  2. The assignments A = ∅, B = {1,…,6} and A = {1,…,6}, B = ∅ are forbidden because both sets must be non-empty.
  3. Hence 64 − 2 = 62 ordered pairs.

Answer: Option (A) — 62 ordered pairs.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 64 ignores the requirement that neither set is empty.
  • (C) 31 = 62/2 counts unordered partitions into two blocks; here the pairs are ordered, so (A, B) and (B, A) are distinct.
  • (D) 63 removes only one of the two empty cases.

Common misconception. The word ordered is doing the work. Reading it as “two blocks” silently halves the answer — the commonest failure on this question type.

Faster method. Ordered splits into k labelled non-empty blocks: inclusion–exclusion gives kⁿ − C(k,1)(k−1)ⁿ + …. For k = 2 that is 2ⁿ − 2.

Takeaway. Ordered two-block splits = 2ⁿ − 2; unordered partitions into two blocks = 2ⁿ⁻¹ − 1 = S(n, 2).

DM-ST1-Q20MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about sets, partitions and Cartesian products are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If A × B = B × A, then A = B, unless one of A, B is empty.
  2. A partition of a set S is a collection of non-empty, pairwise disjoint subsets of S whose union is S.
  3. A 3-element set has exactly 6 partitions.
  4. For finite sets A and B, |A × B| = |A| · |B|.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Cartesian products are ordered, so commutativity forces equality of the factors; partitions are unordered collections of blocks, counted by the Bell numbers.

Step by step.

  1. (A) If both sets are non-empty, pick b₀ ∈ B. For any a ∈ A, (a, b₀) ∈ A × B = B × A, so a ∈ B; hence A ⊆ B, and symmetrically B ⊆ A. True. (The empty case is genuine: ∅ × B = ∅ = B × ∅ even for B ≠ ∅.)
  2. (B) is the definition of a partition: true.
  3. (C) is false. {1,2,3} has 5 partitions: one block; three two-block partitions {{1},{2,3}}, {{2},{1,3}}, {{3},{1,2}}; and three singletons.
  4. (D) is the product rule for finite sets: true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) confuses partitions (5) with permutations (6) of three elements, or with the number of two-block partitions plus something else.

Common misconception. The empty-set exception in (A) is easy to forget: with A = ∅ both products are empty regardless of B. GATE options that include a carefully worded exception like this are usually the true ones.

Faster method. Bell numbers again: B(1)=1, B(2)=2, B(3)=5, B(4)=15. Any option quoting 6 for three elements is false.

Takeaway. A × B = B × A ⇔ A = B (or one factor is empty); partitions are counted by Bell numbers, not by factorials.

DM-ST1-Q25NATeasy

How many partitions does the set {1, 2, 3} have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A partition is a set of non-empty, pairwise disjoint blocks covering the set. Count by the number of blocks using S(n, k), the Stirling numbers of the second kind.

Step by step.

  1. One block: {{1,2,3}} — 1.
  2. Two blocks: one block is a 2-element subset, the other its complement — C(3,2) = 3 partitions.
  3. Three blocks: {{1},{2},{3}} — 1.
  4. Total 1 + 3 + 1 = 5.

Answer: Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Answering 6 counts the permutations of three elements, or counts the two-block splits as ordered pairs (there are 6 of those, but they pair up into 3 partitions). Order is not part of a partition.

Faster method. Bell ladder: B(1)=1, B(2)=2, B(3)=5, B(4)=15, B(5)=52.

Takeaway. The 5 partitions of a 3-element set are in bijection with the 5 equivalence relations on it.

Unit 4 — Relations 50 solved questions
Relation properties 9
DM-REL1-Q01MCQeasy

Let A = {a, b, c} and R = {(a,a), (b,b), (c,c), (a,b), (b,a)}. Which statement about R is TRUE?

  1. R is reflexive and symmetric but not transitive.
  2. R is an equivalence relation with exactly two equivalence classes.
  3. R is antisymmetric.
  4. R is a partial order on A.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An equivalence relation is exactly a relation that is reflexive, symmetric and transitive; its classes are the blocks it cuts the set into.

Step by step.

  1. Reflexive: (a,a), (b,b), (c,c) are all present.
  2. Symmetric: the only off-diagonal pairs are (a,b) and (b,a), which come as a pair.
  3. Transitive: the only compositions to check are (a,b) ∘ (b,a) = (a,a) and (b,a) ∘ (a,b) = (b,b), and both are in R.
  4. So R is an equivalence relation. Its classes are {a, b} and {c} — exactly two.

Answer: Option (B) — two equivalence classes.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails: the relation is transitive, as the two compositions above show.
  • (C) fails: (a,b) and (b,a) are both in R with a ≠ b, which is precisely what antisymmetry forbids.
  • (D) fails for the same reason: a partial order must be antisymmetric, and R is not.

Common misconception. Students check transitivity only on pairs of the form (x,y), (y,z) that are both off-diagonal, and miss the compositions that involve a reversed pair.

Faster method. A relation that is symmetric and antisymmetric can only contain diagonal pairs. Since this one has an off-diagonal pair, it cannot be antisymmetric — which kills (C) and (D) at once.

Takeaway. Symmetric and antisymmetric are not opposites; a relation can be both, but only if it contains no off-diagonal pair at all.

DM-REL2-Q01MCQeasy

Let A = {1, 2, 3} and R = {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (1,2)}. Which statement about R is TRUE?

  1. R is reflexive and antisymmetric but not transitive.
  2. R is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive.
  3. R is symmetric.
  4. R is an equivalence relation on A.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Injectivity asks whether two different inputs can share an output; surjectivity asks whether every element of the codomain is hit at all. On infinite domains the two are independent.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose f(a) = f(b), i.e. 2a = 2b. Dividing by 2 gives a = b, so f is injective.
  2. Now take the codomain element 3. If 2x = 3 then x = 3/2, which is not an integer, so 3 has no preimage in ℤ.
  3. More generally only even integers are values of f, so the image is 2ℤ ⊊ ℤ.
  4. Hence f is injective and not surjective.

Answer: Option (B) — injective, not surjective.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails: odd integers such as 3 are never attained, so f is not onto.
  • (C) fails: doubling is one-to-one, so it is not "onto but not one-to-one".
  • (D) fails: injectivity is already proved in step 1.

Common misconception. Students transfer the finite-set rule "injective ⇔ surjective" to ℤ. That equivalence needs the domain and codomain to be finite of equal size; on infinite sets x ↦ 2x is injective but leaves half the codomain untouched.

Faster method. Check surjectivity first with a single odd target such as 3. One missing preimage settles the question, and injectivity of a linear map is immediate from the slope.

Takeaway. On infinite sets, injective does not imply surjective: the classic witness is f(x) = 2x on ℤ.

DM-REL2-Q02MCQmedium

Let A = {1, 2, 3} and R = {(1,2), (2,1), (1,3), (3,1), (2,3), (3,2)}. Which statement about R is TRUE?

  1. R is reflexive and symmetric.
  2. R is symmetric but not transitive.
  3. R is transitive but not symmetric.
  4. R is an equivalence relation on A.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function into ℤ⁺ is surjective when every positive integer occurs as a value. For a candidate, the quickest test is to try to hit a small target such as 1.

Step by step.

  1. (A) n + 1 ≥ 2 for every n ≥ 1, so 1 is never attained. Not surjective.
  2. (B) 2n is even, so 1 (and every odd number) is missed. Not surjective.
  3. (C) ⌈n/2⌉ takes the values 1, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, … for n = 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, …. Every k ∈ ℤ⁺ is attained, for instance at n = 2k − 1. Surjective.
  4. (D) n² attains only perfect squares, so 2 is missed. Not surjective.

Answer: Option (C) — f(n) = ⌈n/2⌉.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) are strictly increasing with image bounded below by 2 and by the even numbers respectively.
  • (D) misses every non-square.

Common misconception. Assuming a "spreading out" function such as 2n covers everything because it grows. Growth is irrelevant to surjectivity; what matters is which values are hit.

Faster method. For each option, ask "can it produce 1?" Only (C) can.

Takeaway. ⌈n/2⌉ is the standard example of a surjective non-injective map on ℤ⁺.

DM-REL2-Q03MSQeasy

For a relation R on a set A, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If R is both symmetric and antisymmetric, then R ⊆ {(a,a) : a ∈ A}.
  2. Every asymmetric relation is antisymmetric.
  3. Every antisymmetric relation is asymmetric.
  4. If R is irreflexive and transitive, then R ∩ R⁻¹ = ∅.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a function between finite sets of equal size, injective, surjective and bijective are the same condition. That is the pigeonhole principle in disguise.

Step by step.

  1. If f is injective, its n inputs give n distinct outputs inside an n-element codomain, so every element of B is used: f is also surjective, hence bijective. (A) is TRUE.
  2. If f is surjective, all n elements of B are hit; n hits from n inputs with no repeats is impossible unless each output is hit exactly once. So f is injective, hence bijective. (B) is TRUE.
  3. (C) is FALSE: with A = B = {1, 2}, the constant map f ≡ 1 is a function but not bijective.
  4. A bijection has an inverse: f⁻¹(y) is the unique x with f(x) = y. (D) is TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the trap: existence of a function is not existence of a bijection. Most maps between equal finite sets are not bijections.

Common misconception. Reading "injective implies bijective" as a universal law. It holds only because |A| = |B| is finite; drop either hypothesis and it fails.

Faster method. Memorise the finite equal-cardinality triangle: injective ⇔ surjective ⇔ bijective. Then (A) and (B) are immediate and (C) is obviously too strong.

Takeaway. On finite sets of equal size, one-to-one and onto are the same test; on infinite sets they are not.

DM-REL2-Q04MCQhard

Let R be a relation on a finite set A that is irreflexive and transitive. Which of the following must be TRUE?

  1. R is symmetric.
  2. R is asymmetric.
  3. R ∪ I_A is symmetric.
  4. R is reflexive.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Injectivity is destroyed by a single collision f(a) = f(b) with a ≠ b. Surjectivity onto ℝ is destroyed by showing the range is bounded.

Step by step.

  1. Collision: f(2) = 2/5 and f(1/2) = (1/2)/(5/4) = 2/5. Since 2 ≠ 1/2, f is not injective.
  2. In general f(x) = f(1/x) for x ≠ 0, so every value away from 0 is taken at least twice.
  3. Range: by AM–GM, x² + 1 ≥ 2|x|, so |f(x)| ≤ 1/2, with equality at x = ±1.
  4. Values such as 1 lie outside [−1/2, 1/2], so f is not surjective onto ℝ.
  5. Therefore f is neither injective nor surjective.

Answer: Option (C) — neither injective nor surjective.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails because of the collision f(2) = f(1/2).
  • (B) fails because the range is confined to [−1/2, 1/2].
  • (D) fails on both counts.

Common misconception. Testing injectivity only with integers, or assuming a bounded, symmetric-looking curve must still be one-to-one. The symmetry f(x) = f(1/x) is exactly what breaks injectivity.

Faster method. Boundedness settles surjectivity in one line. For injectivity, look for symmetry: any f with f(x) = f(1/x) or f(x) = f(−x) is immediately non-injective.

Takeaway. A function can fail injectivity (symmetry) and surjectivity (bounded range) at the same time; check each property separately.

DM-REL2-Q05NATmedium

How many relations on the set A = {1, 2, 3} are symmetric and transitive but not reflexive?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Solving f(x) = c is solving a quadratic; the number of solutions is the number of distinct real roots, which the discriminant decides.

Step by step.

  1. Set x² − 4x + 7 = 3.
  2. Rearrange: x² − 4x + 4 = 0.
  3. Factor: (x − 2)² = 0, so x = 2 is a double root.
  4. The discriminant is 16 − 16 = 0, confirming exactly one distinct real solution.

Answer: 1

Common misconception. Counting the double root twice and answering 2. "How many real numbers x" asks for distinct solutions, so a repeated root counts once.

Faster method. Complete the square first: f(x) = (x−2)² + 3. Then f(x) = 3 ⇔ (x−2)² = 0, giving the answer at a glance and also showing the minimum value is 3.

Takeaway. Vertex form (x − h)² + k tells you both the range and how many preimages a value has: below k none, at k exactly one, above k two.

DM-REL1-Q02MCQmedium

Let A = {1, 2, 3, 4} and R = {(1,2), (2,3), (3,4)}. Let R⁺ be the transitive closure of R. How many ordered pairs does the symmetric closure of R⁺ contain?

  1. 6
  2. 10
  3. 12
  4. 16
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The transitive closure adds every pair (a,b) for which a path of length ≥ 1 runs from a to b; the symmetric closure then adds the reverse of every pair.

Step by step.

  1. Paths of length 1: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4).
  2. Paths of length 2: (1,3), (2,4).
  3. Paths of length 3: (1,4).
  4. So R⁺ has 3 + 2 + 1 = 6 pairs.
  5. No pair in R⁺ has the form (x,x), so the symmetric closure adds exactly one new pair per pair: 6 + 6 = 12.

Answer: Option (C) — 12 ordered pairs.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 6 is |R⁺| itself; the reverses are forgotten.
  • (B) 10 is |R⁺| plus four reverses, which happens if the length-3 path (1,4) is missed.
  • (D) 16 is |A × A|, which would need the closure to be the universal relation.

Common misconception. Two closures are routinely mixed up: the reflexive closure adds the diagonal pairs (x,x) (10 pairs here), while the symmetric closure adds the reverses (12 pairs here).

Faster method. A chain of n links gives n(n+1)/2 ordered pairs in its transitive closure; here 4·3/2 = 6, then double it.

Takeaway. Count the closure level by level (path length 1, 2, 3, …) before applying the second closure.

DM-REL1-Q03MCQhard

How many relations on a three-element set are both symmetric and antisymmetric?

  1. 8
  2. 16
  3. 27
  4. 64
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Symmetry forces (a,b) and (b,a) to travel together; antisymmetry forbids them from both being present when a ≠ b. Together they force every off-diagonal pair out.

Step by step.

  1. Let A = {1, 2, 3}. Take any a ≠ b with (a,b) ∈ R.
  2. Symmetry gives (b,a) ∈ R; antisymmetry then forces a = b, a contradiction.
  3. Hence R ⊆ {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3)}.
  4. Every subset of the three diagonal pairs is symmetric (no off-diagonal pair exists) and antisymmetric, so all 2³ = 8 subsets qualify.

Answer: Option (A) — 8 relations.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 16 = 2⁴ counts a relation on a two-element set, or treats one off-diagonal pair as free.
  • (C) 27 = 3³ is the number of antisymmetric relations on a three-element set on its own.
  • (D) 64 = 2⁶ is the number of symmetric relations on a three-element set on its own.

Common misconception. The pair of adjectives is read as "symmetric or antisymmetric". The question asks for both, and the two conditions together are far more restrictive than either alone.

Faster method. Symmetric ∧ antisymmetric ⟹ R ⊆ I_A, so the answer is always 2ⁿ for an n-element set.

Takeaway. Two extreme properties can intersect in a very small family — reason from the definitions rather than from counts of each separately.

DM-REL1-Q16MSQeasy

For relations on a finite set A, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A relation that is both symmetric and antisymmetric is contained in the identity relation.
  2. If R is symmetric then R⁻¹ = R.
  3. If R is reflexive and symmetric then R is transitive.
  4. If R is an equivalence relation then so is A × A − R.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each option is settled by applying the definition directly or by exhibiting one small counterexample.

Step by step.

  1. (A): if (a,b) ∈ R with a ≠ b, symmetry gives (b,a) ∈ R and antisymmetry then forces a = b — contradiction. So only diagonal pairs can occur. TRUE.
  2. (B): R⁻¹ = {(b,a) : (a,b) ∈ R}, and symmetry says (a,b) ∈ R ⇔ (b,a) ∈ R, so the two sets coincide. TRUE.
  3. (C): on A = {1,2,3} take R = I_A ∪ {(1,2), (2,1), (2,3), (3,2)}. It is reflexive and symmetric, yet (1,2), (2,3) ∈ R while (1,3) ∉ R. FALSE.
  4. (D): with |A| ≥ 2, A × A − R omits every diagonal pair, so it is not reflexive and hence not an equivalence relation. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) fails because reflexivity plus symmetry says nothing about paths of length two.
  • (D) fails because an equivalence relation must contain I_A, and the complement of a set containing I_A cannot contain I_A.

Common misconception. The complement of a nice relation is assumed to be nice. Complementing destroys reflexivity first, which kills (D) immediately.

Faster method. Check (D) by reflexivity alone — the fastest possible kill — then look for the standard "reflexive + symmetric but not transitive" counterexample for (C).

Takeaway. Symmetric ∧ antisymmetric forces containment in I_A; the complement of a reflexive relation is never reflexive.

Equivalence relations & classes 9
DM-REL1-Q04MCQeasy

On A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6} define aRb exactly when a ≡ b (mod 3). How many equivalence classes does R have?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Congruence modulo m is an equivalence relation, and its classes are the residue classes 0, 1, …, m−1.

Step by step.

  1. Residues of A modulo 3: 1 ≡ 4 ≡ 1, 2 ≡ 5 ≡ 2, 3 ≡ 6 ≡ 0.
  2. The classes are {1, 4}, {2, 5} and {3, 6}.
  3. There are three of them, indexed by the residues 0, 1, 2.

Answer: Option (B) — 3 equivalence classes.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 2 would be the answer for modulus 2.
  • (C) 4 counts the elements of the class {1, 4} as separate classes.
  • (D) 6 is |A|, the count for the identity relation.

Common misconception. Students count the number of distinct residues present among the elements (0, 1, 2 — three) but then sometimes report the modulus range 0…2 as four values, or forget that residue 0 occurs (at 3 and 6).

Faster method. The number of classes of congruence mod m on {1, …, n} is min(m, n) when the set is long enough to hit every residue; here 3.

Takeaway. Modulo m always yields exactly m classes on a set that covers all residues.

DM-REL2-Q06MCQeasy

On A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8} define aRb exactly when a ≡ b (mod 4). How many equivalence classes does R have, and how large is the class containing 6?

  1. 4 classes; the class of 6 has 2 elements.
  2. 4 classes; the class of 6 has 3 elements.
  3. 8 classes; the class of 6 has 1 element.
  4. 2 classes; the class of 6 has 4 elements.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. (f ∘ g)(x) = f(g(x)): the right-hand function acts first, and its output becomes the input of the left-hand function.

Step by step.

  1. (f ∘ g)(x) = f(g(x)) = f(x²).
  2. Substitute x² into f(u) = 2u + 3: f(x²) = 2x² + 3.

Answer: Option (B) — 2x² + 3.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is (g ∘ f)(x) = (2x + 3)², the composition in the other order.
  • (C) and (D) square the wrong coefficient or add the wrong term; neither follows from substituting x² into 2u + 3.

Common misconception. Applying f first. Composition is read right to left: in f ∘ g, g goes first.

Faster method. Write the outer function with a blank: 2( ) + 3, then drop g(x) into the blank.

Takeaway. f ∘ g ≠ g ∘ f in general; here the two orders give 2x² + 3 and 4x² + 12x + 9.

DM-REL2-Q07MCQeasy

Let A = {1, 2, 3, 4} and let R be the smallest equivalence relation on A containing the pair (1,3). How many ordered pairs does R contain?

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 8
  4. 16
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Injectivity of a composite forces injectivity of the first map applied, but says nothing about the second.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose f(a₁) = f(a₂). Applying g gives g(f(a₁)) = g(f(a₂)), i.e. (g∘f)(a₁) = (g∘f)(a₂).
  2. Injectivity of g ∘ f then gives a₁ = a₂. Hence f is injective. (A) holds.
  3. Counterexample for g: A = {1}, B = {p, q}, C = {r}, f(1) = p, g(p) = g(q) = r. Then g ∘ f is injective but g is not, so (B) fails.
  4. Surjectivity is untouched: with A = B = C = {1} and identity maps everything is injective and surjective; with B = {p, q} above, f is not surjective, so (C) fails.

Answer: Option (A) — f must be injective.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) fails by the two-element counterexample above: g may collapse elements of B that f never reaches.
  • (C) and (D) are about surjectivity, which composition does not force from injectivity.

Common misconception. Concluding that g is injective because g ∘ f is. g only has to be injective on the image of f, not on all of B.

Faster method. Remember the two legal deductions: g∘f injective ⇒ f injective, and g∘f surjective ⇒ g surjective. The other two implications are false.

Takeaway. Injectivity propagates backwards through a composite (to the first map); surjectivity propagates forwards (to the second).

DM-REL2-Q08MSQmedium

Let A be a finite set with |A| = n ≥ 1. Which of the following statements about equivalence relations on A are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The number of equivalence relations on A equals the number of partitions of A.
  2. An equivalence relation on A with exactly n classes must be the identity relation I_A.
  3. Every equivalence relation on A contains at least n ordered pairs.
  4. An equivalence relation on A with exactly one class contains exactly n ordered pairs.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Composition preserves both injectivity and surjectivity when both factors have the property, and the composite can also pass a property back to one factor.

Step by step.

  1. (A) If (g∘f)(a₁) = (g∘f)(a₂), injectivity of g gives f(a₁) = f(a₂) and injectivity of f gives a₁ = a₂. TRUE.
  2. (B) Take any c ∈ C. Surjectivity of g gives b ∈ B with g(b) = c; surjectivity of f gives a ∈ A with f(a) = b. Then (g∘f)(a) = c. TRUE.
  3. (C) The image of g ∘ f is g(f(A)) ⊆ g(B). If g(f(A)) = C then certainly g(B) = C. TRUE.
  4. (D) FALSE: take A = {1}, B = {p, q}, C = {r}, f(1) = p, g(p) = g(q) = r. Then g∘f is injective but g is not.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) reverses the correct direction: injectivity of the composite passes back to the first factor f, not to g.

Common misconception. Assuming every property is inherited in both directions. Composition preserves injective/injective and surjective/surjective forwards, but passes a property back to only one factor.

Faster method. Picture the arrows: A → B → C. Anything that must "not collide" is inherited by the map leaving A; anything that must "cover" is inherited by the map entering C.

Takeaway. g∘f injective ⇒ f injective; g∘f surjective ⇒ g surjective; both properties are preserved by composing two maps that have them.

DM-REL2-Q09NAThard

How many equivalence relations on A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} have exactly three equivalence classes?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two composites of the same pair of functions generally differ; comparing them means writing both in closed form and solving, and remembering that x² is not injective.

Step by step.

  1. h(x) = g(f(x)) = (x + 1)² and k(x) = f(g(x)) = x² + 1. They are different functions, so (A) is false.
  2. h(x) = k(x) ⇔ x² + 2x + 1 = x² + 1 ⇔ 2x = 0 ⇔ x = 0. Exactly one solution, so (B) is true.
  3. k(1) = k(−1) = 2, so k is not injective and (C) is false.
  4. k(h(x)) = ((x+1)²)² + 1 = (x+1)⁴ + 1 while h(k(x)) = (x² + 1 + 1)² = (x² + 2)². At x = 0 these are 2 and 4, so (D) is false.

Answer: Option (B) — exactly one real solution, x = 0.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails since h(1) = 4 but k(1) = 2.
  • (C) fails because squaring destroys injectivity.
  • (D) fails at x = 0, where the two iterates differ.

Common misconception. Assuming g ∘ f = f ∘ g for "simple" functions, or that composing twice is automatically commutative. Composition is associative but almost never commutative.

Faster method. Compare at a single convenient point first: h(0) = 1, k(0) = 1 (so 0 is a candidate root); h(1) = 4, k(1) = 2 (so they are different). Then solve the difference, which is linear here.

Takeaway. g∘f and f∘g can agree at isolated points without being equal functions; expanding both sides usually leaves a linear equation.

DM-REL1-Q05MCQmedium

On A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7} define aRb exactly when |a − b| ≤ 2. Which defining property of an equivalence relation does R fail?

  1. Reflexivity
  2. Symmetry
  3. Transitivity
  4. None — R is an equivalence relation
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An equivalence relation must satisfy all three of reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity; a single counterexample to any one of them is enough to disqualify it.

Step by step.

  1. Reflexive: |a − a| = 0 ≤ 2 for every a, so (a,a) ∈ R.
  2. Symmetric: |a − b| = |b − a|, so the condition is unchanged on swapping.
  3. Transitive: take a = 1, b = 2, c = 4. Then |1 − 2| = 1 ≤ 2 and |2 − 4| = 2 ≤ 2, but |1 − 4| = 3 > 2, so (1,4) ∉ R.
  4. Transitivity therefore fails.

Answer: Option (C) — transitivity.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is wrong: distance 0 always satisfies ≤ 2.
  • (B) is wrong: absolute value is unchanged by reversing the order.
  • (D) is wrong because of the explicit counterexample 1R2, 2R4 yet 1R̸4.

Common misconception. A "close enough" relation feels like it should be transitive. Distances add up, so a tolerance relation is almost never transitive — it fails exactly when the two hops together exceed the tolerance.

Faster method. Any relation of the form |a − b| ≤ k with k ≥ 1 is reflexive and symmetric but transitive only when k = 0.

Takeaway. Tolerance relations are the standard counterexample that reflexive + symmetric does not give an equivalence relation.

DM-REL1-Q06MCQhard

How many equivalence relations on A = {1, 2, 3, 4} place 1 and 2 in the same class?

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 7
  4. 8
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Equivalence relations on A correspond one-to-one with partitions of A; requiring 1 and 2 to share a class means gluing them into one block before partitioning.

Step by step.

  1. Fuse 1 and 2 into a single block B = {1, 2}.
  2. The remaining partition problem is over the three objects B, 3, 4.
  3. The number of partitions of a three-element set is the Bell number B(3) = 5.
  4. So there are 5 such equivalence relations.

Answer: Option (B) — 5.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 4 = B(3) − 1, dropping one of the five partitions.
  • (C) 7 counts the partitions of {1,2,3} plus the ones of {1,2,3,4} that keep 1 and 2 apart, which the question excludes.
  • (D) 8 is 2³, treating each of the three objects as independently in or out of the block containing 1 and 2 — which double counts.

Common misconception. Students try to count partitions of four elements and then filter, which is error-prone. Contracting the forced pair first turns the problem into a smaller standard one.

Faster method. Number of partitions of n elements in which a fixed k-set stays together = B(n − k + 1); here B(3) = 5.

Takeaway. "Two elements must be together" is a contraction — reduce the ground set by one and use the Bell number.

DM-REL1-Q17MSQmedium

Let R be an equivalence relation on a non-empty set A, and let [a] denote the equivalence class of a. Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every element of A lies in exactly one equivalence class.
  2. aRb if and only if [a] = [b].
  3. Any two distinct equivalence classes are disjoint.
  4. R has exactly |A| equivalence classes.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The equivalence classes of R form a partition of A: non-empty, pairwise disjoint, and covering A.

Step by step.

  1. (A): a ∈ [a] by reflexivity, so every element is in at least one class; if a ∈ [b] ∩ [c] then bRa and aRc, so bRc and [b] = [c] — at most one class. TRUE.
  2. (B): if aRb and x ∈ [a], then xRa and aRb give xRb, so [a] ⊆ [b]; symmetrically [b] ⊆ [a]. Conversely [a] = [b] with a ∈ [a] gives a ∈ [b], i.e. aRb. TRUE.
  3. (C): if c ∈ [a] ∩ [b] then [c] = [a] and [c] = [b] by (B), so [a] = [b]. TRUE.
  4. (D): the universal relation on A has one class, not |A|; the identity relation has |A|. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) fails: the number of classes ranges from 1 to |A| depending on R.

Common misconception. The count of classes is confused with the size of the set. |A| classes is the maximum, attained only by the identity relation.

Faster method. Everything about equivalence classes follows from "classes = blocks of a partition"; three of the four options are just restatements of that.

Takeaway. Equivalence relation ⟷ partition: the classes are the blocks, and there is no fourth option.

DM-REL1-Q24NATeasy

Let A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} and let R be the smallest equivalence relation on A containing {(1,2), (3,4)}. How many equivalence classes does R have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The smallest equivalence relation containing a set of pairs is obtained by taking the symmetric, transitive, reflexive closure — equivalently, by merging the elements that the given pairs force together.

Step by step.

  1. The pair (1,2) forces 1 and 2 into one class; the pair (3,4) forces 3 and 4 into another.
  2. Element 5 is in no given pair, so it stays alone.
  3. No given pair links the block {1,2} to the block {3,4} or to 5, so nothing further merges.
  4. Classes: {1,2}, {3,4}, {5} — three in total.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Students apply transitivity across the two given pairs, as if 2 were related to 3. It is not: the pairs (1,2) and (3,4) share no element.

Faster method. Count the connected components of the graph whose edges are the given pairs, then add the isolated vertices.

Takeaway. Smallest equivalence relation = connected components of the relation graph; each component is one class.

Partial orders & Hasse diagrams 8
DM-REL1-Q07MCQeasy

Which of the following relations on {1, 2, 3} is a partial order?

  1. {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (1,2), (2,1)}
  2. {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (1,2), (2,3)}
  3. {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3), (1,2), (1,3), (2,3)}
  4. {(1,2), (2,3), (1,3)}
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A partial order is reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive; all three must hold simultaneously.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is reflexive and transitive but not antisymmetric: (1,2) and (2,1) are both present with 1 ≠ 2.
  2. (B) is reflexive and antisymmetric but not transitive: (1,2) and (2,3) are present while (1,3) is not.
  3. (C) is the usual order ≤ on {1,2,3}: reflexive, antisymmetric, and transitive because 1 < 2 < 3 gives 1 < 3.
  4. (D) is transitive and antisymmetric but not reflexive — it is a strict order, and it is missing (1,1), (2,2), (3,3).

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) fails antisymmetry — it contains a 2-cycle.
  • (B) fails transitivity — it is missing the short-circuit pair (1,3).
  • (D) fails reflexivity — no diagonal pairs at all.

Common misconception. Reflexivity is the property most often skipped when students scan a list of pairs; and strict orders (like <) are routinely mistaken for partial orders.

Faster method. Check the three properties in the order reflexivity → antisymmetry → transitivity; the first list that fails one is out, and you rarely need to check all three for every option.

Takeaway. A partial order is ≤-like, not <-like: the diagonal is mandatory.

DM-REL2-Q10MCQeasy

In the divisibility poset on P = {1, 2, 4, 8}, which element does 4 cover?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 8
  4. 4 covers no element of P.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. To evaluate f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹, apply the right-hand inverse first: undo g, then undo f. Equivalently use (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹.

Step by step.

  1. Inverses: f⁻¹(y) = (y − 1)/2 and g⁻¹(y) = (y − 1)/3.
  2. Apply g⁻¹ first: g⁻¹(16) = (16 − 1)/3 = 5.
  3. Then apply f⁻¹: f⁻¹(5) = (5 − 1)/2 = 2.
  4. Check with the other route: (g ∘ f)(x) = 3(2x + 1) + 1 = 6x + 4, and 6x + 4 = 16 gives x = 2. Both routes agree.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Applying f⁻¹ first, which computes (g⁻¹ ∘ f⁻¹)(16) = g⁻¹(7.5) = 13/6 — a different number. In f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹ the function written on the right acts first, exactly as in ordinary composition.

Faster method. Solve (g ∘ f)(x) = 16 directly: 6x + 4 = 16 gives x = 2 in one line.

Takeaway. (g ∘ f)⁻¹ = f⁻¹ ∘ g⁻¹: inverting a composite reverses the order, and the order matters.

DM-REL2-Q11MCQmedium

In the divisibility poset on {1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 10, 15, 30}, how many edges does the Hasse diagram have?

  1. 10
  2. 11
  3. 12
  4. 14
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A function A → B is an independent choice of one codomain element for each domain element, so the count is |B|^|A|.

Step by step.

  1. Each of the 4 elements of A may map to any of the 3 elements of B.
  2. The choices are independent, so multiply: 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 = 3⁴ = 81.

Answer: Option (C) — 81.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 12 is |A| · |B|, which counts pairs, not functions.
  • (B) 24 counts the injective functions only.
  • (D) 64 is 4³, the count with the sizes swapped.

Common misconception. Swapping the exponent: the domain size is the exponent, the codomain size is the base.

Faster method. Say it out loud: "for each of the 4 inputs, 3 choices" → 3⁴.

Takeaway. Number of functions A → B is |B|^|A|.

DM-REL2-Q12NATmedium

In the divisibility poset on P = {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12, 18, 36}, how many edges does the Hasse diagram have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An injective function picks distinct images for the domain elements in turn, so the count is a falling factorial |B|(|B|−1)…(|B|−|A|+1).

Step by step.

  1. The first element of A has 5 possible images.
  2. The second must avoid that one: 4 choices. The third: 3 choices. The fourth: 2 choices.
  3. Multiply: 5 × 4 × 3 × 2 = 120.

Answer: Option (B) — 120.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 20 is 5 × 4, the count if |A| were 2.
  • (C) 625 is 5⁴, the count of all functions, which includes the non-injective ones.
  • (D) 24 is 4!, the count of bijections when |A| = |B| = 4.

Common misconception. Using |B|^|A| and forgetting the distinctness constraint, or using P(5,5) instead of P(5,4).

Faster method. P(5,4) = 5!/(5−4)! = 120.

Takeaway. Injective functions A → B: P(|B|, |A|), and 0 when |A| > |B|.

DM-REL2-Q13MSQhard

Consider the divisibility poset on P = {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36}. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 36 is the greatest element of P.
  2. The pair {12, 18} has no least upper bound in P.
  3. The least upper bound of {4, 6} in P is 12.
  4. The element 6 covers exactly two elements of P.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Counts follow the size comparison: all functions |B|^|A|, injective P(|B|,|A|), surjective 0 whenever |A| < |B|, bijections 0 whenever |A| ≠ |B|.

Step by step.

  1. All functions: 4³ = 64. (A) TRUE.
  2. Injective: 4 × 3 × 2 = 24. (B) TRUE.
  3. Surjective: 3 inputs can hit at most 3 of the 4 codomain elements, so none. (C) TRUE.
  4. Bijections require equal sizes; 3 ≠ 4, so there are 0, not 24. (D) FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the trap: 24 is the number of injections, and every injection here is far from a bijection because two codomain elements are left unused.

Common misconception. Equating "injective count" with "bijection count". They coincide only when the two sets have equal size.

Faster method. First compare sizes. Smaller domain → no surjection; unequal sizes → no bijection. That eliminates (C) and (D) instantly, leaving two arithmetic checks.

Takeaway. With |A| < |B|: |B|^|A| functions, P(|B|,|A|) injections, no surjections, no bijections.

DM-REL1-Q08MCQmedium

In the divisibility poset on {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12}, how many edges does the Hasse diagram have?

  1. 6
  2. 7
  3. 8
  4. 9
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A Hasse diagram draws one edge for each covering relation: a covers b when b < a and no element lies strictly between them.

Step by step.

  1. List the covering pairs. 1 is covered by exactly the atoms 2 and 3.
  2. Above 2: 4 and 6 cover it (both quotients are prime).
  3. Above 3: only 6 covers it.
  4. Above 4: 12 covers it; above 6: 12 covers it.
  5. Edges: 1–2, 1–3, 2–4, 2–6, 3–6, 4–12, 6–12 — that is 7.

Answer: Option (B) — 7 edges.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 6 is what you get by dropping the edge 2–6 and forgetting that 6 covers 2.
  • (C) 8 usually comes from drawing 3–12 (3 divides 12, but 6 lies in between, so 12 does not cover 3).
  • (D) 9 usually comes from drawing both 3–12 and 2–12.

Common misconception. Every divisor pair is drawn as an edge. In a Hasse diagram divisibility alone is not enough — the pair must be a covering pair.

Faster method. In a divisor-closed set, a covers b exactly when a / b is prime. Count the pairs (a, b) with a/b prime: 12/6, 12/4, 6/3, 6/2, 4/2, 3/1, 2/1 — seven.

Takeaway. Hasse edge = covering pair = quotient is prime (inside a divisor-closed set).

DM-REL1-Q09MCQhard

In the poset ({1, 2, …, 30}, ∣) ordered by divisibility, what is the size of a largest antichain?

  1. 10
  2. 15
  3. 16
  4. 30
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An antichain is a set of pairwise incomparable elements. If the poset can be split into c chains, every antichain meets each chain at most once, so it has at most c elements.

Step by step.

  1. Write every n ≤ 30 uniquely as n = 2^k · m with m odd.
  2. Numbers sharing the same odd part m form a chain m, 2m, 4m, …, since each divides the next.
  3. The odd parts that occur are the 15 odd numbers 1, 3, 5, …, 29, so the poset splits into 15 chains.
  4. Hence every antichain has at most 15 elements.
  5. The interval {16, 17, …, 30} is an antichain: for a < b in it, 2a ≥ 32 > 30, so a ∤ b. It has 15 elements.

Answer: Option (B) — 15.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 10 comes from using only the 10 primes up to 30, which is a different (and smaller) antichain question.
  • (C) 16 is an off-by-one on the interval {15, …, 30} — but 15 divides 30, so that interval is not an antichain.
  • (D) 30 is the whole set, which is far from an antichain.

Common misconception. The upper half {16, …, 30} looks obviously incomparable by size, but students forget to check divisibility inside it and often include 15 as well.

Faster method. For {1, …, 2n} the largest antichain under divisibility always has n elements; here n = 15.

Takeaway. Chain decomposition gives the upper bound, an explicit interval gives the lower bound — matching them proves the maximum.

DM-REL1-Q18MSQhard

Consider the divisibility poset on P = {1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24}. Which of the following are covering relations in this poset? (Select all that apply.)

  1. 8 covers 4
  2. 6 covers 2
  3. 12 covers 6
  4. 24 covers 6
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. a covers b when b ∣ a, b ≠ a, and no element of the poset lies strictly between them.

Step by step.

  1. (A) 4 ∣ 8 and the only element of P strictly between them would divide 8 and be divisible by 4 — none exists. TRUE.
  2. (B) 2 ∣ 6 and the only candidate strictly between is 3, which does not divide 6 within a chain from 2… in fact no element of P lies strictly between 2 and 6 (3 is not above 2). TRUE.
  3. (C) 6 ∣ 12 and no element of P lies strictly between 6 and 12 (the divisors of 12 above 6 are only 12). TRUE.
  4. (D) 6 ∣ 24, but 12 ∈ P satisfies 6 ∣ 12 ∣ 24, so 12 lies strictly between them. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) fails because the intermediate element 12 is present in P: 6 ∣ 12 ∣ 24, so 24 does not cover 6. (Note that 24 does cover 8, since no element of P lies between 8 and 24.)

Common misconception. Divisibility is taken as covering. Covering is a statement about the *specific* poset: 24 covers 8 here precisely because 12 is not a multiple of 8, while 24 fails to cover 6 because 12 is a multiple of 6.

Faster method. When the poset is divisor-closed (as P is), a covers b exactly when a/b is prime. Here 8/4 = 2, 6/2 = 3, 12/6 = 2 are prime, while 24/6 = 4 is not.

Takeaway. In a divisor-closed poset, covering ⟺ the quotient is prime; the moment an intermediate element is present, the quotient is composite.

Lattices in posets 8
DM-REL1-Q10MCQeasy

Which of the following posets is NOT a lattice?

  1. (𝒫(S), ⊆) for a set S
  2. (ℤ⁺, ∣) — the positive integers under divisibility
  3. The poset with elements {0, a, b} where 0 < a, 0 < b and a, b are incomparable
  4. ({1, 2, 3, 6, 12}, ∣)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A lattice is a poset in which every pair of elements has a least upper bound and a greatest lower bound inside the poset.

Step by step.

  1. (A): A ∨ B = A ∪ B and A ∧ B = A ∩ B — always a lattice.
  2. (B): a ∨ b = lcm(a,b) and a ∧ b = gcd(a,b) — always a lattice.
  3. (C): the upper bounds of a and b form the empty set, so no least upper bound exists. Not a lattice.
  4. (D): the set is divisor-closed, so every pair has both a join and a meet inside it: 2 ∨ 3 = lcm(2,3) = 6, 6 ∨ 12 = 12, 2 ∧ 12 = gcd(2,12) = 2, 3 ∧ 12 = 3. A lattice.

Answer: Option (C) — the three-element V-poset.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the Boolean lattice 2^S, the archetypal lattice.
  • (B) is the divisibility lattice of the positive integers, with lcm as join.
  • (D) is the divisor lattice of 12 restricted to a divisor-closed set, hence closed under both operations.

Common misconception. "Incomparable" is confused with "no join". Two incomparable elements can still have a join — the failure in (C) is that they have no common upper bound at all.

Faster method. Look for two elements with no common upper bound, or with two minimal common upper bounds. Either defect immediately disqualifies a lattice.

Takeaway. A lattice needs joins and meets for every pair, not just for comparable pairs.

DM-REL2-Q14MCQeasy

In the divisor lattice D(12) = ({1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12}, ∣), what is the join (least upper bound) of 4 and 6?

  1. 2
  2. 12
  3. 24
  4. 4 and 6 have no join in D(12).
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count onto maps by inclusion–exclusion over the codomain elements that are missed: Σ (−1)^k C(n,k) (n−k)^m for m inputs and n targets.

Step by step.

  1. Total functions: 3⁵ = 243.
  2. Subtract those missing at least one target. Missing a fixed target leaves 2⁵ = 32 functions, and there are C(3,1) = 3 choices of the missed target: 3 × 32 = 96.
  3. Add back the double subtractions. Missing two fixed targets leaves 1⁵ = 1 function, with C(3,2) = 3 such pairs: 3.
  4. Nothing misses all three targets. So the count is 243 − 96 + 3 = 150.

Answer: Option (C) — 150.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 90 is 3! · S(5,3) = 6 · 15 — that counts onto maps up to relabelling of the codomain, then multiplies, but S(5,3) = 25, not 15, so 90 is the wrong product.
  • (B) 125 is 5³, the count with the sizes swapped.
  • (D) 243 is 3⁵, all functions, including the 93 that miss something.

Common misconception. Using S(m,n) alone. The Stirling number counts partitions of the domain, so the number of onto maps is n! · S(m,n) = 6 × 25 = 150; forgetting the n! factor (or using a wrong Stirling value) is the usual error.

Faster method. S(5,3) = 25 from the Stirling triangle, then 3! × 25 = 150. Cross-check with inclusion–exclusion as above.

Takeaway. Surjections from an m-set onto an n-set: n! S(m,n), equivalently Σ(−1)^k C(n,k)(n−k)^m.

DM-REL2-Q15MCQmedium

For which of the following values of n is the divisor lattice D(n) NOT a Boolean lattice?

  1. n = 6
  2. n = 30
  3. n = 12
  4. n = 210
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A bijection from a finite set onto itself is a permutation of its elements, so the count is n!.

Step by step.

  1. Choose the image of element 1: 6 ways.
  2. Choose the image of element 2 from what is left: 5 ways, and so on down to 1 way for the last element.
