  3. Multiply: 6 × 5 × 4 × 3 × 2 × 1 = 720.

Answer: 720

Common misconception. Counting 6⁶ (all functions) or 2⁶. A bijection must use each element exactly once, which is a permutation, not an arbitrary assignment.

Faster method. 6! = 720; memorise factorials up to 10! = 3628800.

Takeaway. Bijections of an n-set to itself = n!; between two different n-sets also n!.

DM-REL2-Q16NAThard

In the divisor lattice D(36), how many elements have a complement?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. To force k + 1 objects into one of m boxes, you need km + 1 objects: with only km you could place exactly k in every box.

Step by step.

  1. Here m = 12 months and we want k + 1 = 3, so k = 2.
  2. The bound is 2 × 12 + 1 = 25.
  3. Sharpness: 24 people can be spread with exactly 2 per month, so 24 is not enough. With 25 people some month receives at least ⌈25/12⌉ = 3.

Answer: Option (C) — 25.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 13 forces only two in a month: ⌈13/12⌉ = 2.
  • (B) 24 is the largest number that avoids three in a month, so it does not guarantee it.
  • (D) 27 works but is not the minimum.

Common misconception. Answering 24 because "2 per month is 24". That is exactly the configuration to be avoided; the guarantee needs one more.

Faster method. ⌈N/12⌉ ≥ 3 ⇔ N > 24 ⇔ N ≥ 25.

Takeaway. Guaranteeing k+1 in some box of m boxes needs km + 1 objects — never km.

DM-REL2-Q17MSQmedium

In a lattice (L, ≤) with join ∨ and meet ∧, which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. a ∧ (a ∨ b) = a for all a, b ∈ L.
  2. a ∨ (a ∧ b) = b for all a, b ∈ L.
  3. If a ≤ b then a ∧ b = a.
  4. Every bounded lattice is complemented.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Pigeonhole: 10 objects in 9 boxes force some box to hold at least two. It says nothing about which box, or about how the rest are distributed.

Step by step.

  1. The 10 domain elements are assigned to 9 codomain elements.
  2. If every codomain element were the image of at most one element, the image would contain 10 distinct values inside a 9-element set — impossible.
  3. So some codomain element is hit at least twice. (B) holds.
  4. Nothing forces surjectivity: the map could send all 10 elements to one value and leave 8 values unused. So (C) fails, and (A) fails too.
  5. Nothing forces the excess to be exactly 2: f could send 3 elements to one value. So (D) fails.

Answer: Option (B) — some codomain element is hit at least twice.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is impossible here: injectivity needs at least 10 codomain elements.
  • (C) fails for the constant function, which is a perfectly legal map.
  • (D) is too specific: a 3-to-1 collision is equally allowed.

Common misconception. Strengthening the conclusion from "at least two" to "exactly two". The pigeonhole principle gives an inequality, not a distribution.

Faster method. Compare sizes: 10 > 9, so no injection; that kills (A) and, with a one-line constant-function counterexample, kills (C) and (D).

Takeaway. Pigeonhole conclusions are "at least", never "exactly"; and no injection exists as soon as the domain is larger than the codomain.

DM-REL1-Q11MCQmedium

For a positive integer n, let D(n) be the lattice of positive divisors of n under divisibility. D(n) is a Boolean lattice exactly when

  1. n is prime
  2. n is a product of distinct primes
  3. n is a power of a prime
  4. n is even
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. If n = p₁^{e₁} … p_k^{e_k}, then D(n) is isomorphic to the product of chains C_{e₁+1} × … × C_{e_k+1}. A Boolean lattice is a product of copies of the two-element chain.

Step by step.

  1. Each divisor corresponds to a tuple of exponents (a₁, …, a_k) with 0 ≤ aᵢ ≤ eᵢ, ordered coordinatewise.
  2. That is a product of chains of lengths eᵢ + 1.
  3. A product of chains is Boolean only when every factor is the two-element chain, i.e. every eᵢ = 1.
  4. So D(n) ≅ 2^k exactly when n is squarefree, a product of distinct primes.

Answer: Option (B) — n is a product of distinct primes.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) a prime gives D(p) ≅ 2¹, which is Boolean, but the condition must characterise all cases; n = 12 is neither prime nor Boolean, so (A) is too narrow.
  • (C) D(p³) is a chain of four elements — it has a chain of length 3, which no Boolean lattice has.
  • (D) n = 12 = 2² · 3 is even and D(12) is not Boolean.

Common misconception. Prime powers feel "simple" and are assumed to give Boolean lattices; in fact they give chains, the opposite extreme.

Faster method. Count atoms: D(n) is Boolean iff it has k atoms and exactly 2^k elements. For n = 12 there are 2 atoms but 6 elements ≠ 4.

Takeaway. Boolean divisor lattice ⟺ squarefree n; prime power ⟺ chain.

DM-REL1-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about lattices are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every finite lattice has a least element and a greatest element.
  2. In a distributive lattice an element has at most one complement.
  3. Every lattice is distributive.
  4. In every bounded lattice every element has a complement.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A finite lattice is bounded because the join and meet of all elements exist; distributivity is an extra axiom, and complementedness is another.

Step by step.

  1. (A): in a finite lattice the join of all elements is the greatest element and the meet of all elements is the least element. TRUE.
  2. (B): if b and c are both complements of a, then b = b ∧ 1 = b ∧ (a ∨ c) = (b ∧ a) ∨ (b ∧ c) = 0 ∨ (b ∧ c) = b ∧ c, and symmetrically c = b ∧ c, so b = c. TRUE.
  3. (C): the diamond M₃ and the pentagon N₅ are lattices that are not distributive. FALSE.
  4. (D): in N₅ the middle element has no complement. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) fails on the two classic non-distributive lattices M₃ and N₅.
  • (D) fails because boundedness guarantees only that 0 and 1 exist, not that every element has a complement.

Common misconception. Bounded is read as complemented. A bounded lattice may have elements with no complement at all, and in a non-distributive lattice an element may have several.

Faster method. Distributive + bounded + complemented = Boolean; each adjective is independent, and (C) and (D) each assert that one of them is automatic.

Takeaway. Distributivity and complementedness are independent extras; only distributivity makes complements unique.

DM-REL1-Q23NAThard

In the divisor lattice D(72) — the positive divisors of 72 ordered by divisibility — how many elements have a complement?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a bounded lattice, e is a complement of d when d ∧ e = 1 and d ∨ e = 72, i.e. gcd(d,e) = 1 and lcm(d,e) = 72.

Step by step.

  1. Write 72 = 2³ · 3². A divisor is d = 2^i 3^j with 0 ≤ i ≤ 3, 0 ≤ j ≤ 2.
  2. The two conditions together force d · e = 72, so e = 72/d is the only candidate.
  3. Then gcd(d, 72/d) = 1 requires d to take each prime's full exponent or none: i ∈ {0, 3} and j ∈ {0, 2}.
  4. That gives 2 × 2 = 4 divisors: 1, 8, 9, 72, with complements 72, 9, 8, 1.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. D(72) is distributive and bounded, so students assume every element has a complement. Distributive + bounded is not complemented — the standard counterexample is exactly this kind of divisor lattice.

Faster method. Such divisors are the unitary divisors of n: 2^k of them, where k is the number of distinct primes. Here 2² = 4.

Takeaway. In D(n), exactly 2^{ω(n)} elements have complements, where ω(n) is the number of distinct primes dividing n.

Composition & transitive closure 8
DM-REL1-Q12MCQeasy

Let R = {(1,2), (2,3)} on A = {1, 2, 3}. What is the composite relation R ∘ R?

  1. {(1,2), (2,3)}
  2. {(1,3)}
  3. {(1,2), (2,3), (1,3)}
  4. ∅
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. (a,c) ∈ S ∘ R exactly when some b satisfies (a,b) ∈ R and (b,c) ∈ S — a two-step walk.

Step by step.

  1. Start at 1: the only pair out of 1 is (1,2), so continue from 2. The only pair out of 2 is (2,3), giving (1,3).
  2. Start at 2: leads to 3, and nothing leaves 3, so no pair.
  3. Start at 3: nothing leaves 3, so no pair.
  4. Hence R ∘ R = {(1,3)}.

Answer: Option (B) — {(1,3)}.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is R itself, not the composite.
  • (C) is the transitive closure R⁺, which unions R with R².
  • (D) would need no two-step walk to exist, but 1 → 2 → 3 is one.

Common misconception. R² is read as "the transitive closure" or as "square each element". Composition is path concatenation of exactly two steps.

Faster method. Build a 3×3 boolean matrix for R and square it; the ones in R² are the two-step walks.

Takeaway. R² = two-step walks only; R⁺ = walks of every positive length.

DM-REL2-Q18MCQeasy

Let R = {(1,2), (2,3)} and S = {(2,3), (3,1)} on A = {1, 2, 3}. Which of the following equals S ∘ R?

  1. {(1,3), (2,1)}
  2. {(1,3), (1,1)}
  3. {(2,3), (3,1)}
  4. ∅
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each claim is a pigeonhole count: identify the objects, the boxes, and how many objects force a given occupancy.

Step by step.

  1. (A) 13 people, 12 months: ⌈13/12⌉ = 2, so some month holds at least two. TRUE.
  2. (B) 25 people: ⌈25/12⌉ = 3. TRUE.
  3. (C) 24 people can occupy the 12 months exactly two at a time, so three is not forced. FALSE.
  4. (D) Split {1, …, 20} into the 10 pairs {1,2}, {3,4}, …, {19,20}. Choosing 11 numbers means two come from the same pair, and those two are consecutive. TRUE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the boundary case: 24 = 2 × 12 is achievable with two per month, so it guarantees nothing beyond two.

Common misconception. Treating 24 as "close enough" to force three, and forgetting to build the boxes in (D). For (D) the boxes are the consecutive pairs, not the numbers themselves.

Faster method. For each claim, compute ⌈N/m⌉ and compare with the claimed occupancy; for (D), partition the range into the relevant pairs.

Takeaway. Pigeonhole questions are decided by the right choice of boxes: months, pairs, residues, or subsquares.

DM-REL2-Q19MCQmedium

Let R and S be relations on a set A. Which of the following statements is TRUE in general?

  1. R ∘ S = S ∘ R for all relations R and S.
  2. If R and S are both transitive, then R ∪ S is transitive.
  3. If R and S are both reflexive, then R ∘ S is reflexive.
  4. If R and S are both symmetric, then R ∘ S is symmetric.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Work modulo 3. Only the residues matter, and there are just three of them, so a short case analysis on how the five residues are distributed settles everything.

Step by step.

  1. Reduce the five integers modulo 3, giving five residues in {0, 1, 2}.
  2. Case 1: some residue class contains at least three of them. Three integers with the same residue r sum to 3r ≡ 0 (mod 3). Done.
  3. Case 2: no class has three. With five residues over three classes, the counts must be (2, 2, 1) in some order.
  4. Then one element has each residue 0, 1, 2, and 0 + 1 + 2 = 3 ≡ 0 (mod 3). Done.
  5. So a suitable triple always exists, and (A) is guaranteed.

Answer: Option (A) — some three have a sum divisible by 3.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) fails: take all five equal to 1; every pair sums to 2, which is not divisible by 3.
  • (C) fails for the same five 1s: four of them sum to 4.
  • (D) fails for the same five 1s: the total is 5.

Common misconception. Stopping after Case 1. The interesting case is the "balanced" distribution (2, 2, 1), where no class is large but one element of each residue still gives a multiple of 3.

Faster method. Check the two shapes of a 5-element multiset of residues: a class of size ≥ 3, or the balanced (2,2,1) split. Both give a zero-sum triple, so the claim is immediate.

Takeaway. Any 5 integers contain three whose sum is a multiple of 3; the extremal configuration is all five congruent, which already satisfies the claim.

DM-REL2-Q20NATeasy

On A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} let R = {(1,2), (2,3), (1,3), (3,4), (4,5), (2,5)}. How many ordered pairs must be added to R to obtain its transitive closure?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. "Difference divisible by 9" means the two integers are congruent modulo 9, so the boxes are the 9 residue classes and the objects are the chosen integers.

Step by step.

  1. Two integers differ by a multiple of 9 exactly when they share a residue modulo 9. There are 9 residues, i.e. 9 boxes.
  2. With 9 integers we can pick one from each class, e.g. 0, 1, …, 8, and no two share a residue. So n = 9 is not enough.
  3. With 10 integers, two must fall in the same class, and their difference is divisible by 9.
  4. Hence the smallest such n is 10.

Answer: 10

Common misconception. Answering 9 because there are 9 residue classes. Nine objects can occupy nine boxes one each; the guarantee needs one more object than there are boxes.

Faster method. The answer is always (number of boxes) + 1: here 9 + 1 = 10.

Takeaway. For "two congruent modulo m" the threshold is m + 1; the extremal example is one representative per residue class.

DM-REL2-Q25MCQeasy

Let R = {(1,2), (2,3), (3,1)} on A = {1, 2, 3}. Which of the following equals R ∘ R?

  1. {(1,3), (2,1), (3,2)}
  2. {(1,2), (2,3), (3,1)}
  3. {(1,1), (2,2), (3,3)}
  4. ∅
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unroll the recurrence from the top down, replacing each argument by its floor half until the base case is reached, then add the contributions on the way back up.

Step by step.

  1. T(8) = T(4) + 8.
  2. T(4) = T(2) + 4.
  3. T(2) = T(1) + 2 = 1 + 2 = 3.
  4. So T(4) = 3 + 4 = 7 and T(8) = 7 + 8 = 15.
  5. Check with the sum form: T(8) = 8 + 4 + 2 + 1 = 15, since the arguments along the chain are the powers of two down to the base case.

Answer: 15

Common misconception. Adding the halved values (4 + 2 + 1) instead of the original arguments, which gives 8 rather than 15; in this recurrence the added term is n, the current argument.

Faster method. For n = 2^k the chain is 2^k, 2^{k−1}, …, 1, so T(2^k) = 2^{k+1} − 1. With k = 3: T(8) = 16 − 1 = 15.

Takeaway. Unroll small recurrences explicitly; for power-of-two arguments the answer is a geometric sum n + n/2 + n/4 + … + 1 = 2n − 1.

DM-REL1-Q13MCQmedium

Let R = {(1,2), (2,1), (3,4)} on A = {1, 2, 3, 4}. How many ordered pairs are in the transitive closure R⁺?

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 7
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The transitive closure contains (a,b) exactly when a walk of positive length runs from a to b.

Step by step.

  1. The pairs (1,2) and (2,1) form a 2-cycle, so from 1 you can reach 2, and from 2 you can reach 1.
  2. Composing the cycle with itself gives (1,1) and (2,2), which must be added.
  3. The pair (3,4) is isolated: nothing leaves 4 and nothing enters 3, so it generates no new pair.
  4. So R⁺ = {(1,2), (2,1), (1,1), (2,2), (3,4)} — five pairs.

Answer: Option (B) — 5 ordered pairs.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 4 keeps only R and forgets that a cycle forces its diagonal pairs into the closure.
  • (C) 6 would need an extra pair such as (3,3) or (4,4), which requires a walk, not just an element.
  • (D) 7 would need both diagonals of the isolated pair as well.

Common misconception. The transitive closure is assumed to be the reflexive-transitive closure. Here the diagonal pairs come from the cycle, not from reflexivity — (3,3) and (4,4) are correctly absent.

Faster method. Any strongly connected component of size s ≥ 2 contributes all s² pairs to R⁺; here 2² + 1 = 5.

Takeaway. Cycles generate diagonal pairs inside the closure; isolated edges generate nothing extra.

DM-REL1-Q20MSQeasy

Let R and S be relations on a set A. Which of the following identities hold? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (R ∪ S)⁻¹ = R⁻¹ ∪ S⁻¹
  2. (R ∩ S)⁻¹ = R⁻¹ ∩ S⁻¹
  3. (S ∘ R)⁻¹ = R⁻¹ ∘ S⁻¹
  4. R ∘ R⁻¹ is the identity relation on A
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Inversion commutes with union, intersection and difference because it only swaps the two coordinates of each pair; composition reverses the order of the factors.

Step by step.

  1. (A): (a,b) ∈ (R ∪ S)⁻¹ ⇔ (b,a) ∈ R ∪ S ⇔ (b,a) ∈ R or (b,a) ∈ S ⇔ (a,b) ∈ R⁻¹ ∪ S⁻¹. TRUE.
  2. (B): the same argument with "or" replaced by "and". TRUE.
  3. (C): (a,c) ∈ (S ∘ R)⁻¹ ⇔ (c,a) ∈ S ∘ R ⇔ ∃b, (c,b) ∈ S, (b,a) ∈ R ⇔ ∃b, (b,c) ∈ S⁻¹, (a,b) ∈ R⁻¹ ⇔ (a,c) ∈ R⁻¹ ∘ S⁻¹. TRUE.
  4. (D): R ∘ R⁻¹ = {(a,a) : ∃b, (a,b) ∈ R}. If some element of A has no R-image, its diagonal pair is missing, so the relation is only a subset of I_A. FALSE.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) fails whenever R is not total, e.g. R = {(1,2)} on A = {1,2} gives only (1,1).

Common misconception. The inverse of a composite is written in the same order. It must be reversed: (S ∘ R)⁻¹ = R⁻¹ ∘ S⁻¹, like inverting a product of matrices.

Faster method. For composition, read right-to-left in both the original and the inverse; for union and intersection the inverse distributes freely.

Takeaway. R ∘ R⁻¹ is the identity restricted to the domain of R, not the identity on A.

DM-REL1-Q25NATmedium

On A = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} let R = {(1,2), (2,3), (3,1), (4,5)}. How many ordered pairs are in the transitive closure R⁺?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The transitive closure contains (a,b) exactly when a walk of positive length runs from a to b.

Step by step.

  1. Elements 1, 2, 3 form a directed cycle 1 → 2 → 3 → 1, so every element of the cycle reaches every element of the cycle.
  2. That contributes 3² = 9 pairs.
  3. The pair (4,5) is isolated from the cycle and adds exactly one pair.
  4. Total: 9 + 1 = 10.

Answer: 10.

Common misconception. Students count only the cycle's own three pairs plus (4,5) and get 4, forgetting that a strongly connected component contributes all s² pairs.

Faster method. A strongly connected component of size s contributes s² pairs to R⁺; add the pairs from all other components separately.

Takeaway. Collapse R into strongly connected components, then count Σ s² over the components.

Counting relations 8
DM-REL1-Q14MCQeasy

How many relations are there on a set with three elements?

  1. 64
  2. 256
  3. 512
  4. 729
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A relation on A is any subset of A × A, so the count is 2^{|A|²}.

Step by step.

  1. With |A| = 3, the product set has 3 × 3 = 9 ordered pairs.
  2. Each of those nine pairs is independently either in or out of the relation.
  3. So the number of relations is 2⁹ = 512.

Answer: Option (C) — 512.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 64 = 2⁶ counts relations on a three-element set that are forced to be symmetric (6 free choices), or relations on a set of size 2.45 — not a valid interpretation.
  • (B) 256 = 2⁸ comes from using n² − 1 free pairs, an off-by-one.
  • (D) 729 = 3⁶ is the number of functions from a 6-element set, not of relations.

Common misconception. The exponent is |A × A| = n², not n. Students who write 2³ = 8 are counting subsets of A instead.

Faster method. Memorise the ladder: n = 1, 2, 3, 4 gives 2, 16, 512, 65536 relations.

Takeaway. Relations on an n-element set: 2^{n²}.

DM-REL2-Q21MCQeasy

How many irreflexive relations are there on a set with three elements?

  1. 8
  2. 64
  3. 512
  4. 729
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. ⌊x⌋ is the largest integer not exceeding x; ⌈x⌉ is the smallest integer not less than x. Their difference measures the fractional part.

Step by step.

  1. If x ∈ ℤ, then ⌊x⌋ = ⌈x⌉ = x, so the difference is 0.
  2. If x ∉ ℤ, write x = k + f with k ∈ ℤ and 0 < f < 1. Then ⌊x⌋ = k and ⌈x⌉ = k + 1.
  3. So the difference is 1 exactly for non-integers.
  4. Check: x = 2.3 gives 3 − 2 = 1; x = −2.3 gives −2 − (−3) = 1; x = 5 gives 0.

Answer: Option (B) — 0 for integers, 1 otherwise.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) each fail for one of the two cases: (A) at x = 2.3, (C) at x = 5.
  • (D) never occurs: ⌈x⌉ ≥ ⌊x⌋ always, so the difference is never negative.

Common misconception. Handling negative numbers wrongly. For x = −2.3, ⌊x⌋ = −3 and ⌈x⌉ = −2; the difference is still 1, not −1.

Faster method. The difference is the indicator of "x is not an integer" — worth memorising, together with ⌊x⌋ + ⌈−x⌉ = 0.

Takeaway. ⌈x⌉ − ⌊x⌋ ∈ {0, 1}, equal to 0 exactly on the integers.

DM-REL2-Q22MCQmedium

How many relations on a set with four elements are both reflexive and antisymmetric?

  1. 64
  2. 216
  3. 729
  4. 4096
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Counting "neither" is inclusion–exclusion on the complement: subtract the multiples of 3 and of 5, then add back the multiples of 15 that were removed twice.

Step by step.

  1. Multiples of 3 up to 1000: ⌊1000/3⌋ = 333.
  2. Multiples of 5: ⌊1000/5⌋ = 200.
  3. Multiples of 15 (counted in both lists): ⌊1000/15⌋ = 66.
  4. Divisible by 3 or 5: 333 + 200 − 66 = 467.
  5. Neither: 1000 − 467 = 533.

Answer: Option (B) — 533.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 466 forgets to add back the 66 multiples of 15.
  • (C) 534 is off by one, the usual fencepost slip.
  • (D) 600 is 1000 minus the 400 multiples of 5 and 3 counted separately without the overlap correction.

Common misconception. Subtracting 333 and 200 and stopping: the 66 multiples of 15 get removed twice, so the total of "divisible by 3 or 5" is overstated and the "neither" count is understated by 66.

Faster method. 1000 − 333 − 200 + 66 = 533; note the alternating signs and use floor division only.

Takeaway. For "neither a nor b" up to N: N − ⌊N/a⌋ − ⌊N/b⌋ + ⌊N/lcm(a,b)⌋.

DM-REL2-Q23MCQhard

How many partial orders are there on a set with three elements?

  1. 12
  2. 19
  3. 27
  4. 33
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Negation swaps floor and ceiling; floors add sub-additively, never super-additively.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: reflecting about 0 turns the greatest integer below x into the least integer above −x. Test x = 2.3: ⌊−2.3⌋ = −3 = −⌈2.3⌉.
  2. (B) True: the same reflection the other way. Test x = 2.3: ⌈−2.3⌉ = −2 = −⌊2.3⌋.
  3. (C) False: at x = 0.4, ⌊0.9⌋ = 0 but ⌈0.4⌉ = 1.
  4. (D) True: ⌊x⌋ ≤ x and ⌊y⌋ ≤ y, so ⌊x⌋ + ⌊y⌋ ≤ x + y; the left side is an integer, hence it is at most ⌊x + y⌋.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is a near-identity that fails for fractional parts below ½; it holds only when {x} ≥ ½.

Common misconception. Believing ⌊x + y⌋ = ⌊x⌋ + ⌊y⌋. It can differ by 1 (take x = y = 0.6) but the left side is never smaller.

Faster method. Test each identity at a fractional value such as x = 0.4 and at a negative value: most false identities die at once.

Takeaway. ⌊−x⌋ = −⌈x⌉, ⌈−x⌉ = −⌊x⌋, and ⌊x⌋ + ⌊y⌋ ≤ ⌊x + y⌋ ≤ ⌊x⌋ + ⌊y⌋ + 1.

DM-REL2-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about counting relations on a set A with |A| = n are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every relation on A is a subset of A × A, and every subset of A × A is a relation on A.
  2. The number of symmetric relations on A is 2^(n(n+1)/2).
  3. The number of antisymmetric relations on A is 2^(n(n−1)/2).
  4. A reflexive relation on A contains exactly n² − n ordered pairs.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unrolling a recurrence that halves its argument counts how many halvings are needed to reach 0, i.e. the number of bits of n plus one.

Step by step.

  1. f(1000) = f(500) + 1 = f(250) + 2 = f(125) + 3.
  2. f(125) = f(62) + 1, f(62) = f(31) + 1, f(31) = f(15) + 1, f(15) = f(7) + 1, f(7) = f(3) + 1, f(3) = f(1) + 1, f(1) = f(0) + 1 = 2.
  3. Working back up: f(3) = 3, f(7) = 4, f(15) = 5, f(31) = 6, f(62) = 7, f(125) = 8, f(250) = 9, f(500) = 10, f(1000) = 11.
  4. Closed form: f(n) = ⌊log₂ n⌋ + 2 for n ≥ 1. Here ⌊log₂ 1000⌋ = 9 since 2⁹ = 512 ≤ 1000 < 1024 = 2¹⁰, so f(1000) = 11.

Answer: Option (B) — 11.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 10 forgets the base case f(0) = 1 and counts only the halvings.
  • (C) 12 and (D) 13 over-count by one or two, typically by rounding log₂ 1000 ≈ 9.97 up and then adding the base twice.

Common misconception. Using log₂1000 ≈ 9.97 → 10 and then answering 10. The recurrence counts halvings with floor division, and the base case contributes the extra 1.

Faster method. Count the bits: 1000 = 1111101000₂ has 10 bits, so the number of halvings to reach 0 is 10 and f = 10 + 1 = 11.

Takeaway. For f(n) = f(⌊n/2⌋) + c, f(n) = c⌊log₂ n⌋ + f(0) + c on n ≥ 1.

DM-REL1-Q15MCQmedium

How many reflexive relations are there on a set with four elements?

  1. 1024
  2. 2048
  3. 4096
  4. 65536
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reflexivity forces the n diagonal pairs into the relation; every other pair stays free, so the count is 2^{n² − n}.

Step by step.

  1. There are 4² = 16 ordered pairs in total.
  2. The four diagonal pairs (a,a) must be included — no freedom there.
  3. The remaining 16 − 4 = 12 pairs are free.
  4. So the number of reflexive relations is 2¹² = 4096.

Answer: Option (C) — 4096.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 1024 = 2¹⁰ subtracts the wrong number of forced pairs.
  • (B) 2048 = 2¹¹ is the count for a set of size 4 where only three pairs are forced.
  • (D) 65536 = 2¹⁶ counts all relations, ignoring reflexivity entirely.

Common misconception. Students subtract n from n² but then compute 2^n instead of 2^{n²−n}; or they forget that the diagonal is forced *in*, not just removed from the count.

Faster method. Reflexive: 2^{n(n−1)}. Here 2^{12} = 4096.

Takeaway. Reflexive relations: 2^{n(n−1)}; irreflexive: the same count; symmetric: 2^{n(n+1)/2}.

DM-REL1-Q21NATeasy

How many symmetric relations are there on a set with three elements?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A symmetric relation is determined independently on the diagonal pairs and on each unordered pair {a, b}: for off-diagonal pairs symmetry forces both (a,b) and (b,a) to be in, or both to be out.

Step by step.

  1. There are 3 diagonal pairs, each free: 2³ choices.
  2. There are C(3,2) = 3 unordered off-diagonal pairs; symmetry makes each an all-or-nothing choice: 2³ choices.
  3. Multiply: 2³ · 2³ = 2⁶ = 64.

Answer: 64.

Common misconception. Counting 2⁹ = 512 by treating all nine pairs as independent — symmetry couples the pairs (a,b) and (b,a).

Faster method. Symmetric relations on n elements: 2^{n(n+1)/2}; here 2⁶ = 64.

Takeaway. Symmetric: 2^{n(n+1)/2}. Antisymmetric: 2ⁿ · 3^{n(n−1)/2}.

DM-REL1-Q22NATmedium

How many antisymmetric relations are there on a set with two elements?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Antisymmetry only constrains pairs of the form (a,b) and (b,a) with a ≠ b: they may not both be present.

Step by step.

  1. Let A = {1, 2}. The two diagonal pairs are unconstrained: 2² = 4 choices.
  2. For the pair {1, 2} there are three legal states: include neither, include only (1,2), include only (2,1).
  3. Multiply: 4 × 3 = 12.

Answer: 12.

Common misconception. Counting 2⁴ = 16 by treating all four pairs as free — the state "both (1,2) and (2,1)" is exactly what antisymmetry forbids.

Faster method. Antisymmetric relations on n elements: 2ⁿ · 3^{n(n−1)/2}; here 4 × 3 = 12.

Takeaway. Antisymmetry: each unordered off-diagonal pair has 3 legal states, each diagonal pair 2.

Unit 16 — Logic & Proof Techniques 75 solved questions
Direct proof, contradiction & contrapositive 37
DM-LPT-E-02MCQeasy

The contrapositive of the implication "If n² is even, then n is even" is:

  1. If n is even, then n² is even.
  2. If n² is odd, then n is odd.
  3. If n is odd, then n² is odd.
  4. If n is even, then n² is odd.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The contrapositive of p → q is ¬q → ¬p, and it is logically equivalent to the original.

Step by step.

  1. Here p = "n² is even", q = "n is even".
  2. ¬q = "n is odd", ¬p = "n² is odd".
  3. Contrapositive: "If n is odd, then n² is odd".

Answer: Option (C)..

Common misconception. Option (B) is the inverse ¬p → ¬q, which is equivalent to the converse, not the original. Students often swap contrapositive and inverse.

Takeaway. Contrapositive = negate both, then swap. Inverse = negate both, keep order. Converse = swap only.

DM-LPT-E-04MCQeasy

The standard proof that √2 is irrational begins by assuming √2 = a/b with gcd(a,b) = 1 and derives that both a and b are even. This is an example of:

  1. Direct proof
  2. Proof by cases
  3. Proof by counterexample
  4. Proof by contradiction
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a proof by contradiction we assume the negation of the claim and derive an impossibility.

Step by step.

  1. Assume the negation: √2 is rational, √2 = a/b in lowest terms.
  2. Derive a = 2m and b = 2n, so gcd(a,b) ≥ 2.
  3. This contradicts gcd(a,b) = 1, so the assumption is false.

Answer: Option (D)..

Common misconception. Students sometimes call this a 'contrapositive' proof. It is a contradiction proof: we assume the opposite of the whole statement, not the negation of the conclusion of an implication.

Takeaway. Rationality of √2, infinitude of primes, and √2 + √3 irrationality are all classic contradiction proofs.

DM-LPT-E-06MCQeasy

For the statement "If x > 3, then x² > 9", which of the following is its CONVERSE?

  1. If x² > 9, then x > 3.
  2. If x ≤ 3, then x² ≤ 9.
  3. If x² ≤ 9, then x ≤ 3.
  4. x > 3 if and only if x² > 9.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Converse of p → q is q → p.

Step by step.

  1. p = "x > 3", q = "x² > 9".
  2. Converse = q → p = "If x² > 9, then x > 3".

Answer: Option (A)..

Common misconception. Option (B) is the inverse and (C) is the contrapositive. A converse is not logically equivalent to the original.

Takeaway. Converse swaps; inverse negates; contrapositive does both. Only the contrapositive is equivalent.

DM-LPT-E-08MCQmedium

Which of the following is a VALID rule of inference?

  1. From p → q and q, infer p.
  2. From p → q and ¬p, infer ¬q.
  3. From p → q and ¬q, infer ¬p.
  4. From p → q and p, infer ¬q.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Modus tollens: (p → q) ∧ ¬q ⇒ ¬p is valid. Affirming the consequent and denying the antecedent are fallacies.

Step by step.

  1. (A) affirms the consequent — invalid.
  2. (B) denies the antecedent — invalid.
  3. (C) modus tollens — valid.
  4. (D) contradicts modus ponens — invalid.

Answer: Option (C)..

Common misconception. The two most common fallacies are 'affirming the consequent' and 'denying the antecedent'; both look like modus ponens/tollens but are invalid.

Takeaway. Valid: modus ponens, modus tollens, hypothetical syllogism, disjunctive syllogism, resolution.

DM-LPT-E-09MCQmedium

To prove "if n is an integer and n² is odd, then n is odd", the cleanest standard approach is:

  1. A direct proof expanding n² as a general integer.
  2. Proof by contrapositive: assume n is even and show n² is even.
  3. Proof by counterexample.
  4. Proof by checking n = 1, 3, 5, …
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When the conclusion is hard to use directly, prove the contrapositive instead.

Step by step.

  1. Contrapositive: if n is even, then n² is even.
  2. Let n = 2k; then n² = 4k² = 2(2k²), which is even.
  3. Done.

Answer: Option (B)..

Common misconception. A direct proof of the original is awkward because 'n² odd' is hard to unpack; the contrapositive makes the algebra trivial.

Takeaway. If the hypothesis is a 'negative' or awkward property (odd, irrational, not divisible), try the contrapositive.

DM-LPT-E-12MCQmedium

Consider the statement: "For every real number x, if x² < 0, then x = 7." Which of the following is correct?

  1. False, because x = 7 does not satisfy x² < 0.
  2. True only for x = 7.
  3. Vacuously true, because no real x satisfies x² < 0.
  4. Neither true nor false.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An implication p → q is true whenever the hypothesis p is false. If p is never true, the universal statement is vacuously true.

Step by step.

  1. For every real x, x² ≥ 0, so x² < 0 is false for all x.
  2. Each implication instance is therefore true (false → anything is true).
  3. Hence the universally quantified statement is (vacuously) true.

Answer: Option (C)..

Common misconception. Judging the statement 'false' because the conclusion 'x = 7' seems unrelated; the hypothesis is never satisfied, so the implication never fails.

Takeaway. Empty-domain / impossible-hypothesis implications are vacuously true — a favourite GATE trap.

DM-LPT-E-13MCQmedium

Which of the following statements is most naturally proved by a proof by cases (division into cases)?

  1. √2 is irrational.
  2. There are infinitely many prime numbers.
  3. 2 + 2 = 4.
  4. For every integer n, |n| ≥ n.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A proof by cases splits the domain into exhaustive sub-cases and proves the claim in each.

Step by step.

  1. |n| ≥ n: if n ≥ 0 then |n| = n ≥ n; if n < 0 then |n| = −n > n.
  2. Both cases cover all integers, so the claim holds.

Answer: Option (D)..

Common misconception. Choosing √2 irrationality (contradiction) or infinitude of primes (contradiction) — those are not naturally case proofs.

Takeaway. Whenever a statement depends on the sign or residue of an integer, a case split (parity, sign, mod k) is usually cleanest.

DM-LPT-E-15MCQhard

Which of the following statements about proof techniques is FALSE?

  1. A proof by contrapositive of p → q is a direct proof of ¬q → ¬p.
  2. Proof by contradiction can be used for statements that are not implications.
  3. If a statement is proved by contradiction, then its negation is false.
  4. The inverse ¬p → ¬q of p → q is logically equivalent to p → q.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. p → q is equivalent to its contrapositive ¬q → ¬p, but NOT to its inverse ¬p → ¬q.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: that is the definition of a contrapositive proof.
  2. (B) True: contradiction is general (e.g. 'there are infinitely many primes').
  3. (C) True: deriving a contradiction shows ¬S is impossible, so S holds.
  4. (D) False: ¬p → ¬q ≡ q → p, the converse, not p → q.

Answer: Option (D)..

Common misconception. Believing the inverse is equivalent to the original. Only the contrapositive is equivalent.

Takeaway. Equivalent to p→q: itself, its contrapositive. NOT equivalent: converse, inverse.

DM-LPT-E-16MSQeasy

Which of the following are logically equivalent to p → q ? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ¬p ∨ q
  2. ¬p → ¬q
  3. ¬q → ¬p
  4. ¬(p ∧ ¬q)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q ≡ ¬q → ¬p ≡ ¬(p ∧ ¬q).

Step by step.

  1. Material implication: p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q.
  2. Contrapositive: p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p.
  3. Negation of conjunction: ¬p ∨ q ≡ ¬(p ∧ ¬q).
  4. ¬p → ¬q ≡ p ∨ ¬q ≡ q → p, the converse — not equivalent.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)..

Common misconception. Including (B): the inverse is equivalent to the converse, not to the original implication.

Takeaway. Memorise the three equivalent rewrites of an implication; the inverse is never one of them.

DM-LPT-E-17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every proof by contrapositive of p → q can also be phrased as a proof by contradiction.
  2. Every proof by contradiction is a proof by contrapositive.
  3. A proof by contrapositive of p → q proceeds by proving ¬q → ¬p directly.
  4. A proof by contradiction of p → q may assume both p and ¬q and derive a contradiction.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Contrapositive is a special, direct way of doing what contradiction does indirectly.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: assume p and ¬q, the contrapositive derivation gives a contradiction.
  2. (B) False: contradiction applies to non-implication statements too.
  3. (C) True: that is the definition.
  4. (D) True: assuming p and ¬q is the standard contradiction setup for an implication.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D)..

Common misconception. Thinking the two methods are identical. Contrapositive is strictly a proof of ¬q → ¬p; contradiction is more general.

Takeaway. Contrapositive ⊂ contradiction: every contrapositive proof is a contradiction proof, not vice versa.

DM-LPT-E-18MSQmedium

Let S be the statement: "If a positive integer is divisible by 6, then it is divisible by 3." Which of the following are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The converse of S is true.
  2. The inverse of S is true.
  3. The contrapositive of S is true.
  4. S itself is true.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. S is true; its contrapositive is always equivalent to it; the converse and inverse need not be true.

Step by step.

  1. S: 6 | n ⇒ 3 | n. True, since 3 | 6.
  2. Contrapositive: 3 ∤ n ⇒ 6 ∤ n. True (equivalent to S).
  3. Converse: 3 | n ⇒ 6 | n. False, e.g. n = 9.
  4. Inverse: 6 ∤ n ⇒ 3 ∤ n. False, e.g. n = 9.

Answer: Options (C) and (D)..

Common misconception. Assuming that if an implication is true then its converse is too. Converse and inverse can both fail.

Takeaway. Test converse/inverse with a small counterexample (here n = 9).

DM-LPT-E-19MSQhard

Consider the claim C: "For every integer n, n² + n is even." Which of the following are VALID ways to establish C? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Disproof by exhibiting n = 2.
  2. Proof by cases: consider n even and n odd separately.
  3. Proof by contradiction: suppose n² + n is odd and derive a contradiction.
  4. Rewrite n² + n = n(n + 1) and note one of two consecutive integers is even.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Parity of n² + n can be shown by cases, by contradiction, or by a product-of-consecutive-integers argument.

Step by step.

  1. (B) Valid: n = 2k → n²+n = 2(2k²+k); n = 2k+1 → n²+n = 2(2k²+3k+1).
  2. (C) Valid: assuming odd leads to n(n+1) odd, impossible for consecutive integers.
  3. (A) Invalid: n = 2 satisfies C, it is not a disproof.
  4. (D) Valid: of n and n+1 one is even, so the product is even.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D)..

Common misconception. Thinking a single verified example (n = 2) 'proves' or 'disproves' a universal claim. Examples never prove a universal statement.

Takeaway. n² + n is always even — a template result worth memorising for parity arguments.

DM-LPT-E-23NATmedium

How many of the following four propositions are tautologies? (i) p → (p ∨ q) (ii) (p ∧ q) → p (iii)(p → q) → (q → p) (iv) (p → q) ↔ (¬p ∨ q) (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A tautology is true for every truth assignment; use the truth table or algebraic equivalences.

Step by step.

  1. (i) p → (p∨q): when p is true p∨q is true; when p is false the implication is true. Tautology.
  2. (ii) (p∧q) → p: if p∧q true then p true. Tautology.
  3. (iii)(p→q)→(q→p): p = F, q = T gives (T)→(F) = F. Not a tautology.
  4. (iv)(p→q)↔(¬p∨q): both sides are the same formula, so it is always true. Tautology.
  5. Tautologies: (i), (ii), (iv) = 3.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Marking (iii) as a tautology because both components 'look like implications'. Test p = F, q = T.

Takeaway. (iv) is an instance of the equivalence p→q ≡ ¬p∨q, so it is a tautology by definition.

DM-LPT-E-25NAThard

Consider the proposition (p ∨ q) → (p ∧ q). Over the four possible truth assignments of (p, q), how many make this proposition TRUE? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An implication is false only when the antecedent is true and the consequent is false.

Step by step.

  1. (p,q) = (T,T): T → T = T.
  2. (T,F): T → F = F.
  3. (F,T): T → F = F.
  4. (F,F): F → F = T.
  5. True for 2 assignments.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Assuming (F,F) is false. A false antecedent makes the implication vacuously true.

Takeaway. (p∨q) → (p∧q) is true exactly when p and q agree — i.e. it is equivalent to p ↔ q.

DM-LPT2-M-01MCQeasy

A direct proof of the statement “For every integer `n`, if `n` is odd then `n²` is odd” proceeds by

  1. assuming `n` is odd and deriving that `n²` is odd.
  2. assuming `n²` is odd and deriving that `n` is odd.
  3. assuming `n²` is even and deriving that `n` is even.
  4. exhibiting several odd integers and checking that each of their squares is odd.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A direct proof of `p → q` takes the hypothesis `p` as a given and, by a chain of valid deductions, arrives at the conclusion `q`. Nothing about `q` may be assumed on the way.

Step by step.

  1. The statement has the form ∀n (Odd(n) → Odd(n²)). Fix an arbitrary integer n and suppose Odd(n).
  2. Write n = 2k + 1 for some integer k. This is the definition of odd, so the step is justified by the hypothesis alone.
  3. Then n² = (2k+1)² = 4k² + 4k + 1 = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1, which is of the form 2m + 1 with m = 2k² + 2k ∈ ℤ.
  4. Hence n² is odd, which is exactly the conclusion. Since n was arbitrary, the universal statement holds.

Answer: Option (A) — assume n odd, derive n² odd..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) starts from the conclusion and derives the hypothesis; that is the converse, and it proves a different statement.
  • (C) is a proof by contrapositive of the statement, not a direct proof — it is valid but it is not what “direct” means.
  • (D) checks only finitely many cases; no number of examples proves a statement about all integers.

Common misconception. Students often assume the conclusion and work backwards. That is legitimate scratch work for discovering a proof, but the written direct proof must run from hypothesis to conclusion, never the other way.

Faster method. In the exam, match the shape: “direct” = assume p, reach q. If the option assumes q or ¬q, it is a different technique whatever the algebra looks like.

Takeaway. Identify the technique by its opening assumption before you read the algebra.

DM-LPT2-M-02MCQeasy

The contrapositive of “If `a + b ≥ 15`, then `a ≥ 8` or `b ≥ 8`” (for real `a, b`) is

  1. If `a < 8` and `b < 8`, then `a + b < 15`.
  2. If `a ≥ 8` or `b ≥ 8`, then `a + b ≥ 15`.
  3. If `a < 8` or `b < 8`, then `a + b < 15`.
  4. If `a + b < 15`, then `a < 8` and `b < 8`.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The contrapositive of `p → q` is `¬q → ¬p`, and it is logically equivalent to the original. Negating a disjunction needs De Morgan: ¬(a ≥ 8 ∨ b ≥ 8) ≡ (a < 8 ∧ b < 8).

Step by step.

  1. Set p: a + b ≥ 15, q: a ≥ 8 ∨ b ≥ 8.
  2. Negate the conclusion: ¬q ≡ ¬(a ≥ 8) ∧ ¬(b ≥ 8) ≡ (a < 8) ∧ (b < 8).
  3. Negate the hypothesis: ¬p ≡ a + b < 15.
  4. So the contrapositive is: if a < 8 and b < 8, then a + b < 15.

Answer: Option (A)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the converse (q → p), not the contrapositive.
  • (C) negates the disjunction wrongly as a disjunction — the classic De Morgan slip.
  • (D) is the inverse (¬p → ¬q). It happens to be equivalent to (B), not to the original.

Common misconception. Most errors here are not about logic but about De Morgan: negating “or” gives “and”, and the inequality flips strictly.

Faster method. Swap the two sides and negate both: p → q becomes ¬q → ¬p. Never reorder without negating.

Takeaway. Contrapositive = swap + negate. Converse = swap only. Inverse = negate only.

DM-LPT2-M-03MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements is most naturally proved by contradiction rather than by a direct proof or a contrapositive proof?

  1. If `n` is an even integer, then `n²` is even.
  2. There are infinitely many prime numbers.
  3. If `3n + 2` is odd, then `n` is odd.
  4. The sum of two odd integers is even.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Contrapositive proof needs the claim to be a conditional `p → q`. A claim about infinitude has no hypothesis to assume, so the natural approach is to assume its negation and derive an impossibility.

Step by step.

  1. Options (A), (C) and (D) are all conditionals or have an obvious direct route: (A) and (D) unfold definitions, (C) is a two-line contrapositive (n even ⇒ 3n+2 even).
  2. Option (B) is not a conditional. Suppose, for contradiction, that there are only finitely many primes p₁, p₂, …, pₖ.
  3. Form N = p₁p₂…pₖ + 1. N > 1, so it has a prime divisor q, and q must be one of the pᵢ.
  4. But then q divides N and q divides p₁p₂…pₖ, so q divides N − p₁p₂…pₖ = 1, impossible.
  5. Hence the assumption of finiteness fails and there are infinitely many primes.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (D) are immediate from the definitions of even and odd; a direct algebraic proof is shorter.
  • (C) is proved in one line by contrapositive: if n is even, 3n + 2 is even.

Common misconception. “Contradiction” is often chosen for any hard-looking statement. It is the right tool when the conclusion is not an implication, or when the negation gives you a concrete object to work with (a largest prime, a rational √2, a smallest counterexample).

Faster method. Ask: is the claim a conditional? If yes, contrapositive is usually cleaner. If the conclusion is an infinitude, a uniqueness or an irrationality, assume the negation.

Takeaway. Contradiction is not a general-purpose tool; it is the tool for conclusions that are hard to reach positively.

DM-LPT2-M-06MCQeasy

Consider the argument: “If it rained last night, the ground is wet. The ground is wet. Therefore it rained last night.” The argument is

  1. valid, by modus ponens.
  2. invalid; it commits the fallacy of affirming the consequent.
  3. valid, by modus tollens.
  4. invalid; it commits the fallacy of denying the antecedent.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Modus ponens has the form `p → q, p ∴ q`. Modus tollens has `p → q, ¬q ∴ ¬p`. Affirming the consequent uses `p → q, q ∴ p`, which is not valid.

Step by step.

  1. Formalise: p = it rained last night, q = the ground is wet. The premises are p → q and q; the conclusion drawn is p.
  2. The schema `p → q, q ∴ p` fails the truth-table test: with q true and p false, both premises hold but the conclusion fails.
  3. A concrete model: the sprinkler ran, so the ground is wet although it did not rain.
  4. Hence the argument is invalid, and the specific error is affirming the consequent.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) modus ponens would need the second premise to be p, not q.
  • (C) modus tollens would need the second premise to be ¬q and the conclusion ¬p.
  • (D) denying the antecedent would have the premises p → q and ¬p with conclusion ¬q.

Common misconception. A valid-looking chain is often accepted because the conclusion happens to be plausible in the real world. Validity is about form, not about whether the conclusion is in fact true.

Faster method. Two valid patterns only: affirm the antecedent, or deny the consequent. Anything else is a fallacy.

Takeaway. Modus ponens and modus tollens are the only two valid implication-elimination patterns.

DM-LPT2-M-07MCQmedium

To prove that the square of every integer leaves remainder 0 or 1 on division by 4, the standard proof by cases uses the case split

  1. `n` even and `n` odd.
  2. `n > 0` and `n < 0`.
  3. `n ≡ 0, 1, 2 (mod 3)`.
  4. `n` prime and `n` composite.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A proof by cases must partition the whole domain: the cases must be exhaustive, and each case must be handled. The modulus of interest is 4, and parity is exactly what controls `n² mod 4`.

Step by step.

  1. Case 1: n = 2k. Then n² = 4k², so n² ≡ 0 (mod 4).
  2. Case 2: n = 2k + 1. Then n² = 4k² + 4k + 1 = 4(k² + k) + 1, so n² ≡ 1 (mod 4).
  3. Every integer is even or odd, so the cases are exhaustive and the claim follows.

Answer: Option (A)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) omits n = 0, so the split is not exhaustive.
  • (C) is exhaustive over the integers, but residues mod 3 do not separate the values of n² mod 4 — within one class you still get both remainders, so the case analysis would not terminate.
  • (D) omits n = 0, n = 1 and all negative composites.

Common misconception. Students pick a split that merely looks natural. The split must be (i) exhaustive and (ii) fine enough that each case forces a single outcome.

Faster method. Residues mod 4 give the answer immediately: 0² ≡ 0, 1² ≡ 1, 2² ≡ 0, 3² ≡ 1. Parity is the same partition and gives the cleaner write-up.

Takeaway. Two case splits are exhaustive over ℤ: even/odd, and n < 0 / n = 0 / n > 0.

DM-LPT2-M-09MCQmedium

The statement “If 7 is a prime, then 3 + 4 = 7” is proved by observing that 3 + 4 = 7 is true, without using the hypothesis at all. Such a proof is called

  1. a vacuous proof.
  2. a trivial proof.
  3. a proof by contrapositive.
  4. an invalid proof, because the hypothesis was not used.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For `p → q`: if `q` is proved true on its own, the implication holds regardless of `p`, and the proof is called trivial. If `p` is proved false, the implication holds vacuously, and the proof is called vacuous.

Step by step.

  1. The claim is p → q with p: 7 is prime and q: 3 + 4 = 7.
  2. Since 3 + 4 = 7 is true, q is true. In the truth table, p → q is true in every row where q is true.
  3. So the implication holds, and the hypothesis p was never needed.
  4. A proof that uses the falsity of the hypothesis is vacuous; a proof that uses the truth of the conclusion is trivial. This is the latter.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) a vacuous proof would need the hypothesis “7 is prime” to be false, which it is not.
  • (C) a contrapositive proof would argue from ¬q to ¬p.
  • (D) the implication is genuinely true; not using the hypothesis is allowed, not an error.

Common misconception. Students believe a proof must use every hypothesis. That is a stylistic habit, not a logical requirement — an implication with a true conclusion is true whatever the hypothesis says.

Faster method. Vacuity: hypothesis false. Triviality: conclusion true. Both give a valid proof of p → q.

Takeaway. Do not confuse the two names; GATE has tested exactly this pair.

DM-LPT2-M-10MCQmedium

To prove that there is a unique real number `x` with `3x − 7 = 5`, a complete proof must

  1. exhibit `x = 4` and stop, because a linear equation has at most one root.
  2. exhibit `x = 4`, then suppose some `y` also satisfies `3y − 7 = 5` and derive `y = 4`.
  3. suppose two distinct numbers both satisfy the equation and derive `0 = 0`.
  4. show only that no two distinct numbers satisfy the equation.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. ∃!x P(x) means ∃x P(x) ∧ ∀y∀z ((P(y) ∧ P(z)) → y = z). Both halves — existence and uniqueness — must be established.

Step by step.

  1. Existence: x = 4 gives 3(4) − 7 = 5, so a solution exists.
  2. Uniqueness: let y be any real with 3y − 7 = 5. Then 3y = 12, so y = 4.
  3. Therefore every solution equals the one exhibited, so the solution is unique.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) asserts uniqueness from the shape of the equation instead of proving it.
  • (C) deriving 0 = 0 is not a contradiction, so nothing is concluded — and it does not establish existence either.
  • (D) proves at most one solution but not that one exists.

Common misconception. “There is at most one” is not the same as “there is exactly one”. Uniqueness proofs need both halves, and the exam options are designed to give you only one.

Faster method. Template: “Existence: take x = …, check. Uniqueness: suppose P(a) and P(b); then … hence a = b.”

Takeaway. Existence + at-most-one = exactly one. Missing either half makes the proof incomplete.

DM-LPT2-M-11MCQmedium

Which one of the following does NOT establish the implication `p → q`?

  1. Assume `p` and derive `q`.
  2. Assume `¬q` and derive `¬p`.
  3. Assume `p` and `¬q` and derive a contradiction.
  4. Assume `q` and derive `p`.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three legitimate routes to `p → q` are the direct proof, the contrapositive, and the contradiction proof. `q → p` is the converse and is logically independent of `p → q`.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the direct proof: assume p, derive q. Valid.
  2. (B) is the contrapositive: ¬q → ¬p ≡ p → q. Valid.
  3. (C) is contradiction: p ∧ ¬q is exactly the negation of p → q, so a contradiction refutes it. Valid.
  4. (D) establishes q → p, the converse. A single model with q true and p false makes q → p... precisely, take p false and q true: then q → p is false while p → q is true. The two are not interchangeable.

Answer: Option (D)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) are the standard direct, contrapositive and contradiction templates and are all sound.

Common misconception. Proving the converse is the single most frequent structural error in student proofs, and it is exactly what (D) describes.

Faster method. Only three openings are allowed for p → q: assume p; assume ¬q; assume p and ¬q. Anything else is a different theorem.

Takeaway. Check the opening assumption of a proof before checking its algebra.

DM-LPT2-M-12MCQmedium

Which statement is logically equivalent to “If `n` is an integer and `n²` is odd, then `n` is odd”?

  1. If `n` is odd, then `n²` is odd.
  2. If `n` is even, then `n²` is even.
  3. If `n²` is even, then `n` is odd.
  4. If `n` is odd, then `n²` is even.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. `p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p`. Negate both sides and swap.

Step by step.

  1. p: n is an integer and n² is odd. q: n is odd.
  2. ¬q: n is even. ¬p: n is not an integer, or n² is even.
  3. So the contrapositive is: if n is even, then n² is even (with n an integer understood throughout the statement).
  4. That is option (B).

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the converse of the related “n odd ⇒ n² odd” claim, not the contrapositive of the given one.
  • (C) has the hypothesis of the contrapositive but keeps the conclusion unchanged.
  • (D) is a false statement, and it is neither the converse nor the contrapositive.

Common misconception. Swapping the two parts without negating gives the converse, which is a different statement. The contrapositive must negate as well.

Faster method. Write the contrapositive mechanically: “not q” then “not p”. Do not re-read the English for meaning until the swap is done.

Takeaway. The contrapositive is the only reordering of an implication that preserves truth value.

DM-LPT2-M-13MCQmedium

A proof by cases of a claim `∀x P(x)` over a domain `D` is invalid if

  1. the cases overlap, i.e. some element of `D` belongs to two cases.
  2. the cases do not cover every element of `D`.
  3. the same conclusion is reached in each case.
  4. more than two cases are used.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A case analysis is sound when the cases are exhaustive (their union is the whole domain); overlap is harmless, because an element handled twice is still handled at least once.

Step by step.

  1. If some d ∈ D lies in no case, the proof never addresses P(d), so the universal claim is unproved.
  2. Overlap causes duplication, not a gap — it does not invalidate the argument.
  3. Reaching the same conclusion in each case is the goal, not a defect.
  4. The number of cases is irrelevant to correctness.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) overlap is allowed; proofs by cases routinely overlap for convenience.
  • (C) is the required outcome.
  • (D) any finite number of cases is acceptable.

Common misconception. “The cases must be disjoint” is a very common over-correction. Disjointness is a tidiness preference; exhaustiveness is the logical requirement.

Faster method. Check exhaustiveness first: does every element of the domain fall into at least one case?

Takeaway. Exhaustive, not disjoint. That is the only hard requirement of a proof by cases.

DM-LPT2-M-14MCQmedium

In a proof by contradiction of “For every integer `n`, if `3n + 2` is odd then `n` is odd”, the assumption made at the very start is

  1. `3n + 2` is odd and `n` is even.
  2. `3n + 2` is even and `n` is even.
  3. `3n + 2` is odd and `n` is odd.
  4. `n` is even, only.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A contradiction proof of `p → q` assumes `p ∧ ¬q` — the negation of the implication — and derives a contradiction. This is not the same as assuming only `¬q` (that is the contrapositive route).

Step by step.

  1. The implication is p → q with p: 3n + 2 is odd, q: n is odd.
  2. ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q, i.e. 3n + 2 is odd AND n is even.
  3. So the opening assumption is both statements together. Assuming only ¬q is the contrapositive method.
  4. From n even, n = 2k, so 3n + 2 = 6k + 2 = 2(3k + 1) is even — contradicting p.

Answer: Option (A)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) negates the hypothesis as well, so it assumes more than the negation of the statement.
  • (C) assumes the statement rather than its negation.
  • (D) is the contrapositive assumption; valid as a technique, but it is not the contradiction assumption.

Common misconception. “Contradiction” and “contrapositive” are often treated as the same proof. They are not: the contradiction proof carries both p and ¬q; the contrapositive proof carries only ¬q.

Faster method. Negate the whole statement, quantifiers included: ¬∀n(p → q) ≡ ∃n(p ∧ ¬q).

Takeaway. The negation of p → q is p ∧ ¬q, not ¬q alone.

DM-LPT2-M-15MCQhard

A “proof” that `1 = 2` runs as follows. Let `a = b`. Then `a² = ab`, so `a² − b² = ab − b²`, i.e. `(a − b)(a + b) = b(a − b)`. Cancelling `(a − b)` gives `a + b = b`, and since `a = b`, `2b = b`, hence `2 = 1`. The flaw is

  1. `a² = ab` does not follow from `a = b`.
  2. `a² − b² = ab − b²` is algebraically incorrect.
  3. the cancellation divides both sides by `a − b`, which equals 0.
  4. `2b = b` does not imply `2 = 1`.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For real numbers, `ac = bc` implies `a = b` only when `c ≠ 0`. Division by a quantity that may be zero is the standard hidden error in this family of false proofs.

Step by step.

  1. From a = b we get a − b = 0.
  2. The step (a−b)(a+b) = b(a−b) → (a+b) = b divides both sides by (a − b), i.e. by 0.
  3. Division by zero is undefined, so the inference is invalid; the equation (a−b)(a+b) = b(a−b) is simply 0 = 0, which carries no information.
  4. Everything before the cancellation is valid, and everything after it is unsupported. The flaw is precisely the cancellation.

Answer: Option (C)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: multiplying a = b by a on both sides gives a² = ab legitimately.
  • (B) is false: subtracting b² from the equal quantities a² and ab preserves equality.
  • (D) would be a second error, but it is not reached, because the derivation has already failed.

Common misconception. Students hunt for the error in the algebra and miss the division. Any proof that cancels a symbolic factor must justify that the factor is non-zero — this is also the standard trap in inequalities and in limits.

Faster method. Scan for division by a term that could vanish. Here a − b = 0 by the very first line.

Takeaway. Before cancelling, prove the factor is non-zero. Most “1 = 0” style false proofs hide the error exactly there.

DM-LPT2-S-16MSQeasy

Which of the following are valid ways to prove `p → q`? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Assume `p` and derive `q`.
  2. Assume `¬q` and derive `¬p`.
  3. Assume `p` and `¬q` and derive a contradiction.
  4. Assume `q` and derive `p`.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The implication `p → q` is equivalent to its contrapositive `¬q → ¬p`, and its negation is `p ∧ ¬q`. These give three sound proof templates; the converse `q → p` is a different statement.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Direct proof: p → q by definition. Sound.
  2. (B) Contrapositive: ¬q → ¬p ≡ p → q. Sound.
  3. (C) Contradiction: if p ∧ ¬q is impossible then ¬(p ∧ ¬q) holds, which is exactly p → q. Sound.
  4. (D) q → p is the converse. Counter-model: p false, q true makes q → p false while p → q is true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are correct; (D) is not..

Common misconception. “Any proof that mentions both p and q must be a proof of the implication” — no. The direction of the deduction is what matters.

Takeaway. Three openings, three names: direct, contrapositive, contradiction.

DM-LPT2-S-18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements are FALSE over the domain stated? (Select all that apply.)

  1. “Every prime number is odd.” (over the primes)
  2. “For every integer `n`, if `4 | n²` then `n` is even.” (over ℤ)
  3. “For every real number `x`, `x² ≥ x`.” (over ℝ)
  4. “For every odd integer `n`, `8 | (n² − 1)`.” (over ℤ)
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A universal claim is false exactly when a counterexample exists. Each option is settled either by one counterexample or by a short algebraic proof.

Step by step.

  1. (A) False: 2 is prime and even. This is the classic single counterexample.
  2. (B) True: 4 | n² means n² = 4k, so n² is even, hence n is even. Equivalently, if n were odd then n² would be odd.
  3. (C) False: take x = 1/2. Then x² = 1/4 < 1/2 = x. The claim fails on the open interval (0, 1).
  4. (D) True: write n = 2k + 1. Then n² − 1 = 4k(k + 1), and k(k + 1) is even, so 8 | (n² − 1).

Answer: Options (A) and (C) are false; (B) and (D) are true..

Common misconception. Both false claims fail on a single small value, and both true claims look “too strong”. Test small cases first — especially 0, 1, 2 and a fraction in (0,1).

Faster method. For “for all x” claims over ℝ, always test x = 1/2 and x = 0 before believing the claim.

Takeaway. 2 is the counterexample to every “primes are odd” style claim; the interval (0,1) refutes every claim of the form x² ≥ x.

DM-LPT2-S-19MSQhard

Which of the following case splits are EXHAUSTIVE over the set of all integers? (Select all that apply.)

  1. `n` is even; `n` is odd.
  2. `n ≡ 0 (mod 4)`; `n ≡ 2 (mod 4)`.
  3. `n < 0`; `n = 0`; `n > 0`.
  4. `n > 0`; `n < 0`.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A split is exhaustive when every element of the domain lies in at least one case. Cases need not be disjoint; they must cover.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Every integer is even or odd — exhaustive.
  2. (B) Covers only the even integers; every odd integer is missed. Not exhaustive.
  3. (C) The trichotomy of the order on ℤ: every integer is negative, zero, or positive — exhaustive.
  4. (D) Misses 0. Not exhaustive.

Answer: Options (A) and (C) are exhaustive..

Common misconception. Splits that “look complete” are accepted without checking the edge value 0, or without checking the odd case. Exhaustiveness must be verified, not assumed.

Faster method. Test n = 0, n = 1, n = 2, n = −1 against the cases. If any escapes, the split fails.

Takeaway. Trichotomy (n < 0, n = 0, n > 0) and parity are the two universally safe splits over ℤ.

DM-LPT2-S-20MSQhard

Treating `⊢` as “logically entails”, which of the following argument forms are VALID? (Select all that apply.)

  1. `p → q, q ⊢ p`
  2. `p → q, ¬q ⊢ ¬p`
  3. `p → q, ¬p ⊢ ¬q`
  4. `p ∨ q, ¬p ⊢ q`
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An argument form is valid when there is no truth assignment making all premises true and the conclusion false. Test each form on the two-variable truth table.

Step by step.

  1. (A) p → q, q ⊢ p: take p = F, q = T. Premises T, T; conclusion F. Invalid (affirming the consequent).
  2. (B) p → q, ¬q ⊢ ¬p: this is modus tollens, the contrapositive of the premise. Valid.
  3. (C) p → q, ¬p ⊢ ¬q: take p = F, q = T. Premises T, T; conclusion F. Invalid (denying the antecedent).
  4. (D) p ∨ q, ¬p ⊢ q: disjunctive syllogism. If p ∨ q holds and p is false, q must hold. Valid.

Answer: Options (B) and (D) are valid..

Common misconception. Options (A) and (C) feel symmetric to the valid ones and are frequently accepted. Only modus ponens, modus tollens and disjunctive syllogism are sound among these patterns.

Faster method. Look for the one assignment that breaks the form: for implication patterns try p = F, q = T.

Takeaway. Two fallacies to memorise by name: affirming the consequent (A) and denying the antecedent (C).

DM-LPT2-N-23NAThard

How many of the following four argument forms are VALID? (i) `p → q, q ⊢ p` (ii) `p → q, ¬q ⊢ ¬p` (iii) `p ∨ q, ¬p ⊢ q` (iv) `p → q, ¬p ⊢ ¬q`

Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An argument form is valid if no truth assignment makes all premises true and the conclusion false. With two propositional variables there are only four assignments to check.

Step by step.

  1. (i) p → q, q ⊢ p. Assignment p = F, q = T: both premises true, conclusion false. INVALID (affirming the consequent).
  2. (ii) p → q, ¬q ⊢ ¬p. Modus tollens. If ¬q is true then q is false, so p cannot be true (else q would be true). VALID.
  3. (iii) p ∨ q, ¬p ⊢ q. Disjunctive syllogism: p is false and p ∨ q holds, so q holds. VALID.
  4. (iv) p → q, ¬p ⊢ ¬q. Assignment p = F, q = T: both premises true, conclusion false. INVALID (denying the antecedent).
  5. Valid forms: (ii) and (iii) — two in all.

Answer: 2..

Common misconception. Forms (i) and (iv) are accepted because they “look like” the valid ones. The single assignment p = F, q = T refutes both.

Faster method. For any implication-based form, try p = F, q = T first. It kills the converse and the inverse in one step.

Takeaway. Two variables, four rows, five seconds — always verify rather than pattern-match.

DM-LPT3-M-01MCQeasy

A proof of the statement for every real number x, if x³ + x + 1 = 0 then x < 0 begins like this: “Suppose x ≥ 0. Then x³ ≥ 0 and x + 1 ≥ 1, so x³ + x + 1 ≥ 1 > 0, which contradicts x³ + x + 1 = 0.” The technique being used is

  1. a direct proof.
  2. a proof by contrapositive.
  3. a proof by cases.
  4. a disproof by counterexample.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The contrapositive of p → q is ¬q → ¬p, and the two are logically equivalent. A contrapositive proof assumes only the negation of the conclusion and derives the negation of the hypothesis.

Step by step.

  1. Here p is “x³ + x + 1 = 0” and q is “x < 0”.
  2. The proof assumes ¬q, namely x ≥ 0.
  3. From x ≥ 0 it derives x³ + x + 1 ≥ 1 > 0, i.e. x³ + x + 1 ≠ 0, which is ¬p.
  4. Only ¬q was assumed and only ¬p was produced, so this is exactly ¬q → ¬p, the contrapositive.

Answer: Option (B) — a proof by contrapositive..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A direct proof would assume x³ + x + 1 = 0 and try to reach x < 0.
  • (C) No partition of the domain into cases is used; there is a single assumption.
  • (D) A counterexample would be one specific x satisfying the equation with x ≥ 0, and the argument never produces such an x — it argues for all x.

Common misconception. Any proof that opens with “suppose not…” is often labelled a proof by contradiction. The distinguishing test is what is assumed: only ¬q means contrapositive; both p and ¬q means contradiction.

Faster method. Look at the first line. One assumption that is the negation of the conclusion → contrapositive. Two assumptions, one of which is the hypothesis → contradiction.

Takeaway. Name the technique from its opening assumption before reading the algebra; GATE questions on “which method is this” are decided in the first line.

DM-LPT3-M-05MCQeasy

A proof by contradiction of the statement “there is no largest prime” begins by assuming that

  1. there are infinitely many primes.
  2. there is a largest prime p, so the primes form a finite list p₁ < p₂ < … < p_k = p.
  3. the number p₁p₂…p_k + 1 is prime.
  4. every integer greater than 1 has a prime divisor.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. To prove a statement S by contradiction, assume ¬S and derive an impossibility. “There is no largest prime” has the form ¬∃p L(p), whose negation is ∃p L(p).

Step by step.

  1. S : there does not exist a prime that is larger than every prime, i.e. ¬∃p (p is prime ∧ p is largest).
  2. ¬S : ∃p (p is prime ∧ p is largest). So the opening assumption is that a largest prime p exists.
  3. Because p is largest, the primes are exactly the finite list p₁ < p₂ < … < p_k = p.
  4. The standard continuation forms N = p₁p₂…p_k + 1, shows N > 1 has a prime divisor q from the list, and q | N and q | p₁…p_k force q | 1 — impossible. Hence no largest prime exists.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the statement to be proved, not its negation.
  • (C) is a claim used (in the form “N has some prime divisor”) inside the proof, not the opening assumption — and in fact N need not be prime.
  • (D) is a true theorem invoked during the argument, not the negated conclusion.

Common misconception. Students confuse “assume the opposite of what I want” with “assume something convenient”. The negation must be written out mechanically, quantifiers included.

Faster method. Write S with explicit quantifiers, then flip them: ¬∃p L(p) becomes ∃p L(p). The assumption writes itself.

Takeaway. Negate the whole statement, not just its conclusion. For a non-conditional claim, contradiction is usually the natural route because there is no hypothesis to assume.

DM-LPT3-M-07MCQmedium

The contrapositive of “if n is an odd integer with n > 1, then 8 divides n² − 1” is

  1. if 8 divides n² − 1 then n is an odd integer with n > 1.
  2. if 8 does not divide n² − 1 then n is even or n ≤ 1.
  3. if n is even or n ≤ 1 then 8 does not divide n² − 1.
  4. if 8 divides n² − 1 then n is even or n ≤ 1.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The contrapositive of p → q is ¬q → ¬p. Negating a conjunction needs De Morgan: ¬(A ∧ B) ≡ ¬A ∨ ¬B.

Step by step.

  1. p : n is odd and n > 1. q : 8 | (n² − 1).
  2. ¬q : 8 does not divide n² − 1.
  3. ¬p : ¬(n odd) ∨ ¬(n > 1) ≡ n is even ∨ n ≤ 1.
  4. So the contrapositive is: if 8 does not divide n² − 1, then n is even or n ≤ 1.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the converse (q → p), which is not equivalent.
  • (C) is the inverse (¬p → ¬q), which is equivalent to the converse, not to the statement.
  • (D) mixes the hypothesis of one form with the conclusion of another and is not equivalent to anything here.

Common misconception. “And” is negated as “and”. Negating the hypothesis of a compound statement is where most marks are lost; De Morgan must be applied to the hypothesis too.

Faster method. Swap and negate. Write ¬q first, then mechanically negate p with De Morgan before worrying about meaning.

Takeaway. Contrapositive = swap + negate both sides. When a hypothesis is a conjunction, its negation is a disjunction.

DM-LPT3-M-11MCQmedium

To prove “for every integer n, if 3 divides n² then 3 divides n” by contrapositive, one shows that if n is not divisible by 3 then n² is not divisible by 3. The case analysis that achieves this is

  1. n ≡ 1 (mod 3) or n ≡ 2 (mod 3); in both cases n² ≡ 1 (mod 3).
  2. n ≡ 1 (mod 3) only, since n ≡ 2 (mod 3) is symmetric and may be omitted.
  3. n ≡ 0, 1, 2 (mod 3), with n² ≡ 0, 1, 4 (mod 3) respectively.
  4. n odd or n even; in both cases n² is odd.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Inside a contrapositive proof the hypothesis is “3 ∤ n”, so the cases must cover exactly the residues allowed by that hypothesis, and each case must force 3 ∤ n².

Step by step.

  1. 3 ∤ n means n leaves remainder 1 or 2 on division by 3, i.e. n ≡ 1 or n ≡ 2 (mod 3).
  2. Case n ≡ 1: n² ≡ 1² = 1 (mod 3).
  3. Case n ≡ 2: n² ≡ 2² = 4 ≡ 1 (mod 3).
  4. In both cases n² ≡ 1 (mod 3), so 3 ∤ n². The contrapositive is proved, hence the original statement holds.

Answer: Option (A)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) drops a case; residues 1 and 2 are not interchangeable without an argument, and none is given.
  • (C) includes n ≡ 0 (mod 3), which contradicts the hypothesis and is therefore not available.
  • (D) splits by parity, which does not separate the residues mod 3 that decide divisibility by 3.

Common misconception. A split is chosen because it is familiar (odd/even) rather than because it is aligned with the modulus in the claim. A case split must be exhaustive over the hypothesis and fine enough to force the conclusion.

Faster method. The modulus in the statement dictates the split: divisibility by 3 → residues mod 3; by 4 → residues mod 4.

Takeaway. Cases must cover the hypothesis exactly — no more (residue 0 is excluded) and no less (residue 2 is required).

DM-LPT3-M-15MCQhard

In the classical proof that √p is irrational for a prime p, where exactly is the primality of p used?

  1. In arranging that the fraction a/b can be taken in lowest terms.
  2. In passing from p | a² to p | a, which is valid when p is prime but fails for composite p — for example 4 | 6² although 4 ∤ 6.
  3. In concluding at the end that a and b are both divisible by p.
  4. Nowhere: the argument works verbatim for every integer p ≥ 2.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. If p | a² then p | a, because p prime forces p to divide one of the two equal factors a·a. This is Euclid's lemma, and it is exactly the step that needs primality.

Step by step.

  1. Assume √p = a/b with integers a, b and gcd(a, b) = 1 (possible for any rational).
  2. Then p b² = a², so p | a².
  3. By Euclid's lemma (p prime), p | a. Write a = pc.
  4. Substituting gives p b² = p²c², so b² = p c², hence p | b² and again p | b. Then p divides both a and b, contradicting gcd(a, b) = 1.
  5. The step p | a² ⇒ p | a is false for composite p: 4 | 36 = 6² but 4 ∤ 6. So primality is used exactly there.

Answer: Option (B)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Reducing to lowest terms is available for every rational, prime or not.
  • (C) The conclusion that p divides both a and b is a consequence of the lemma, not the place where primality is consumed.
  • (D) The claim is false for composite p: √4 = 2 is rational.

Common misconception. The “both a and b are even” version of the √2 proof is remembered as the heart of the argument, so the lemma p | a² ⇒ p | a is treated as automatic.

Faster method. Ask which single inference would break if p were composite. Here it is p | a² ⇒ p | a, and 4 | 36, 4 ∤ 6 breaks it.

Takeaway. For √p irrationality, primality enters through Euclid's lemma. For composite p the statement is simply false.

DM-LPT3-S-19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about proofs of an implication p → q are true? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A contrapositive proof assumes ¬q and derives ¬p.
  2. A contradiction proof assumes p and ¬q and derives a contradiction.
  3. Every contrapositive proof of p → q can be rewritten as a contradiction proof.
  4. Every contradiction proof of p → q can be rewritten as a contrapositive proof.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p ≡ ¬(p ∧ ¬q). The first equivalence is the contrapositive, the second is the contradiction form.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of a contrapositive proof. True.
  2. (B) ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q, so a contradiction proof assumes both and derives ⊥. True.
  3. (C) If ¬q → ¬p has been proved, then assuming p and ¬q yields ¬p, contradicting p. So any contrapositive proof becomes a contradiction proof by adding the hypothesis p to the assumptions. True.
  4. (D) A contradiction proof of p → q may use p in a way that does not reduce to a derivation of ¬p from ¬q alone (for instance when the contradiction is with an external fact such as the irrationality of √2 rather than with p). So the rewrite is not always available. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C)..

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) overstates the equivalence. The two methods are not interchangeable in general, only in one direction.

Common misconception. Because both methods start with “suppose not…”, they are assumed to be the same technique. The difference is what is in the assumption set: ¬q alone versus p and ¬q.

Faster method. Contrapositive is a special case of contradiction (add p to the assumptions and contradict it). The converse inclusion fails.

Takeaway. Contrapositive ⊆ contradiction as techniques. When a proof by contradiction does not end by contradicting p, it is not a contrapositive proof in disguise.

Counterexamples 17
DM-LPT-E-07MCQeasy

Consider the statement: "For every real number x, if x² = 4, then x = 2." Which of the following correctly assesses it?

  1. True; provable directly.
  2. False; x = −2 is a counterexample.
  3. False; x = 4 is a counterexample.
  4. True; provable by contradiction.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A universally quantified implication fails if some x makes the hypothesis true and the conclusion false.

Step by step.

  1. Take x = −2. Then x² = 4 (hypothesis true) but x ≠ 2 (conclusion false).
  2. So the statement is false.

Answer: Option (B)..

Common misconception. x = 4 does not satisfy x² = 4, so it is not a counterexample — students often pick any 'unusual' value without checking the hypothesis.

Takeaway. Always verify a counterexample actually satisfies the hypothesis before using it.

DM-LPT-E-11MCQeasy

Consider the statement: "For every real number x, x² > x." Which of the following is a correct disproof?

  1. x = 0.5
  2. x = 2
  3. x = −1
  4. x = 3
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. x² > x fails exactly when 0 ≤ x ≤ 1.

Step by step.

  1. x² − x > 0 ⇔ x(x−1) > 0 ⇔ x < 0 or x > 1.
  2. So the statement fails for every x in [0,1].
  3. x = 0.5 gives 0.25 > 0.5, which is false — a counterexample.

Answer: Option (A)..

Common misconception. Choosing a value such as x = 2 (which satisfies the inequality) instead of a failing value.

Takeaway. Solve the inequality first; the complement of its solution set is exactly where counterexamples live.

DM-LPT-E-14MCQhard

A student wishes to disprove the claim "Every odd integer greater than or equal to 5 can be written as a sum of three primes." The logically correct strategy is to:

  1. exhibit one odd integer ≥ 5 that cannot be written as a sum of three primes.
  2. prove the claim for all odd integers ≥ 5.
  3. exhibit an odd integer less than 5 that is not a sum of three primes.
  4. show that 3 + 5 + 7 = 15.
Show answer & detailed solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A claim of the form ∀n≥5 (odd(n) → S(n)) is refuted by one qualifying counterexample inside the stated range.

Step by step.

  1. The claim quantifies over odd integers ≥ 5.
  2. Disproof requires one odd integer ≥ 5 violating S(n).
  3. A value below 5 is outside the domain and cannot refute the claim.

Answer: Option (A)..

Common misconception. Using an out-of-domain value (e.g. n = 1 or 3) as a 'counterexample'. It must satisfy every hypothesis of the claim.

Takeaway. A counterexample must lie inside the quantified domain AND satisfy the hypothesis.

DM-LPT-E-22NATmedium
